This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to notes written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files:* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying:* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the process of converting images of typed or handwritten text into machine-encoded text</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution:* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal:* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface of the work, establishing the central metaphor between the biological body and the political state.</meta>

<image-desc>A wide rectangular woodcut headpiece consisting of a symmetrical, ornate pattern of interlacing scrolling vines, stylized foliage, and floral elements.</image-desc>

## THE
# PREFACE.

Laws and government are to the political bodies of civil societies what the vital spirits <note>original: "Vital Spirits"; in early medicine, these were thought to be substances that moved through the body to sustain life and function.</note> and life itself are to the natural bodies of living creatures. Those who study the anatomy of dead carcasses may see that the chief organs and the most delicate springs required to keep our "machine" <note>The author refers to the human body as a mechanical system, a common view in 17th and 18th-century philosophy.</note> in motion are not the hard bones, strong muscles, and nerves, nor the smooth white skin that so beautifully covers them. Instead, they are small, seemingly trivial membranes and little pipes that are either overlooked or seem insignificant to common eyes. 

In the same way, those who examine the nature of man—separating what is natural from what is learned through art and education—may observe that what truly makes him a sociable animal does not consist of his desire for company, his good nature, pity, politeness, or other graces of a pleasant exterior. Rather, his most "vile" and hateful qualities are actually the most necessary accomplishments to fit him for the largest and—according to the world—the happiest and most flourishing societies.

<summary>The author argues that just as microscopic biological parts sustain the human body, man's "vile" or selfish qualities, rather than his virtues, are the hidden foundations that allow large, prosperous societies to function.</summary>

<keywords>political philosophy, social contract, human nature, anatomy metaphor, civil society, vice vs virtue</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from the philosophical justification of the work to a brief history of the specific poem, "The Grumbling Hive," and the author's reasons for republication.</meta>

# The Preface.

The following fable, in which what I have said is described at length, was printed about fifteen years ago <note>This was written in 1714.</note> in a six-penny pamphlet called *The Grumbling Hive; or, Knaves Turned Honest*. Soon after, it was pirated and sold about the streets in a half-penny sheet. Since its first publication, I have encountered several people who, either willfully or ignorantly misunderstanding the purpose, argued that its intent was a satire upon virtue and morality, and that the whole thing was written to encourage vice. This made me resolve that, whenever it was reprinted, I would find some way to inform the reader of the real intent with which this little poem was written.

I do not dignify these few loose lines with the name of *poem* because I want the reader to expect any actual poetry in them, but simply because they rhyme; in reality, I am puzzled as to what name to give them. They are neither heroic nor pastoral, neither satire, burlesque, nor heroi-comic. As a tale, they lack probability, and the whole work is rather too long for a fable. All I can say of them is that they are a story told in doggerel, which I have endeavored to do in as easy and familiar a manner as possible, without the least intention of being witty. The reader is welcome to call them whatever he pleases. It was said of *Montaigne* that he was quite well-versed in the defects of mankind but unacquainted with the excellencies of human nature. If I am treated no worse than that, I shall consider myself well-treated.

---

<summary>The author explains the publishing history of "The Grumbling Hive" and defends the work against accusations that it promotes vice, clarifying that his simple "doggerel" verses are intended to illustrate his points about human nature.</summary>

<keywords>The Grumbling Hive, publishing history, piracy, satire, morality, vice, doggerel, Montaigne, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface, moving from the history of the poem to a clarification of its allegorical meaning and its defense of the complex nature of a prosperous society.</meta>

# The Preface.

<sig>A 3</sig>

<meta>catchword: were</meta>

<warning>The bottom portion of the page has significant ink smearing and bleed-through, though the text remains mostly legible.</warning>

Whatever country in the universe is meant by the *Bee-Hive* represented here, it is evident from what is said of its laws and constitution, and the glory, wealth, power, and industry of its inhabitants, that it must be a large, rich, and warlike nation that is happily governed by a limited monarchy. The satire, therefore, to be found in the following lines concerning the various professions and callings, and almost every rank and status of people, was not intended to harm or point to specific individuals, but only to show the vileness of the ingredients that together compose the wholesome mixture of a well-ordered society. This is done in order to praise the wonderful power of political wisdom, with the help of which so beautiful a machine is raised from the most contemptible branches. For the main design of the fable (as it is briefly explained in the *Moral*) is to show the impossibility of enjoying all the most elegant comforts of life found in an industrious, wealthy, and powerful nation, and at the same time being blessed with all the virtue and innocence that one could hope for in a Golden Age. It aims, therefore, to expose the unreasonableness and folly of those who, desiring to be an opulent and flourishing people and being wonderfully greedy for all the benefits they can receive as such, yet are always murmuring against and complaining about those vices and inconveniences which, from the beginning of the world to this present day, have been inseparable from all kingdoms and states that have ever

<summary>The author explains that his "Bee-Hive" represents a prosperous, limited monarchy and clarifies that his satire targets social structures rather than individuals. He argues that the vices people complain about are actually necessary components of a wealthy and powerful nation.</summary>

<keywords>Bee-Hive, monarchy, satire, political wisdom, Fable of the Bees, Golden Age, social complexity, vice vs. prosperity</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface, completing the argument that historical greatness and national vice have always coexisted, and outlining the author’s goal to show how individual failings contribute to public prosperity.</meta>

were famed for strength, riches, and refinement at the same time.

To do this, I first slightly touch upon some of the faults and corruptions with which the various professions and callings are generally charged. After that, I show that those very vices of every individual person, by skillful management, were made subservient to the grandeur and worldly happiness of the whole. Lastly, by setting forth what must necessarily be the consequence of general honesty and virtue, and national temperance, innocence, and contentment, I demonstrate that if mankind could be cured of the failings they are naturally guilty of, they would cease to be capable of being raised into such vast, powerful, and refined societies as they have been under the various great commonwealths and monarchies that have flourished since the Creation.

If <unclear>you</unclear> ask me why I have done all this, *cui bono?* and what good these notions will produce? Truly, besides the reader’s entertainment, I believe none at all; but if I were asked what naturally ought to be expected from them, I would answer: that in the first place, the people who continually find fault with others, by reading them, would be taught to look at home, and by examining their own consciences, be made ashamed of always railing at what they are more or less guilty of themselves; and that in the next, those who are so fond of the ease and comforts, and reap all the benefits that are the consequence of a great and flourishing nation, would

<summary>The author explains how individual vices are managed to create national prosperity and argues that a perfectly virtuous society could never achieve the scale or power of a great monarchy. He suggests his work aims to show hypocritical critics their own faults and help people understand the trade-offs required for a refined, wealthy nation.</summary>

<keywords>vices, national prosperity, social management, virtue vs. wealth, hypocrisy, commonwealths, monarchies, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface, completing the argument that national prosperity depends on private vices, and begins addressing potential criticisms of his work.</meta>

learn more patiently to submit to those inconveniences which no government on Earth can remedy, once they see the impossibility of enjoying any great share of the first [benefits] without also sharing in the latter.

This, I say, ought naturally to be expected from publishing these notions, if people could be made better by anything that could be said to them; but since mankind has remained the same for so many ages, despite the many instructive and elaborate writings that have attempted their improvement, I am not so vain as to hope for better success from such an insignificant trifle.

Having admitted the small advantage this little whim is likely to produce, I think myself obliged to show that it cannot be harmful to anyone; for what is published, if it does no good, ought at least to do no harm. To this end, I have provided some explanatory notes, to which the reader will find himself referred in those passages that seem most likely to face objections.

The censorious critics who never saw *The Grumbling Hive* will tell me that, regardless of what I say about the fable, since it takes up less than a tenth of the book, it was only devised to introduce the remarks; that instead of clearing up doubtful or obscure passages, I have only chosen those that I wanted to expand upon; and that far from striving to lessen the errors committed, I have made bad worse and shown myself to be a blatant champion for vice, to the

<summary>Mandeville admits his work is unlikely to change human nature but argues it is harmless. He anticipates that critics will accuse him of using his poem merely as a pretext to promote vice through his extensive commentary.</summary>
<keywords>social reform, human nature, The Grumbling Hive, explanatory notes, criticism of vice, national prosperity, Bernard Mandeville</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's response to critics who might accuse him of promoting vice in his commentary on the fable.</meta>

# The Preface

rambling digressions than I had done in the fable itself.

I shall spend no time in answering these accusations; where men are prejudiced, the best apologies are lost; and I know that those who think it criminal to suppose a necessity of vice in any case whatever will never be reconciled to any part of the performance; but if this is thoroughly examined, all the offense it can give must result from the wrong inferences that may perhaps be drawn from it, and which I desire nobody to make. When I assert that vices are inseparable from great and potent societies, and that it is impossible their wealth and grandeur should subsist without them, I do not say that the particular members of them who are guilty of any should not be continually reproved, or not be punished for them when they grow into crimes.

There are, I believe, few people in London, of those that are at any time forced to go on foot but what could wish the streets of it much cleaner than generally they are, while they regard nothing but their own clothes and private convenience: but, when once they come to consider that what offends them is the result of the plenty, great traffic and opulence of that mighty city, if they have any concern in its welfare, they will hardly ever wish to see the streets of it less dirty. For if we mind the materials of all sorts that must supply such an infinite number of trades and handicrafts as are always going forward, the vast quantity of vic-

<meta>catchword: tuals,</meta>

He uses the metaphor of London's dirty streets to illustrate how certain inconveniences are the inevitable byproduct of a city's prosperity.</summary>
<keywords>vice, social necessity, wealth and grandeur, London, public welfare, trade and industry, moral philosophy</keywords>

# The Preface

victuals, drink, and fuel that are daily consumed in it, the waste and superfluities that must be produced from them; the multitudes of horses and other cattle that are always daubing the streets, the carts, coaches, and heavier carriages that are perpetually wearing and breaking the pavement of them, and above all the countless swarms of people that are continually harassing and trampling through every part of them: If, I say, we consider all these, we shall find that every moment must produce new filth; and considering how far distant the great streets are from the riverside, no matter what cost and care is bestowed to remove the nastiness almost as fast as it is made, it is impossible for *London* to be cleaner until it is less flourishing. Now I would ask if a good citizen, in consideration of what has been said, might not assert that dirty streets are a necessary evil inseparable from the prosperity of *London*, without being the least hindrance to the cleaning of shoes or the sweeping of streets, and consequently without any detriment either to the *Blackguard* <note>A term for the lower class of street laborers, such as shoe-shiners or link-boys.</note> or the *Scavengers* <note>Laborers or officials appointed to clean the streets and remove refuse.</note>.

But if, without <unclear>any</unclear> regard to the interest or happiness of the city, the question was put, <unclear>What</unclear> place I thought most pleasant to walk in? Nobody can doubt but that, rather than the stinking streets of *London*, I would value a fragrant garden or a <unclear>shady</unclear> grove in the country. In the same man<unclear>ner</unclear>, if laying aside all worldly greatness and <unclear>vain</unclear> glory, I should be asked where I thought <unclear>it</unclear> most probable that men might enjoy true
->Happiness<-

<meta>This page continues the author's preface, transitioning from an analogy about the cleanliness of London to a philosophical preference for modest societies over decadent empires. It then addresses the public and legal backlash following the book's second edition.</meta>

# The Preface.

<sig>A 3</sig>

happiness, I would prefer a small, peaceable society in which men, neither envied nor esteemed by their neighbors, should be contented to live upon the natural products of the spot they inhabit, rather than a vast multitude abounding in wealth and power that would always be conquering others by their *arms abroad* and debauching themselves by *foreign luxury* at *home*.

I had said this much to the reader in the *first edition*, and I have added nothing by way of a preface in the *second*. But since then, a violent outcry has been made against the book, exactly matching the expectation I always had regarding the justice, wisdom, charity, and fair dealing of those whose goodwill I despaired of obtaining. It has been presented by the *grand jury* and condemned by thousands who never saw a word of it. It has been preached against before my *Lord Mayor*, and a complete refutation of it is daily expected from a *reverend divine*, who has called me names in the *advertisements* and threatened to answer me in two months' time for over five months together. What I have to say for myself, the reader will see in my *vindication* at the end of the book, where he will likewise find the *grand jury’s presentment* and a letter to the *Right Honourable Lord C.*, which is very rhetorical but lacks argument or connection. The author shows a fine talent for *invectives* and great sagacity in discovering *atheism* where others can find none. He is zealous against wicked books and points at the *Fable*;

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the harsh reception of his work, specifically responding to a critic who attacked "The Fable of the Bees" with extreme rhetoric and calls for censorship or divine punishment.</meta>

# The Preface.

of the *Bees*, and is very angry with the *Author*: he bestows four strong *Epithets* on the enormity of his guilt, and by several elegant *Innuendos* to the *Multitude*—such as the danger there is in allowing such authors to live and the *Vengeance* of *Heaven* upon a whole nation—very charitably recommends him to their care.

Considering the length of this *Epistle*, and that it is not aimed solely at me, I thought at first to make some *Extracts* from it regarding what related to myself; but finding, on a closer inquiry, that what concerned me was so blended and interwoven with what did not, I was obliged to trouble the reader with it in its entirety; not without hopes that, wordy as it is, its extravagance will be entertaining to those who have read the *Treatise* it condemns with so much horror.

# THE
## Grumbling HIVE:
->OR,<-
### KNAVES *turned Honest.*

A spacious hive well stocked with bees,
That lived in luxury and ease;
And yet as famous for laws and arms,
As yielding large and early swarms;
Was counted the great nursery
Of sciences and industry.
No bees had better government,
More fickleness, or less contentment:
They were not slaves to tyranny,
Nor ruled by wild democracy;
But kings, who could not do wrong, because
Their power was circumscribed by laws.
These insects lived like men, and all
Our actions they performed, in small:
They did whatever is done in town,
And what belongs to sword or gown: <note>The "sword" refers to the military, and the "gown" refers to professional classes such as lawyers, the clergy, or academics.</note>
Though artful works, by nimble sleight
Of minute limbs, escaped human sight;
Yet they have no engines, laborers,
Ships, castles, arms, artificers, <note>Artificers refers to skilled craftsmen or artisans.</note>

<vocab>Grumbling Hive, Bees, Luxury, Laws, Democracy, Artificers, Mandeville</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a prosperous and sophisticated society of bees that mirrors human civilization, complete with a constitutional monarchy, industry, and diverse professions. Despite their success and good government, the bees are characterized by their fickleness and lack of contentment.</summary>

<keywords>The Grumbling Hive, Bernard Mandeville, allegory, social structure, constitutional monarchy, 18th-century poetry, industry, law</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem "The Grumbling Hive," further detailing the social and economic structure of the bee society as an allegory for human civilization.</meta>

<header>The Grumbling Hive: Or,</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

Craft, science, shop, or instrument,
But they had an equivalent:
Which, since their language is unknown,
Must be called, as we do our own.
As grant, that among other things,
They lacked dice, yet they had kings;
And those had guards; from which we may
Justly conclude, they had some games;
Unless a regiment can be shown
Of soldiers, who make use of none.

VAST numbers thronged the fruitful hive;
Yet those vast numbers made them thrive;
Millions endeavoring to supply
Each other’s lust and vanity;
While other millions were employed,
To see their handiwork destroyed;
They furnished half the universe;
Yet had more work than laborers.
Some with vast capital, and little effort,
Jumped into business of great gains;
And some were condemned to scythes and spades,
And all those hard laborious trades;
Where willing wretches daily sweat,
And wear out strength and limbs to eat:
<margin>Brazen faces</margin>
((A)). While others followed mysteries <note>In this context, "mysteries" refers to specialized trades or crafts.</note>,
To which few folks bind apprentices;
That require no capital, except for boldness,
And may set up business without a coin <note>To "set up without a cross" refers to starting a business without money, as many coins were marked with a cross.</note>;
Such as sharpers, parasites, pimps, players,
Pickpockets, coiners, quacks, soothsayers,
And all those, who in hostility
To honest labor, cunningly
Convert to their own use the labor
Of their good-natured, heedless neighbor.
<margin>Knave</margin>
((B)) These were called knaves, but except for the name,
The grave and industrious were the same:
*All trades and places knew some cheat,*
*No calling was without deceit.*

<margin>Law and <unclear>Church</unclear></margin>
The Lawyers, whose art was based on
Instigating feuds and splitting cases,
Opposed all property registers, so that cheats
Might create more work with mortgaged estates; <note>"Dipt estates" refers to properties encumbered by debt or mortgages.</note>
As if it were unlawful that one's own property,
Without a lawsuit, should be known.
They willfully delayed hearings
To pocket the refreshing fee; <note>A "refreshing fee" was an additional payment made to a lawyer for a case that had been delayed or carried over.</note>
And to defend a wicked cause,
They examined and surveyed the laws,
Just as burglars do to shops and houses,
To find the best place to break through.

PHYSICIANS valued fame and wealth
Above the failing patient's health,
Or even their own skill: the greatest part
Studied, instead of the rules of medical science,
Solemn, pensive looks and dull behavior,
To gain the apothecary's favor;
The praise of midwives, priests, and all
Who served at birth or funeral.
To tolerate the ever-talking tribe,
And listen to my lady's aunt prescribe;
With a formal smile and a kind "How are you?"
To fawn on the whole family;
And—which of all is the greatest curse—
To endure the impertinence of nurses.

Among the many priests of *Jove*,
Hired to draw down blessings from above,
A few were learned and eloquent,
But thousands were zealous and ignorant:
Yet all passed muster if they could hide
Their sloth, lust, greed, and pride;
For which they were as famous as tailors
Are for "cabbage," <note>"Cabbage" was a slang term for the scraps of cloth stolen by tailors from their customers' fabric.</note> or sailors for brandy:
Some, thin-looking and poorly dressed,
Would mystically pray for "bread,"
Meaning by that an ample supply,
<unclear>Yet</unclear> literally received no more;

And, while these holy drudges starved,
The lazy ones, whom they served,
Indulged their ease, with all the graces
Of health and plenty in their faces.

<margin>Soldiers</margin>
((C)) **THE** soldiers, who were forced to fight,
If they survived, gained honor by it;
Though some, who shunned the bloody fray,
Had limbs shot off as they ran away:
Some valiant generals fought the foe;
Others took bribes to let them go:
Some always ventured where the fighting was hot,
Losing now a leg, and then an arm;
Until quite disabled, and set aside,
They lived on half their salary;
While others never saw action,
And stayed at home for double pay.

<margin>Courtiers, bribes, and others</margin>
**THEIR** kings were served, but knavishly
Cheated by their own ministers;
Many, who slaved for the crown's welfare,
Robbed the very crown they saved:
Pensions were small, and they lived high,
Yet boasted of their honesty.
Calling, whenever they overstepped their rights,
The slippery trick a "perquisite";
And when people understood their jargon,
They changed that word for "emolument";
Unwilling to be brief or plain,
In anything concerning profit;
((D.)) For there was not a bee who would not
Take more—I won't say than he should;
But more than he dared to let those know
((E.)) Who paid for it; just like gamblers,
Who, even in a fair game, will never admit
Before the losers how much they have won.

<margin>Dung sellers</margin>
But who can repeat all their frauds?
The very stuff, which in the street
They sold as manure to enrich the ground,
Was often found by the buyers

Adulterated with a quarter

Who sold the other salt for butter.
<margin>Justice blind against the poor</margin>
  JUSTICE herself, famed for fair dealing,
By blindness had not lost her feeling;
Her left hand, which the scales should hold,
Had often dropped them, bribed with gold;
And, though she seemed impartial,
Where punishment was physical,
Pretended to a regular course,
In murder, and all crimes of force;
Though some, first pilloried for cheating,
Were hanged in hemp of their own beating;
Yet, it was thought, the sword she bore
Checked only the desperate and the poor;
That, urged by mere necessity,
Were tied up to the wretched tree
For crimes, which did not deserve that fate,
But to secure the rich and great.
<margin>Vice</margin>
  THUS every part was full of vice,
Yet the whole mass a paradise;
Flattered in peace, and feared in wars,
They were the esteem of foreigners,
And lavish of their wealth and lives,
The balance of all other hives.
Such were the blessings of that state:
Their crimes conspired to make them great:
(F.) And virtue, who from politics
Had learned a thousand cunning tricks,
Was, by their happy influence,
Made friends with vice: And ever since,
(G.) The worst of all the multitude
Did something for the common good.

# The Grumbling Hive : Or,

(H.) Parties directly opposite,
Assist each other, as if for spite;
And temperance with sobriety,
Serve drunkenness and gluttony.
(I.) THE root of evil, avarice,
That damned, ill-natured, baneful vice, <gloss>vice</gloss>
Was slave to prodigality, <gloss>aid</gloss>
((K.)) That noble sin, ((L.)) whilst luxury <gloss>ing</gloss>
Employed a million of the poor,
(M.) And odious pride a million more:
(N.) Envy itself, and vanity,
Were ministers of industry;
Their darling folly, fickleness,
In diet, furniture, and dress,
That strange ridiculous vice, was made
The very wheel that turned the trade.
Their laws and clothes were equally
Objects of mutability;
For, what was well done for a time,
In half a year became a crime;
Yet while they altered thus their laws,
Still finding and correcting flaws,
They mended by inconstancy
Faults, which no prudence could foresee.
THUS vice nursed ingenuity,
Which joined with time and industry,
Had carried life's conveniences,
(O) Its real pleasures, comforts, ease,
(P.) To such a height, the very poor
Lived better than the rich before,
And nothing could be added more.
How vain is mortal happiness!
Had they but known the bounds of bliss;
And that perfection here below
Is more than gods can well bestow;
<margin>citizens</margin>
The grumbling brutes had been content
With ministers and government.
But they, at every ill success,
Like creatures lost without redress,

<summary>This section of the poem argues that societal vices like avarice, pride, and fickleness are the actual drivers of economic industry and innovation. It concludes that these "vices" resulted in a standard of living where the poor lived better than the wealthy of previous generations, yet the citizens remained ungrateful and complained about their government.</summary>

# Knaves turned Honest.

Cursed politicians, armies, fleets;
While every one cried, *Damn the cheats*,
And would, though conscious of his own,
In others barbarously tolerate none.

<margin>Gnat and Camel</margin>
One, who had gathered a princely store,
By cheating master, king, and poor,
Dared cry aloud, *The land must sink*
*For all its fraud*; and whom do you think
The sermonizing rascal scolded?
A glover who sold lamb for kid.

<margin>Honesty established generally, pages 7 & 8.</margin>
The least thing was not done amiss,
Or crossed the public business;
But all the rogues cried brazenly,
*Good Gods, had we but honesty!*
*Mercury* smiled at the impudence,
And others called it want of sense,
Always to rail at what they loved:
But *Jove*, with indignation moved,
At last in anger swore, *He would rid*
*The bawling hive of fraud*; and did.
The very moment it departs,
And honesty fills all their hearts;
There shows them, like the instructive tree,
Those crimes which they are ashamed to see;
Which now in silence they confess,
By blushing at their ugliness:
Like children, who would hide their faults,
And by their color own their thoughts;
Imagining, when they are looked upon,
That others see what they have done.
But, Oh you Gods! What consternation,
How vast and sudden was the alteration!
In half an hour, the nation round,
Meat fell a penny in the pound.
The mask of hypocrisy is flung down,
From the great statesman to the clown:
And some in borrowed looks well known,
Appeared like strangers in their own.

<page-num>8</page-num>

### The Grumbling Hive: Or,

The Bar was silent from that day;
For now the willing debtors pay,
Even what was by creditors forgotten,
Who released those who had nothing.
Those who were in the wrong stood mute,
And dropped the patched-up, vexatious suit:
On which, since nothing thrives less
Than lawyers in an honest hive,
All, except those who had earned enough,
Marched off with inkhorns by their sides.
JUSTICE hanged some, set others free;
And after jail delivery,
Her presence being no longer required,
Retired with all her train and pomp.
First marched some smiths with locks and grates,
Fetters, and doors with iron plates:
Next jailers, turnkeys, and assistants:
Before the Goddess, at some distance,
Her chief and faithful minister,
Squire CATCH <note>A traditional name for the public executioner, referring to the historical figure Jack Ketch.</note>, the law’s great finisher,
Carried not the imaginary sword,
But his own tools: an axe and rope:
Then on a cloud the blindfolded lady,
JUSTICE herself, was pushed by air:
Around her chariot, and behind,
Were sergeants, bailiffs of every kind,
Tip-staffs, and all those officers
That squeeze a living out of tears.

<margin>Doctors became honest</margin>
Though medicine continued while folks were ill,
None would prescribe it but bees of skill,
Who were dispersed so widely through the hive
That none of them had any need to ride on horseback;
They waived vain disputes and strove to free
Their patients from their misery;
They abandoned drugs grown in cheating countries,
And used the products of their own;
Knowing the Gods sent no disease
To nations without providing remedies.

<meta>catchword: THEIR</meta>

<summary>Following the arrival of honesty, the legal system collapses as lawsuits vanish and debtors pay their debts, leading Justice and her executioners to depart the hive. The medical profession is similarly reformed, with physicians becoming humble, avoiding unnecessary disputes, and favoring local remedies over expensive imports.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, legal reform, debtors, Squire Catch, executioner, medicine, physicians, local remedies, honesty, Mandeville</keywords>

THEIR Clergy, roused from laziness,
Did not lay their duties on Journey-Bees <note>A play on "journeyman," referring to underpaid curates who performed the tasks of wealthy, absent clergymen.</note>,
But served themselves, free from vice,
The Gods with prayer and sacrifice;
All those who were unfit, or knew
Their service could be spared, withdrew:
Nor was there work for so many;
(If the honest stand in need of any,)
Only a few stayed with the High Priest,
To whom the rest paid obedience:
He himself was employed in holy cares,
And resigned state affairs to others.
He turned no starving person from his door,
Nor pinched the wages of the poor;
But at his house the hungry are fed,
The worker finds unlimited bread,
The needy traveler finds board and bed.

<margin>
Courtiers turned honest,
</margin>
Among the King’s great Ministers,
And all the lower Officers,
The change was great; (Q.) for frugally
They now lived on their salary:
That a poor bee should have to come ten times
To ask for his due, a trifling sum,
And be forced by some well-hired clerk
To give a crown <note>A silver coin; here referring to a bribe.</note>, or never be paid,
Would now be called a total cheat,
Though formerly it was a perquisite <note>A tip or social custom that allowed officials to profit beyond their wages.</note>.
All offices managed first by three,
Who watched each other’s dishonesty,
And often, out of a fellow-feeling,
Promoted one another’s stealing,
Are now happily supplied by one,
By which some thousands more are gone.

<margin>
Liveries
Coaches
Horses
Debts
</margin>
(R.) No man of honor now could be content
To live and owe for what he spent;
Liveries <note>The distinctive uniforms worn by a nobleman's servants.</note> are hung in brokers' shops,
They part with coaches for a very small price;
They sell sets of stately horses,
And country houses, to pay debts.

### The Grumbling Hive: Or,

VAIN spending is shunned as much as fraud;
They have no military forces kept abroad;
They laugh at the high regard of foreigners,
And the empty glory gained by wars;
They fight only for their country's sake,
When right or liberty is at stake.

Now observe the glorious hive, and see
How honesty and trade agree.
The show is gone; it thins quickly,
And looks with quite another face.
For it was not only that those departed
By whom vast sums were yearly spent;
But multitudes that lived off them
Were daily forced to do the same.
In vain they would fly to other trades;
All were over-stocked accordingly.

<margin>und</margin>
<margin>y</margin>
<margin>ual</margin>
<margin>us</margin>
<margin>he</margin>
THE price of land and houses falls;
Miraculous palaces, whose walls,
Like those of *Thebes* <note>In Greek mythology, the walls of Thebes were said to have been built by the music of Amphion’s lyre; here Mandeville suggests these palaces were built by the "play" of gambling or speculation.</note>, were raised by play,
Are to be let; while the once gay,
Well-situated household gods <note>A reference to the *Penates*, or Roman spirits of the home, representing the dignity and status of a household.</note> would be
More pleased to expire in flames than see
The humble inscription on the door
Mocking the lofty ones they formerly bore.
The building trade is quite destroyed;
Craftsmen are not employed;
(S.) No painter <note>Limner: a term for a painter, particularly one who paints portraits or miniatures.</note> is famous for his art;
Stone-cutters and carvers are not even mentioned.

THOSE who remained, having become temperate, strive
Not how to spend, but how to live;
And, when they paid their tavern bill,
They resolved to enter it no more.
No wine-seller's mistress in all the hive
Could now wear cloth of gold and thrive;
Nor were people forced to pay such vast sums
For *Burgundy* and *Orléans* wines;
The courtier is gone, who with his mistress
Supped at his house on Christmas peas <note>Fresh peas in December were an extremely expensive out-of-season luxury in the 18th century.</note>;

Spending as much in a two-hour stay,
As keeps a troop of horses a day.
THE haughty *Chloe*, to live grandly,
Had made her (*T.*) husband rob the state:
But now she sells her furniture,
For which the *Indies* had been ransacked;
She reduces her expensive bill of fare,
And wears her sturdy suit for a whole year:
The frivolous and fickle age is past;
And clothes, as well as fashions, last.
Weavers, who combined rich silk with silver thread <note>Plate: silver or gold thread used in weaving expensive fabrics.</note>,
And all the subordinate trades,
Are gone. Still, peace and plenty reign,
And everything is cheap, though plain:
Kind Nature, free from gardeners' force,
Allows all fruits in her own course;
But rarities cannot be had,
Where the effort to get them is not rewarded.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As pride and luxury decrease,
So by degrees they leave the seas.
Not merchants now, but companies
Move whole manufacturing industries.
All arts and crafts neglected lie;
(*V.*) Contentment, the ruin of industry,
Makes them admire their simple store,
And neither seek nor covet more,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So few in the vast hive remain,
That a hundredth part they cannot maintain
Against the insults of numerous foes;
Whom they yet valiantly oppose:
Until some well-fortified retreat is found,
And here they die, or stand their ground.
<image-desc>A large curly brace in the left margin groups the final eight lines of the text.</image-desc>
No hired soldier in their army is known;
But bravely fighting for their own,
Their courage and integrity
At last were crowned with victory.
They triumphed not without their cost;
For many thousand bees were lost.

Hardened with toils and exercise,
They counted ease itself a vice;
Which so improved their temperance;
That, to avoid extravagance,
They flew into a hollow tree,
Blessed with contentment and honesty.

## The MORAL.

THEN *stop complaining: only fools strive*
(X.) *To make a great hive an honest one.*
(Y.) *To enjoy the world's conveniences,*
*To be famous in war, yet live in ease,*
*Without great vices, is a vain*
*UTOPIA seated in the brain.*
*Fraud, luxury, and pride must live,*
*While we receive the benefits:*
*Hunger is a dreadful plague, no doubt,*
*Yet who digests or thrives without?*
*Do we not owe the growth of wine*
*To the dry, shabby, crooked vine?*
*Which, while its shoots neglected stood,*
*Choked other plants and ran to wood;*
*But blessed us with its noble fruit,*
*As soon as it was tied and cut:*
*So vice is found to be beneficial,*
*When it is lopped and bound by justice;*
<image-desc>A large curly bracket on the right margin groups the following three lines.</image-desc>
*Indeed, where the people would be great,*
*Vice is as necessary to the state,*
*As hunger is to make them eat.*
*Virtue alone cannot make nations live*
*In splendor; those who would revive*
*A Golden Age must be as free*
*To eat acorns as to have honesty.*

->F I N I S.<-

<vocab>Mandeville, The Grumbling Hive, Moral, Vice, Virtue, Utopia, A Golden Age, Honesty, State</vocab>

<summary>The poem concludes by explaining that a prosperous, powerful nation cannot exist without the "vices" of greed and luxury to drive its economy. Mandeville argues that seeking a wealthy yet perfectly honest society is a foolish fantasy, as "greatness" requires the same uncomfortable impulses as hunger.</summary>

<keywords>The Moral, private vices, public benefits, Bernard Mandeville, social paradox, economic theory, virtue vs. prosperity, Golden Age</keywords>

# THE
## INTRODUCTION.

ONE of the greatest *reasons* why so few *people* understand themselves is that most *writers* are always teaching *men* what they should be, and hardly ever trouble their *heads* with telling them what they really are. As for my part, without any *compliment* to the *courteous reader* or myself, I believe *man* (besides *skin*, *flesh*, *bones*, etc., that are obvious to the *eye*) to be a *compound* of various *passions*. All of these, as they are provoked and come to the surface, govern him by turns, whether he wants them to or not. To show that these *qualifications*, which we all pretend to be ashamed of, are the great support of a *flourishing society*, has been the subject of the previous *poem*. But since there are some *passages* in

<summary>The author introduces his prose commentary, arguing that writers focus too much on how people ought to behave rather than how they actually function. He asserts that human beings are driven by various passions, and his poem aims to show how these supposedly shameful traits actually support a prosperous society.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, passions, morality vs reality, social stability, Bernard Mandeville, The Fable of the Bees, qualifications</keywords>

<header>The Introduction.</header>
<meta>catchword: AN</meta>

*it that seem paradoxical, I have in the preface promised some explanatory remarks on it; to make these more useful, I have thought it fit to inquire how man, no better qualified, might yet be taught by his own imperfections to distinguish between virtue and vice: and here I must ask the reader once and for all to take notice, that when I say men, I mean neither Jews nor Christians, but mere man, in the state of nature and ignorance of the true Deity.*

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal essay "An Enquiry into the Origin of Moral Virtue," following the introduction where the author established his intent to analyze human nature in its raw state, independent of religious revelation.</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal rectangular woodcut headpiece with symmetrical floral scrollwork and ornate foliate patterns.</image-desc>

### AN
# ENQUIRY
### Into the ORIGIN of
## MORAL VIRTUE.

All untaught animals are only concerned with pleasing themselves and naturally follow the direction of their own inclinations, without considering the good or harm that will come to others from their being pleased. This is the reason that, in the wild state of nature, those creatures are best suited to live peaceably together in great numbers who show the least understanding and have the fewest appetites to satisfy; and consequently, without the restraint of government, no species of animal is less capable of agreeing for long in large groups than man. Yet such are his qualities—whether good or bad, I shall not determine—that no creature besides himself can ever be made sociable. But because he is an extraordinarily selfish, headstrong, and cunning animal, however much he may be subdued by superior strength, it is impossible by force alone to make him manageable and ready to receive the improvements he is capable of.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<meta>This page continues the essay "An Enquiry into the Origin of Moral Virtue," building on the premise that humans are naturally selfish and difficult to govern through force alone. The author now examines the psychological strategies used by early leaders to civilize society.</meta>

The main thing, therefore, that lawgivers and other wise men who worked to establish society have tried to do, was to make the people they governed believe that it was more beneficial for everyone to conquer their appetites rather than indulge them, and much better to care for the public interest than for what seemed to be their own private interest. As this has always been a very difficult task, no wit or eloquence has been left untried to achieve it; and the moralists and philosophers of all ages have used their greatest skill to prove the truth of such a useful claim. But whether mankind would have ever believed it or not, it is unlikely that anyone could have persuaded them to disapprove of their natural inclinations, or to prefer the good of others over their own, if they had not also been shown an equivalent reward for the violence they must necessarily commit against themselves by doing so. Those who undertook to civilize mankind were not ignorant of this; but being unable to give enough real rewards to satisfy everyone for every individual action, they were forced to invent an imaginary one. This would serve on all occasions as a general equivalent for the trouble of self-denial and, without costing anything to themselves or others, would still be a most acceptable reward for the recipients.

They thoroughly examined all the strengths and frailties of our nature, and observing that no one was so savage as to be immune to praise, or so lowly as to patiently endure contempt, they rightly concluded that flattery must be the most powerful argument that could be used on human beings. Using this bewitching tool, they praised the excellence of our nature above other animals, and setting forth with un-*bounded praises the wonders of our wisdom and the vastness of our understanding, bestowed a thousand*

<summary>The author argues that lawgivers and philosophers used flattery and the promise of social status as "imaginary rewards" to persuade naturally selfish humans to practice self-denial and prioritize the public good. By appealing to human pride and the desire for praise, they made civilization and social cooperation possible without the need for constant tangible rewards.</summary>

To introduce, moreover, a sense of competition among men, they divided the whole species into two classes, vastly differing from one another. The one consisted of abject, low-minded people that, always hunting after immediate enjoyment, were wholly incapable of self-denial; and without regard to the good of others, had no higher aim than their private advantage. Such people, being enslaved by voluptuousness, yielded without resistance to every gross desire, and made no use of their rational faculties except to heighten their sensual pleasure. These vile, groveling wretches, they said, were the dross of their kind, and having only the shape of men, differed from animals in nothing but their outward shape.

These they called the true representatives of their sublime species, exceeding the first class in worth by more degrees than that class itself was superior to the beasts of the field.

Just as in all animals that are not too imperfect to manifest pride, we find that the finest, most beautiful, and most valuable of their kind generally have the greatest share of it; so in man, the most perfect of animals, it is so inseparable from his very essence (no matter how cunningly some may learn to hide or disguise it) that without it, the compound he is made of would lack one of its chief ingredients. If we consider this, it can hardly be doubted that lessons and arguments so skillfully adapted to the good opinion man has of himself—such as those I have mentioned—must, if scattered among a multitude, not only gain the assent of most people regarding the theoretical part, but also induce several (especially the fiercest, most resolute, and best among them) to endure a thousand inconveniences and undergo just as many hardships. <page-num>29</page-num>
<header>the Origin of Moral Virtue.</header>

...ordinary pains to master some of their natural appetites, and preferred the good of others to any visible interest of their own, would not recede an inch from the fine notions they had received concerning the dignity of rational creatures; and having always the authority of the government on their side, they would, with all imaginable vigor, assert the esteem that was due to those of the second class, as well as their superiority over the rest of their kind. Secondly, those who lacked a sufficient stock of either pride or resolution to sustain them in mortifying what was dearest to them followed the sensual dictates of nature, yet would be ashamed to confess themselves to be those despicable wretches who belonged to the inferior class and were generally considered to be so little removed from brutes; and therefore, in their own defense, they would say as others did, and hiding their own imperfections as well as they could, they would praise self-denial and public-spiritedness as much as anyone. For it is highly probable that some of them, convinced by the real proofs of fortitude and self-conquest they had seen, would admire in others what they found lacking in themselves; others would be afraid of the resolution and prowess of those of the second class, and all of them were kept in awe by the power of their rulers. Therefore, it is reasonable to think that none of them (whatever they thought within themselves) would dare openly to contradict what everyone else thought it criminal to doubt.

This was (or at least might have been) the manner in which Savage Man was broken; from which it is evident that the first rudiments of **morality**, introduced by skillful politicians to make men useful to each other as well as manageable, were chiefly contrived so that the ambitious might reap more benefit from them and govern vast numbers of them with greater ease and security. <page-num>21</page-num>

Power, and consequently that the notions of Good and Evil, and the distinction between Virtue and Vice, were never the contrivance of politicians, but the pure effect of religion. Before I answer this objection, I must repeat what I have said already: that in this *Inquiry* into the *Origin* of *Moral Virtue*, I speak of neither *Jews* nor *Christians*, but man in his state of nature and ignorance of the true Deity. And then I affirm that the idolatrous superstitions of all other nations, and the pitiful notions they had of the Supreme Being, were incapable of exciting man to virtue, and were good for nothing but to awe and amuse a rude and unthinking multitude.

It is evident from history that in all considerable societies, no matter how stupid or ridiculous people’s received notions have been regarding the deities they worshiped, human nature has ever exerted itself in all its branches. There is no earthly wisdom or moral virtue in which men have not excelled at one time or another in all monarchies and commonwealths that have been in any way remarkable for riches and power.

The *Egyptians*, not satisfied with having deified all the ugly monsters they could think of, were so silly as to adore the onions of their own sowing; yet at the same time, their country was the most famous nursery of arts and sciences in the world, and they themselves were more eminently skilled in the deepest mysteries of nature than any nation has been since.

No states or kingdoms under heaven have yielded more or greater patterns in all sorts of moral virtues than the *Greek* and *Roman* empires, especially the latter; and yet how loose, absurd, and ridiculous were their sentiments regarding sacred matters? For without reflecting on the extravagant number of their deities, if we only consider the infamous stories they fathered upon them, it <unclear>cannot</unclear> be denied that their religion, far

It is the same with boys, whom they will strive to persuade that all fine gentlemen do as they are told, and that none but beggar boys are rude or dirty their clothes; indeed, as soon as the wild brat with his untaught fist begins to fumble for his hat, the mother—to make him pull it off—tells him before he is two years old that he is a man; and if he repeats that action when she desires him, he is presently a captain, a lord mayor, a king, or something higher if she can think of it; until, egged on by the force of praise, the little urchin endeavors to imitate a man as well as he can, and strains all his faculties to appear as what his shallow head imagines he is believed to be.

Whatever mortification this truth might be to the second thoughts of an *Alexander* or a *Caesar*), the great recompense in view—for which the most exalted minds have with so much alacrity sacrificed their quiet, health, sensual pleasures, and every inch of themselves—has never been anything else but the breath of man, the airy coin of praise. Who can help laughing when he thinks of all the great men who have been so serious on the subject of that *Macedonian* madman, his capacious soul, and that mighty heart, in one corner of which, according to *Lorenzo Gratian*, the world was so conveniently lodged that in the whole there was room for six more? Who can help laughing, I say, when he compares the fine things that have been said of *Alexander* with the end he proposed for himself from his vast exploits, as proved from his own mouth; when the vast pains he took to pass the *Hydaspes* forced him to cry out: "*Oh you Athenians, could you believe what dangers I expose myself to, to be praised by you!*" To define, then, the reward of glory in the fullest manner, the most that can be said of it is that it consists in a supreme happiness which a man, who is conscious of having performed a noble action, enjoys in self-love, while he is thinking of the applause he expects from others.

But here I shall be told that, besides the noisy toils of war and public bustle of the ambitious, there are noble and generous actions that are performed in silence; that virtue being its own reward, those who are really good have a satisfaction in their consciousness of being so, which is all the recompense they expect from the most worthy performances; that among the heathens there have been men who, when they did good to others, were so far from coveting thanks and applause that they took all imaginable care to be forever <unclear>conceal'd</unclear> from those on whom they bestowed their <unclear>Benefits</unclear>; and consequently that pride has no hand

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the motives of human virtue, moving from the pursuit of public glory to a critique of pity, arguing that it is a natural impulse rather than a reasoned moral virtue.</meta>

...in spurring man on to the highest height of self-denial.

In answer to this I say, that it is impossible to judge a man’s performance unless we are thoroughly acquainted with the principle and motive from which he acts. Pity, though it is the most gentle and the least mischievous of all our passions, is still as much a frailty of our nature as anger, pride, or fear. The weakest minds generally have the greatest share of it, for which reason none are more compassionate than women and children. It must be admitted that of all our weaknesses, it is the most amiable and bears the greatest resemblance to virtue; indeed, without a considerable mixture of it, society could hardly survive. But as it is an impulse of nature that consults neither the public interest nor our own reason, it may produce evil as well as good. It has helped to destroy the honor of virgins and corrupted the integrity of judges; and whoever acts from it as a principle—whatever good he may bring to society—has nothing to boast of except that he has indulged a passion that happened to be beneficial to the public. There is no merit in saving an innocent baby ready to drop into the fire. The action is neither good nor bad, and whatever benefit the infant received, we only served ourselves; for to have seen it fall and not striven to prevent it would have caused a pain which self-preservation compelled us to avoid. Nor has a rich prodigal, who happens to have a compassionate temperament and loves to gratify his passions, any greater virtue to boast of when he relieves an object of compassion with what is a trifle to himself.

But such men as can part with what they value themselves, without complying with any weakness of their own, and perform a worthy action *in silence* from no other motive than their love of goodness—such men, I confess, have acquired

<summary>The author argues that pity is a natural impulse and human frailty rather than a true virtue, often acting independently of reason or the public interest. He distinguishes between actions driven by emotional impulse and those performed out of a genuine, silent love for goodness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that even seemingly selfless acts of virtue are rooted in human passions, specifically arguing that the inner satisfaction felt after a virtuous deed is a form of pride.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>the Origin of Moral Virtue.</header>
<warning>Heavy ink staining and bleed-through from the reverse side are visible throughout the lower half of the page.</warning>

...more refined notions of virtue than those I have spoken of so far; yet even in these (which have never been common in the world) we may discover no small symptoms of pride. Even the humblest man alive must confess that the reward of a virtuous action—which is the satisfaction that follows it—consists in a certain pleasure he procures for himself by contemplating his own worth. This pleasure, along with the occasion for it, are as certain signs of pride as looking pale and trembling at any imminent danger are symptoms of fear.

If the overly scrupulous reader should at first glance condemn these notions concerning the origin of moral virtue, and perhaps think them offensive to Christianity, I hope he will withhold his censures when he considers that nothing can make the unsearchable depth of Divine Wisdom more conspicuous than that man, whom Providence had designed for society, should not only be led toward temporal happiness by his own frailties and imperfections, but also receive, from a seeming necessity of natural causes, a trace of that knowledge in which he was later to be made perfect by True Religion, for his eternal welfare.

->(A.) *While others followed* Mysteries,<-
->*To which few people bind* apprentices:<-

->Page 2. Line 25.<-

In the *education* of *youth*, in order for them to earn a *livelihood* when they have reached *maturity*, most people look for some warrantable *employment* or another, of which there are entire *bodies* or *companies* in every large *society* of *men*. By this means, all *arts* and *sciences*, as well as *trades* and *handicrafts*, are perpetuated in the *commonwealth* as long as they are found useful; the *young ones* who are daily brought up in them continually supply the loss of the *old ones* who die. But since some of these *employments* are vastly more *reputable* than others, according to the great difference in the *costs* required to set up in each of them, all prudent *parents*, in choosing among them, chiefly consult their own *abilities* and the *circumstances* they are in. A man who pays three or four hundred pounds for his *son* to apprentice with a great *merchant*, and does not have two or three thousand pounds to spare for when the son’s *apprenticeship* is finished to begin his career with, is much to blame for not having brought his *child* up to something that might be followed with less *money*.

There are plenty of *men* with a *genteel education* who have only very small *revenues*, and yet are forced, by their *reputable callings*, to maintain a greater *figure* than ordinary people with twice their *income*

<header>Remark (A.)</header>
<warning>Dark smudging in the bottom left corner obscures some text.</warning>

If these have any children, it often happens that as their indigence renders them incapable of bringing them up to reputable occupations, so their pride makes them unwilling to apprentice them to any of the lowly, laborious *trades*. Then, in hopes either of an alteration in their *fortune*, or that some friends or a favorable opportunity shall offer, they from time to time put off placing them, until they gradually reach adulthood and are at last brought up to nothing. Whether this neglect is more cruel to the children or harmful to society, I shall not determine. In *Athens*, all *children* were forced to assist their *parents* if they fell into *need*; but *Solon* made a law that no son should be obliged to relieve his *father* who had not raised him in any *calling*.

Some parents apprentice their sons to good trades very suitable to their then-present abilities, but happen to die or fail in the world before the children have finished their *apprenticeships* or are made fit for the *business* they are to follow. On the other hand, many young men are handsomely provided for and established for themselves, yet (some for want of *industry* or else a sufficient knowledge of their *callings*, others by indulging their *pleasures*, and some few by *misfortunes*) are reduced to poverty and are altogether unable to maintain themselves by the *business* they were brought up to. It is impossible but that the neglects, mismanagements, and misfortunes I named must very frequently happen in populous places, and consequently, great numbers of people are daily cast unprovided for into the wide world, however rich and potent a commonwealth may be, or whatever care a government may take to prevent it. Those who ever took delight in plays and romances, and have a touch of gentility, will, in all probability, look toward the stage; and if they have good elocution with a tolerable mien, turn *actors*. Some who love their bellies above anything else, if they have a good palate and a little knack for cookery, will strive to get in with *gluttons* and *epicures*, learn to cringe and endure all manner of treatment, and so become *parasites*, always flattering the master and making mischief among the rest of the family. Others, who judge people’s lack of restraint by their own and their companions' lewdness, will naturally fall into intriguing, and endeavor to live by pimping for those who either lack the leisure or the skill to speak for themselves. Those of the most abandoned principles of all, if they are sly and dexterous, turn sharpers, pickpockets, or coiners, if their skill and ingenuity allow them. Others again, who have observed the credulity of simple women and other foolish people, if they have impudence and a little cunning, either set themselves up as doctors or else pretend to tell fortunes; and everyone, turning the vices and frailties of others to his own advantage, endeavors to make a living in the easiest and shortest way his talent and abilities will allow him.

These are certainly the bane of civil society; but they are fools who, not considering what has been said, rage at the laxity of the laws that allow them to live, while wise men content themselves with taking all imaginable care not to be outwitted by them, without quarreling at <unclear>t</unclear>hat which no human prudence can prevent. # Remark (B.)

->(B.) These were called *Knaves*, but except for the name,<-
->The serious *Industrious* were the same.<-

---

->Page 2. Line 35.<-

THIS, I confess, is but a very poor compliment to all the trading portion of the people. But if the word *Knave* can be understood in its full breadth, and includes everyone who is not sincerely honest, and does to others what he would dislike to have done to himself, I do not doubt that I shall prove the accusation. Ignoring the innumerable tricks by which buyers and sellers outwit one another—which are daily permitted and practiced among the most honest of dealers—show me the tradesman who has always revealed the defects of his goods to those who bargained for them; indeed, where will you find one who has not, at one time or another, deliberately concealed them to the detriment of the *Buyer*? Where is the *Merchant* who has never, against his conscience, praised his wares beyond their worth to make them sell better?

*Decio*, a man of great importance who held large commissions for sugar from several parts overseas, negotiates for a considerable quantity of that commodity with *Alcander*, an eminent *West-India Merchant*. Both understood the market very well, but could not agree: *Decio* was a man of wealth and thought nobody should buy cheaper than he; *Alcander* was the same, and since he did not lack money, he stood firm on his price. While they were conducting their bargain at a tavern near the *Exchange*, *Alcander*'s servant brought his master a letter from the <unclear>post-house</unclear> that informed him of a much greater

> (C.) *The soldiers who were forced to fight,*
> *If they survived, gained honor by it.*

->Page 4. Line 5.<-

**SO** unaccountable is the desire to be thought well of in men, that although they are dragged into war against their will—and some of them for their crimes—and are compelled to fight with threats, and often blows, yet they wish to be esteemed for what they would have avoided if it had been in their power. Whereas if reason in man were of equal weight with his pride, he could never be pleased with praises which he is conscious he does not deserve.

By honor, in its proper and genuine significance, we mean nothing else but the good opinion of others, which is considered more or less substantial based on how much noise or bustle is made about the demonstration of it. And when we say the sovereign is the fountain of honor, it signifies that he has the power, by titles or ceremonies, or both together, to stamp a mark upon whomever he pleases, which shall be as current as his coin and procure the owner the good opinion of everyone, whether he deserves it or not.

# Remark (C.)

...ment for bad ones; and the more or less public or heinous the manner in which this contempt of others is shown, the more or less the person suffering is degraded by it. This ignominy is likewise called shame, from the effect it produces; for although the good and evil of honor and dishonor are imaginary, yet there is a reality in shame. It signifies a passion that has its own symptoms, overrules our reason, and requires as much labor and self-denial to be subdued as any other passion; and since the most important actions of life are often regulated according to the influence this passion has upon us, a thorough understanding of it must help to illustrate the notions the world has of honor and ignominy. I shall therefore describe it at length.

First, to define the passion of shame, I think it may be called a *sorrowful reflection on our own unworthiness, proceeding from an apprehension that others either do, or might, if they knew all, deservedly despise us.* The only significant objection that can be raised against this definition is that innocent virgins are often ashamed and blush when they are guilty of no crime, and can give no reason for this frailty; and that men are often ashamed for others, with whom they have neither friendship nor affinity, and consequently that there may be a thousand instances of shame given to which the words of the definition are not applicable. To answer this, I would have it first considered that the modesty of women is the result of custom and education, by which all unfashionable nakedness and filthy expressions are rendered frightful and abominable to them; and that, despite this, the most virtuous young woman alive will often, in spite of herself, have thoughts and confused ideas of things arise in her imagination which she would not reveal to some people for a thousand worlds. Then, I say, that when obscene words are

# Remark (C.)

...spoken in the presence of an inexperienced virgin, she is afraid that someone will think she understands what they mean, and consequently that she understands this or that, and several things which she desires to be thought ignorant of. Reflecting on this—and the fact that thoughts are being formed to her disadvantage—brings upon her that passion which we call shame. Whatever can throw her (no matter how remote from lewdness) into that set of thoughts I hinted at, and which she considers criminal, will have the same effect, especially before men, as long as her modesty lasts.

To test the truth of this, let them talk as much bawdry as they please in the room next to the same virtuous young woman, where she is sure that she is undiscovered, and she will hear it, if not listen to it, without blushing at all, because then she views herself as no party concerned. If the discourse should stain her cheeks with red, whatever her innocence may imagine, it is certain that what causes her color is a passion not half as mortifying as that of shame. But if in the same place she hears something said of herself that must lead to her disgrace, or if anything is named of which she is secretly guilty, then it is ten to one that she will be ashamed and blush, even though nobody sees her; because she has reason to fear that she is, or, if all were known, would be thought of contemptibly.

That we are often ashamed and blush for others, which was the second part of the objection, is nothing else but that sometimes we make the case of others too nearly our own; just as people shriek when they feel others are in danger. While we are reflecting with too much earnestness on the effect which such a blameworthy action would produce in us if it were ours, the spirits, and consequently the blood, are imperceptibly moved in the same way as if that action were our own, and so the same symp<unclear>toms</unclear> must appear.

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Mandeville argues that shame in women arises from the fear of being perceived as knowledgeable about lewd topics, rather than the topics themselves. He also explains that blushing for others is a result of empathetic projection, where we imagine ourselves in another's disgraceful position.</summary>
<keywords>shame, modesty, blushing, social perception, empathy, physiological spirits, virtue, reputation</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the psychological analysis of shame and its connection to self-esteem, transitioning into a description of the physiological symptoms of both shame and pride.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

<header>Remark (C.)</header>

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

The shame that raw, ignorant, and ill-bred people—though seemingly without a cause—reveal before their superiors is always accompanied by, and proceeds from, a consciousness of their weakness and inabilities; and even the most modest man, however virtuous, knowledgeable, and accomplished he might be, was never yet ashamed without some guilt or lack of confidence. Those who, out of rusticity and a lack of education, are unreasonably subject to and frequently overcome by this passion, we call bashful; and those who, out of disrespect for others and a false opinion of their own sufficiency, have learned not to be affected by it when they should be, are called impudent or shameless. What strange contradictions man is made of! The reverse of shame is pride (*see Remark M.*), yet nobody can be touched by the first who has never felt anything of the latter; for the fact that we have such an extraordinary concern for what others think of us can proceed from nothing but the vast esteem we have for ourselves.

That these two passions, in which the seeds of most virtues are contained, are realities in our physical makeup and not imaginary qualities, is demonstrable from the clear and distinct effects that are produced, in spite of our reason, as soon as we are affected by either.

When a man is overwhelmed with shame, he notices a sinking of his <term>spirits</term>; the heart feels cold and constricted, and the blood flies from it to the extremities of the body; the face glows, and the neck and part of the chest partake of the fire. He feels heavy as lead; his head is hung down, and his eyes, through a mist of confusion, are fixed on the ground. No insults can move him; he is weary of his existence and heartily wishes he could make himself invisible. But when, gratifying his vanity, he exults in his pride, he displays quite contrary symptoms: his <term>spirits</term> swell and fan

<summary>Mandeville argues that shame and pride are inextricably linked to self-esteem and describes the distinct physiological reactions, such as blushing and the movement of "spirits," that accompany these emotions.</summary>

<keywords>shame, pride, bashfulness, impudence, self-esteem, animal spirits, physiology, Bernard Mandeville</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the physiological description of pride as a contrast to the symptoms of shame described on the previous page, and begins an argument for the social necessity of shame.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Remark (C.)</header>
<meta>catchword: are</meta>

the arterial blood; a more than ordinary warmth strengthens and dilates the heart; the extremities are cool; he feels light to himself and imagines he could tread on air; his head is held high, his eyes roll about with liveliness; he rejoices in his existence, is prone to anger, and would be glad if the whole world took notice of him.

It is incredible how necessary an ingredient shame is to make us sociable; it is a frailty in our nature. Everyone, whenever it affects them, submits to it with regret and would prevent it if they could; yet the happiness of conversation depends upon it, and no society could be polished if the majority of mankind were not subject to it. Since the sense of shame is therefore troublesome, and all creatures are always working for their own defense, it is probable that man, striving to avoid this uneasiness, would largely conquer his shame by the time he was grown up. However, this would be detrimental to society; therefore, from his infancy throughout his education, we endeavor to increase instead of lessening or destroying this sense of shame. The only remedy prescribed is a strict observance of certain rules to avoid those things that might bring this troublesome sense of shame upon him. But as for ridding or curing him of it, the politician would sooner take away his life.

The rules I speak of consist in a dexterous management of ourselves, a stifling of our appetites, and hiding the real sentiments of our hearts before others. Those who are not instructed in these rules long before they reach maturity seldom make any progress in them afterward. To acquire and bring to perfection the accomplishment I hint at, nothing is more helpful than pride and good sense. The greediness we have for the esteem of others, and the raptures we enjoy at the thought of being liked—and perhaps admired—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding how pride and good sense act as tools to manage our impulses.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>Remark (C.)</header>
<meta>catchword: Fashion</meta>

are equivalents that more than compensate for the conquest of the strongest passions, and consequently keep us at a great distance from all such words or actions that can bring shame upon us. The passions we chiefly ought to hide for the happiness and improvement of society are lust, pride, and selfishness; therefore, the word modesty has three different meanings that vary with the passions it conceals.

As to the first—I mean that branch of modesty which has a general claim to chastity for its object—it consists in a sincere and painstaking effort to use all our faculties to stifle and hide from others that natural inclination we have to reproduce our species. Its lessons, like those of grammar, are taught to us long before we have occasion for them or understand their usefulness. For this reason, children are often ashamed and blush out of modesty before the natural impulse I am hinting at makes any impression on them. A girl who is modestly educated may, before she is two years old, begin to observe how careful the women she interacts with are about covering themselves before men; and as the same caution is instilled in her by rule as well as by example, it is very probable that at six years old she will be ashamed of showing her leg, without knowing any reason why such an act is blameworthy or what its tendency is.

To be modest, we ought in the first place to avoid all unfashionable exposures. A woman is not to be blamed for going with her neck bare if the custom of the country allows it; and when fashion dictates that stays <note>stiffened bodices or corsets</note> are to be cut very low, a blooming young woman may, without fear of reasonable criticism, show the whole world:

> *Upon her ample chest at a great distance grow.*

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of modesty from the previous page, which established that modesty has three meanings related to concealing lust, pride, and selfishness.
