# Remark (C.)

Fashion for women to hide their very feet is a breach of modesty, and she is impudent who shows half her face in a country where decency requires her to be veiled. In the second place, our language must be chaste and not only free but remote from obscenities—that is, whatever belongs to the reproduction of our species is not to be spoken of, and the least word or expression that, though at a great distance, has any relation to that function ought never to come from our lips. Thirdly, all postures and motions that can in any way sully the imagination—that is, put us in mind of what I have called obscenities—are to be avoided with great caution.

A young woman, moreover, who would be thought well-bred ought to be circumspect before men in all her behavior and never be known to receive, much less to bestow, favors upon them unless the great age of the man, close family relationship, or a vast superiority on either side excuse her. A young lady of refined education keeps a strict guard over her looks as well as her actions, and in her eyes we may read an awareness that she has a treasure about her, not out of danger of being lost, and which yet she is resolved not to part with on any terms. Thousands of satires have been made against prudes, and as many eulogies to extol the careless grace and negligent air of virtuous beauty. But the wiser sort of mankind are well assured that the free and open countenance of the smiling fair one is more inviting and yields greater hopes to the seducer than the ever-watchful look of a forbidding eye.

This strict reserve is to be complied with by all young women, especially virgins, if they value the esteem of the polite and knowing world. Men may take greater liberty because in them the appetite is more violent and ungovernable. Had equal harshness of discipline been imposed

<summary>This page defines three aspects of female modesty: concealing the body according to fashion and decency, maintaining chaste speech by avoiding any reference to reproduction, and controlling postures and motions that might provoke improper thoughts. It emphasizes that well-bred young women must guard their behavior scrupulously around men and maintain a reserved demeanor, as openness invites seduction, while acknowledging that men are permitted greater freedom due to their stronger natural appetites.</summary>

<keywords>modesty, chastity, women's behavior, reserve, decorum, obscenity, propriety, femininity, seduction, discipline, reputation, virgin women</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the social necessity of modesty and how absolute restraint in both sexes would interfere with the survival of society.</meta>

<page-num>40.</page-num>
<header>Remark (C.)</header>

upon both, neither of them could have made the first advances, and reproduction would have come to a standstill among all fashionable people. Since this was far from the politician’s <note>social leader or legislator</note> goal, it was advisable to ease and indulge the sex that suffered most from this severity, and to relax the rules where the passion was strongest and the burden of strict restraint would have been most intolerable.

For this reason, men are allowed to openly declare the veneration and great esteem they have for women, and to show greater satisfaction, mirth, and gaiety in their company than they usually do elsewhere. He may not only be polite and helpful to them on all occasions, but it is considered his duty to protect and defend them. He may praise the good qualities they possess and extol their merit with as many exaggerations as his imagination allows, provided they are consistent with good sense. He may talk of love; he may sigh and complain of the coldness of beautiful women; and what his tongue must not speak, he has the privilege to speak with his eyes. In that language, he may say whatever he pleases, as long as it is done with decency and short, sudden glances. But to pursue a woman too closely and stare fixedly at her is considered very rude. The reason is plain: it makes her uneasy and, if she is not sufficiently protected by artifice and pretense, often throws her into visible distress. Since the eyes are the windows of the soul, this impudent staring throws a raw, inexperienced woman into a panic, fearing that she may be seen through and that the man will discover, or has already detected, what is happening within her. It keeps her on a perpetual rack <note>a state of intense torment or pressure</note> that compels her to reveal her secret wishes and seems designed to extort from her the ultimate truth, which modesty commands her to deny with all her powers.

<summary>This page explains that because total modesty in both sexes would prevent reproduction, social rules allow men more freedom to pursue women through polite praise and the "language of the eyes." It also describes how fixed staring is considered rude because it pressures women to reveal the very desires that modesty requires them to hide.</summary>
<keywords>modesty, courtship, gender roles, reproduction, social etiquette, non-verbal communication, the politician, eyes as windows to the soul</keywords>

The multitude will hardly believe the excessive

same age is told to take up his coats and piss like
a man. It is shame and education that contain
the seeds of all politeness, and he that has neither,
and offers to speak the truth of his heart and
what he feels within, is the most contemptible
creature upon earth, though he committed no other
sin. If a man should tell a woman that he
could like nobody as well to propagate his spe-

desire at that moment to go about it, and according-

consequence would be that he would be called a
brute, the woman would run away, and he himself
would never be admitted into any civil company. There
is nobody who has any sense of shame but would
conquer the strongest passion rather than be so
exposed. But a man need not conquer his passions;
it is sufficient that he conceals them. Virtue bids
us subdue, but good breeding only requires we
should hide our appetites. A fashionable gentle-
man may have as violent an inclination for a wo-
man as the brutish fellow; but then he behaves

submission, presents, and assiduity, he endeavors
to secure her liking to his person, which if he
can achieve, the lady in a little while resigns her
body to him before witnesses in a most solemn man-
ner; at night they go to bed together, where the
modest reserved virgin very tamely suffers him to do

<summary>Mandeville argues that the differences in behavior between men and women are products of education and social training rather than nature. He distinguishes between virtue, which seeks to subdue passions, and "good breeding," which merely requires that one hide their natural appetites behind social rituals like courtship and marriage.</summary>

<header>Remark (O.)</header>

...whatever he pleases, and the result is that he obtains what he wanted without ever having asked for it directly.

The next day they receive visitors, and nobody laughs at them or speaks a word of what they have been doing. As for the young couple themselves—I am speaking of well-bred people—they take no more notice of one another than they did the day before; they eat and drink, enjoy themselves as usual, and, having done nothing to be ashamed of, are looked upon as the most modest people on earth—which, in reality, they may be. My point in saying this is to demonstrate that by being well-bred, we suffer no reduction in our sensual pleasures; rather, we only work for our mutual happiness and assist each other in the luxurious enjoyment of all worldly comforts. The fine gentleman I mentioned does not need to practice any greater self-denial than the savage, though the latter acted more according to the laws of nature and sincerity than the former. The man who gratifies his appetites in the manner that the customs of the country allow has no criticism to fear. If he is more lustful than goats or bulls, as soon as the ceremony is over, let him satisfy and exhaust himself with joy and ecstasies of pleasure, raising and indulging his appetites by turns as extravagantly as his strength and manhood will permit. He may safely laugh at the wise men who would reprove him; all the women and more than nine out of ten men are on his side. Indeed, he has the freedom to take pride in the fury of his unbridled passion; and the more he wallows in lust and strains every faculty to be abandonedly voluptuous, the sooner he will have the goodwill and gain the affection of women—not only the young, vain, and lascivious, but also the prudent, serious, and most sober matrons.

Because impudence is a vice, it does not follow that modesty is a virtue; it is built upon shame, a passion in our nature, and may be either good...

<header>Remark (C.)</header>

...or bad according to the actions performed from that motive. Shame may prevent a prostitute from yielding to a man in public, and that same shame may cause a bashful, good-natured creature, who has been overcome by frailty, to kill her infant. Passions may do good by chance, but there can be no merit except in conquering them.

If there were virtue in modesty, it would have the same force in the dark as it does in the light, which is not the case. Men of pleasure know this very well; they never trouble themselves with a woman’s virtue as long as they can conquer her modesty. Therefore, seducers do not make their attacks at midday, but dig their trenches at night.

> *Illa verecundis lux est praebenda puellis,*
> *Qua timidas latebras sperat habere pudor.*
<note>That light should be given to modest girls, in which shame hopes to have timid hiding places.</note>

People of means may sin without being exposed for their stolen pleasure; but servants and the poorer sort of women seldom have an opportunity to conceal a pregnancy, or at least its consequences. It is possible that an unfortunate girl from a good family may be left destitute and know of no way to make a living other than becoming a nursemaid or a chambermaid. She may be diligent, faithful, and obliging; she may possess great modesty and, if you like, be religious. She may resist temptations and preserve her chastity for years together, and yet at last encounter an unhappy moment in which she surrenders her honor to a powerful deceiver, who afterwards abandons her. If she becomes pregnant, her sorrows are unspeakable, and she cannot reconcile herself to the wretchedness of her condition; the fear of shame attacks her so vividly that every thought distracts her. The entire family she lives with has a high opinion of her virtue, and her last mistress regarded her as a saint. How will her enemies, who envied her—

<header>Remark (C.)</header>

her character, rejoice! How her relatives will detest her! The more modest she is now, and the more violently the dread of coming to shame hurries her away, the more wicked and more cruel her resolutions will be, either against herself or what she carries.

It is commonly imagined that she who can destroy her child—her own flesh and blood—must possess a vast amount of barbarity and be a savage monster, different from other women. But this is likewise a mistake, which we make for lack of understanding nature and the power of the passions. The same woman who murders her bastard child in the most execrable manner, if she is married afterward, may take care of, cherish, and feel all the tenderness for her infant that the fondest mother is capable of. All mothers naturally love their children; but as this is a passion, and all passions center in Self-Love, it may be subdued by any superior passion to soothe that same Self-Love, which—if nothing had intervened—would have commanded her to fondle her offspring. Common prostitutes, whom the whole world knows to be such, hardly ever destroy their children; indeed, even those who assist in robberies and murders are seldom guilty of this crime. This is not because they are less cruel or more virtuous, but because they have lost their modesty to a greater degree, and the fear of shame makes hardly any impression upon them.

Our love for what has never been within the reach of our senses is poor and insignificant; therefore, women have no natural love for what they carry. Their affection begins after the birth; what they feel before is the result of reason, education, and thoughts of duty. Even when children are first born, a mother’s love is weak and increases with the responsiveness of the child; *and grows to a prodigious height when, by signs, it begins to express his sorrows and joys, makes*

<header>Remark (C.)</header>

What labors and hazards have women not undergone to maintain and save their children; what force and fortitude beyond their sex have they not shown on their behalf! But the vilest women have exerted themselves in this regard as violently as the best. All are prompted to it by a natural drift and inclination, without any consideration of the injury or benefit society receives from it. There is no merit in pleasing ourselves, and the offspring itself is often irreparably ruined by the excessive fondness of parents; for though infants for two or three years may be the better for this indulgent care of mothers, yet afterward, if not moderated, it may totally spoil them, and it has brought many to the gallows.

If the reader thinks I have been too tedious on that branch of modesty by which we endeavor to appear chaste, I shall make him amends with the brevity with which I design to treat the remaining part, by which we would make others believe that the esteem we have for them exceeds the value we have for ourselves, and that we have no disregard so great for any interest as we have for our own. This laudable quality is commonly known by the name of manners and good breeding, and consists of a fashionable habit, acquired by precept and example, of flattering the pride and selfishness of others, and concealing our own with judgment and dexterity. This must only be understood regarding our commerce with our equals and superiors, and while we are in <unclear>peace</unclear> and amity with them; for our complaisance must never interfere with the rules of honor, <unclear>nor</unclear> the homage that is due to us from servants and <unclear>others</unclear> who depend upon us.

<unclear>i</unclear>s a piece or an example of either Good Breeding
or Bad Manners; and it will be very difficult through-

stranger to him, without consideration, is called
impudent, because he shows openly his selfishness
without having any regard to the selfishness of the
other. We may see in it likewise the reason why
a man ought to speak of his wife and children,
and everything that is dear to him, as sparingly as
is possible, and hardly ever of himself, especially
in commendation of them. A well-bred man may
be desirous, and even greedy for praise and the
esteem of others, but to be praised to his face of-
fends his modesty: the reason is this; all human
creatures, before they are yet polished, receive an
extraordinary pleasure in hearing themselves praised:
this we are all conscious of, and therefore when
we see a man openly enjoy and feast on this de-
light, in which we have no share, it rouses our sel-
fishness, and immediately we begin to envy and
hate him. For this reason the well-bred man con-
ceals his joy, and utterly denies that he feels any,
and by this means considering and soothing our sel-
fishness, he averts that envy and hatred, which
otherwise he would have justly had to fear. When from
our childhood we observe how those are ridiculed
who calmly can hear their own praises, it is possi-
ble that we may so strenuously endeavor to avoid
that pleasure, that in the course of time we grow uneasy
at the approach of it; but this is not following the
dictates of nature, but warping her by education
and custom; for if the majority of mankind
took no delight in being praised, there could be n<unclear>o</unclear>
modesty in refusing to hear it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of manners and good breeding, moving from the general psychological motivations behind modesty to specific social examples of self-denial and the "fashionable deceit" that facilitates polite society.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>Remark (C.)</header>

The man of manners does not pick the best, but rather takes the worst from the dish, and takes a portion of everything (unless it is forced upon him), always choosing the most ordinary share. By this civility, the best remains for others, and since this is a compliment to everyone present, everybody is pleased with it. The more they love themselves, the more they are forced to approve of his behavior; and as gratitude sets in, they are obliged—almost whether they want to or not—to think favorably of him. In this way, the well-bred man insinuates himself into the esteem of every company he enters; and if he gains nothing else by it, the pleasure he receives from reflecting on the applause he knows is secretly given to him is, to a proud man, more than an equivalent for his previous self-denial, and repays his self-love with interest for the loss it sustained through his politeness to others.

If there are seven or eight apples or peaches among six formal people who are of nearly equal rank, the one who is persuaded to choose first will take that which—if there is any significant difference—a child would know to be the worst. He does this to imply that he considers those he is with to be of superior merit, and that there is no one for whom he does not wish better things than he does for himself. It is custom and general practice that make this fashionable deceit familiar to us without us being shocked by its absurdity; for if people had been accustomed to speaking from the sincerity of their hearts and acting according to the natural sentiments they felt within until they were twenty-three or twenty-four, it would be impossible for them to participate in this comedy of manners without either loud laughter or indignation. And yet, it is certain that such behavior makes us more tolerable to one another than we could be otherwise.

It is very advantageous to our self-knowledge to be able to distinguish well between

<header>Remark (C.)</header>

...good qualities and virtues. The social bond requires from every member a certain regard for others, which even the highest-ranking person is not exempt from in the presence of the lowest, even in an empire. However, when we are by ourselves, and so far removed from company as to be beyond the reach of their senses, the words modesty and impudence lose their meaning. A person may be wicked, but he cannot be immodest while he is alone; and no thought can be impudent if it was never communicated to another. A man of extreme pride may hide it so well that no one shall be able to discover that he has any, and yet he may receive greater satisfaction from that passion than another who indulges himself by declaring it before the whole world. Good manners have nothing to do with virtue or religion; instead of extinguishing the passions, they actually inflame them. The man of sense and education never triumphs more in his pride than when he hides it with the greatest skill. In feasting on the applause which he is sure all good judges will give to his behavior, he enjoys a pleasure completely unknown to the short-sighted, surly alderman who shows his haughtiness clearly on his face, tips his hat to no one, and hardly deigns to speak to an inferior.

A man may carefully avoid everything that the world considers a result of pride without suppressing his own feelings or making the least conquest over his passion. It is possible that he only sacrifices the dull, outward part of his pride—which only foolish and ignorant people take delight in—for the sake of that part we all feel within, which men of the highest spirit and greatest genius feed on with such ecstasy in silence. The pride of great and polite men is nowhere *more obvious than in the debates about ceremony and precedence, where they have an opportunity to give their vices the appearance of*

### Page 4. Line 29.

The vast esteem we have for ourselves and the small value we have for others make us all very unfair judges in our own cases. Few men can be persuaded that they gain too much from those they sell to, however extraordinary their gains are, while at the same time there is hardly any profit so small that they will not grudge it to those they buy from. For this reason, since the smallness of the seller’s advantage is the greatest persuasion for the buyer, tradesmen are generally forced to tell lies in their own defense and invent a thousand improbable stories rather than reveal what they actually make on their goods. This being a general practice which nobody can be ignorant of who has ever seen any gambling, there must be something in the nature of man that is the cause of it: But as searching into this will seem very trifling to many, I desire the reader to skip this remark, unless he be in a perfect good humor, and has nothing at all to do.

That gamblers generally endeavor to conceal their gains before the losers seems to me to proceed from a mixture of gratitude, pity, and self-preservation. All men are naturally grateful while they receive a benefit, and what they say or do, while it affects them and feels warm to them, is real and comes from the heart; but when that is over, the returns we make generally proceed from virtue, good manners, reason, and the thought of duty, but not from gratitude, which is a motive of the inclination. ...increases the Customs, and is in many ways beneficial to the public; yet it cannot be denied that its greatest dependence is on *lavishness* and *drunkenness*. The same may be said not only of card and dice makers, who are the immediate ministers to a legion of vices, but also of mercers, upholsterers, tailors, and many others, who would be starved within six months if *pride* and *luxury* were suddenly banished from the nation.

### Page 5. Line 33.

I know this will seem like a strange paradox to many, and I shall be asked what benefit the public receives from thieves and burglars. They are, I admit, very harmful to human society, and every government ought to take all possible care to root out and destroy them; yet if all people were strictly honest, and nobody would meddle with or pry into anything but their own, half the smiths in the nation would lack employment. Furthermore, a great deal of workmanship (which now serves for ornament as well as defense) is to be seen everywhere in both town and country that would never have been conceived of except to secure us against the attempts of pilferers and robbers.

<meta>This page continues the argument from Remark (G), specifically addressing the paradoxical ways in which "the worst of all the multitude" still contributes to the common good through consumption and the circulation of wealth.</meta>

# Remark (G.)

If what I have said is thought far-fetched, and my assertion still seems like a paradox, I desire the reader to look at the consumption of things. He will find that the laziest and most inactive, the profligate and most mischievous, are all forced to do something for the common good. As long as their mouths are not sewn up, and they continue to wear and otherwise destroy what the industrious are daily employed to make, fetch, and procure, they are—in spite of themselves—obliged to help maintain the poor and the public expenses. The labor of millions would soon come to an end if there were not other millions, as I say in the Fable,

->Employed,<-
->To see their handiworks destroyed.<-

But men are not to be judged by the consequences that may follow their actions, but by the facts themselves and the motives from which it appears they acted. If an ill-natured miser, who is almost a <term>Plumb</term> <note>A slang term for someone worth £100,000.</note> and spends only fifty pounds a year—even though he has no relative to inherit his wealth—should be robbed of five hundred or a thousand guineas, it is certain that as soon as this money begins to circulate, the nation would be the better for the robbery. It would receive the same and as real a benefit from it as if an archbishop had left the same sum to the public. Yet justice and the peace of society require that the person or persons who robbed the miser should be hanged, even if half a dozen of them were involved.

Thieves and pickpockets steal for a livelihood; either what they can get honestly is not sufficient to support them, or else they have an aversion to constant work. They want to gratify their vices, have food, strong drink, lewd women, and to be idle when they please. The victualer <note>An innkeeper or tavern owner licensed to sell food and drink.</note> who entertains them and takes their money, knowing how they came by it, is very

They each received over eighty pounds, which freed every one of them from the difficulties they labored under, and made them, in their opinion, the happiest people in the world.

Nothing is more destructive, either in regard to the health or the vigilance and industry of the poor, than that infamous liquor, the name of which—derived from juniper in *Dutch*—is now, through frequent use and the laconic spirit of the nation, shrunk from a word of medium length into a monosyllable: intoxicating **Gin**. It charms the inactive, the desperate, and the frail of either sex, and makes the starving drunkard behold his rags and nakedness with stupid indifference, or mock both with senseless laughter and even more insipid jokes. # Remark (G.)

A cause of murder. It has broken and destroyed the strongest constitutions, thrown them into consumptions, and been the fatal and immediate cause of apoplexies, frenzies, and sudden death. But as these latter mischiefs happen but seldom, they might be overlooked and tolerated; however, this cannot be said of the many diseases that are commonly associated with the liquor, and which are daily and hourly produced by it: such as loss of appetite, fevers, black and yellow jaundice, convulsions, kidney stones and gravel, dropsies, and leucophlegmacies <note>An archaic term for a condition involving pale, flabby swelling of the body, often associated with edema.</note>.

Among the doting admirers of this liquid poison, many of the lowest rank—out of a sincere affection for the commodity itself—become dealers in it. They take delight in helping others to what they love themselves, just as *prostitutes* become *madams* to make the profits of one trade support the pleasures of the other. But as these starvelings commonly drink more than their gains, they seldom improve the wretchedness of the condition they labored under while they were only buyers. In the slums and outskirts of the town, and in all places of the vilest reputation, it is sold in some part of almost every house—frequently in cellars, and sometimes in the attic. The petty traders in this *Stygian* comfort are supplied by others in a somewhat higher station who keep professional brandy shops and are as little to be envied as the former. Among the middle class, I know of no more miserable way to make a living than their profession; whoever wishes to thrive in it must, in the first place, be of a watchful and suspicious, as well as a bold and resolute temperament, so that he may not be cheated by swindlers and con artists, nor bullied by the oaths and curses of hackney-coachmen and foot-soldiers. *Secondly, he ought to be a dabbler in crude jokes and loud laughter, and have all the winning ways to allure customers and draw out their mo-*

# Remark (G.)

With patience and good humor, he must be able to endure the filthy actions and viler language of nasty harlots and the lewdest scoundrels, and without a frown or the least aversion, bear with all the stench and squalor, noise and impertinence that the utmost poverty, laziness, and drunkenness can produce in the most shameless and abandoned *common people*.

The vast number of the shops I speak of throughout the city and suburbs is astonishing evidence of the many seducers who, in a lawful occupation, are accessory to the introduction and increase of all the sloth, drunkenness, want, and misery which the abuse of *spirits* is the immediate cause of. This trade lifts perhaps half a score of men who deal in the same commodity by wholesale above mediocrity, while among the retailers—though qualified as I required—a much greater number are broken and ruined for not abstaining from the *Circean cup* <note>A reference to the enchantress Circe from Greek mythology, who used a magic cup to turn men into beasts.</note> they hold out to others. Even the more fortunate among them are obliged for their whole lifetime to take uncommon pains, endure hardships, and swallow all the ungrateful and shocking things I named for little or nothing beyond a bare sustenance and their daily bread.

The short-sighted *common people* in the chain of causes can seldom see further than one link; but those who can enlarge their view and will give themselves the leisure of gazing on the prospect of linked events may, in a hundred places, see good spring up and sprout from evil as naturally as chickens do from eggs. Nothing was more instrumental in forwarding the Reformation than the sloth and stupidity of the *Roman* clergy; yet the same Reformation has roused them from the laziness and ignorance they then labored under; and the followers of *Luther, Calvin,* and others, may be said to have reformed not only those whom they drew into their sentiment, but likewise those who remained their greatest opposers. The clergy of *England*, by being severe upon the *schismatics* and upbraiding them with a lack of learning, have raised for themselves such formidable enemies as are not easily answered; and again, the *dissenters*, by prying into the lives and diligently watching all the actions of their powerful antagonists, render those of the Established Church more cautious of giving offense than in all probability they would be if they had no malicious onlookers to fear. For it is not only that women in general would meet with far greater temptations, and attempts to ensnare the innocence of virgins would seem more excusable—even to the sober part of mankind—than they do now; but some men would grow outrageous, and rape would become a common crime. Where six or seven thousand sailors arrive at once, as often happens at *Amsterdam*, who have seen none but their own sex for many months together, how is it to be supposed that honest women could walk the streets unmolested if there were no prostitutes to be had at reasonable prices? For this reason, the wise rulers of that well-ordered city always tolerate a certain number of houses where women are hired as publicly as horses at a livery stable; and since there is a great deal of prudence and economy <note>management or administration of resources</note> to be seen in this toleration, a short account of it will be no tiresome digression.

In the first place, the houses I speak of are allowed to be nowhere but in the most slovenly and unpolished part of the town, where seamen and strangers of no reputation chiefly lodge and gather. The street in which most of them stand is considered scandalous, and the infamy extends to the entire neighborhood around it. Secondly, they are only places to meet and bargain in, to make appointments for more secret interviews, and no kind of lewdness is ever allowed to be transacted within them. This rule is so strictly observed that, aside from the bad manners and noise of the people who frequent them, you will meet with no more indecency—and generally less lustfulness—there than is seen at a playhouse in our country. Secondly, as on several accounts it might be dangerous to let the multitude into the secret that those houses and the trade that is driven in them are connived at, so by this means appearing unblamable, the wary magistrates preserve themselves in the good opinion of the weaker sort of people, who imagine that the government is always endeavoring, though unable, to suppress what it actually tolerates: whereas if they had a mind to root them out, their power in the administration of justice is so supreme and extensive, and they know so well how to have it executed, that one week, nay one night, might send them all packing.

In *Italy* the toleration of strumpets is yet more barefaced, as is evident from their public brothels. At *Venice* and *Naples* impurity is a kind of merchandise and traffic; the *courtesans* at *Rome*, and the *Cantoneras* in *Spain*, compose a body in the state, and are under a legal tax and impost. It is well known, that the reason why so many good politicians as these tolerate lewd houses, is not their lack of religion, but to prevent a worse evil, an impurity of a more execrable kind, and to provide for the safety of women of honor. *About two hundred and fifty years ago, says Monsieur de St. Didier, Venice being in want of courtesans, the Republic was obliged to procure a great number from foreign parts.* *Doglioni*, who has written the memorable *Affairs of Venice*, highly extols the wisdom of the Republic in this point, which secured the chastity of women of honor daily exposed to public violence, the churches and consecrated places not being a sufficient asylum for their chastity.

Our universities in England are much belied if in some colleges there was not a monthly allowance *ad expurgandos Renes* <note>Latin: "to purge the kidneys"—a medical euphemism used at the time for sexual discharge</note>; and there was a time when the monks and priests in Germany were allowed concubines on paying a certain yearly duty to the prelate. <header>Remark (I.)</header>

(to whom I owe the last paragraph) that <term>avarice</term> was the cause of this shameful indulgence; but it is more probable their design was to prevent them from tempting modest women, and to quiet the uneasiness of husbands, whose resentment the clergy do well to avoid. From what has been said, it is clear that there is a necessity of sacrificing one part of womankind to preserve the other, and to prevent a filthiness of a more heinous nature. From this, I think I may justly conclude (the seeming paradox I set out to prove) that chastity may be supported by incontinence, and the best of virtues requires the assistance of the worst of vices.

> (I.) *The root of evil, avarice,*
> *That damned, ill-natured, baneful vice,*
> *Was slave to prodigality.*

->Page 6. Line 5.<-

I have joined so many hateful epithets to the word <term>avarice</term>, in compliance with the fashion of mankind, who generally bestow more ill language upon this than upon any other vice; and indeed not undeservedly, for there is hardly a mischief to be named which it has not produced at one time or other. But the true reason why everyone exclaims so much against it is that almost everyone suffers by it; for the more money is hoarded up by some, the scarcer it must grow among the rest, and therefore when men rail very much at misers, there is generally self-interest at the bottom.

As there is no living without money, so those who are unprovided, and have nobody to give it to them, are obliged to do some service or other to society before they can obtain it; but every

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (I), discussing the social perception of avarice and its relationship with prodigality, illustrating how the hoarding of the miser provides the necessary resources for the spender.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<header>Remark (I.)</header>
<meta>catchword: any.</meta>

person valuing his labor as he does himself—which is generally not at a low value—most people who want money only to spend it again immediately imagine they do more for it than it is worth. Men cannot help looking upon the necessities of life as their due, whether they work or not; because they find that Nature, without consulting whether they have food or not, tells them to eat whenever they are hungry. For this reason, everyone endeavors to get what he wants with as much ease as he can; and therefore, when men find that the trouble they are put to in getting money is either more or less depending on whether those they seek it from are more or less stingy, it is very natural for them to be angry at <term>covetousness</term> in general; for it obliges them either to go without what they need, or else to take greater pains for it than they are willing.

<term>Avarice</term>, notwithstanding it is the cause of so many evils, is yet very necessary to society, to glean and gather what has been dropped and scattered by the contrary vice. Were it not for avarice, <term>spendthrifts</term> would soon lack resources; and if no one would save and acquire money faster than they spend it, very few could spend faster than they earn. That it is a "slave to <term>prodigality</term>," as I have called it, is evident from the many <term>misers</term> we daily see who toil and labor, pinch pennies and starve themselves to enrich a lavish heir.

Though these two vices appear very opposite, yet they often assist each other. *Florio* is an extravagant young fellow of a very profuse temperament; as he is the only son of a very rich father, he wants to live high, keep horses and dogs, and throw his money about, as he sees some of his companions do: but the old <term>hunks</term> <note>a stingy, crusty, or miserly man</note> will part with no money and hardly allows him the necessities. *Florio* would have borrowed money upon his own credit long ago; but as all would be lost if he died before his father, no prudent man would lend him

<summary>The author explains that people resent avarice because it makes acquiring money more difficult, yet he argues that the miser's hoarding is a necessary social function that accumulates the wealth eventually dispersed by spendthrifts. This relationship is illustrated by Florio, an extravagant son whose desires are frustrated by his miserly father.</summary>
<keywords>avarice, prodigality, spendthrifts, misers, social utility, wealth accumulation, Florio, covetousness, labor value</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (I), concluding the example of Florio and the usurer Cornaro to illustrate the symbiotic relationship between avarice and prodigality before transitioning to Remark (K).</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>Remark (K.)</header>

7. At last he has met with the greedy *Cornaro*, who lets him have money at *thirty percent*, and now *Florio* thinks himself happy and spends a thousand a year. Where would *Cornaro* ever have gotten such a prodigious interest, if it were not for such a fool as *Florio*, who will pay such a great price for money just to throw it away? And how would *Florio* find money to spend if he had not come across such a greedy usurer as *Cornaro*, whose excessive covetousness makes him overlook the great risk he runs in venturing such large sums on the life of a wild debauchee.

Avarice is no longer the opposite of profuseness than when it signifies that sordid love of money and narrowness of soul that hinders misers from parting with what they have and makes them desire only to hoard it up. But there is a sort of avarice which consists of a greedy desire for riches in order to spend them, and this often meets with prodigality in the same persons, as is evident in most courtiers and high-ranking officials, both civil and military. In their buildings and furniture, carriages and entertainments, their gallantry is displayed with the greatest profusion; while the base actions they submit to for profit, and the many frauds and impositions they are guilty of, reveal the utmost avarice. This mixture of contrary vices matches exactly the character of *Catiline*, of whom it is said that he was *appetens alieni & sui profusus*—greedy for the goods of others and lavish with his own.

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (K), distinguishing between the avaricious prodigality described at the end of the previous page and a more "noble" form of the vice that benefits society.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>Remark (K.)</header>

makes Men unreasonably profuse to some as they unjustly extort from others, but that amiable good-natured Vice that makes the Chimney smoke, and all the Tradesmen smile; I mean the unmixed Prodigality of heedless and Voluptuous Men, that being educated in Plenty, abhor the vile Thoughts of Lucre, and lavish away only what others took pains to scrape together; such as indulge their Inclinations at their own Expense, have the continual Satisfaction of bartering Gold for new Pleasures, and from the extreme largeness of a diffusive Soul, are made guilty of slighting too much what most People over-value.

When I speak thus honorably of this Vice, and treat it with so much Tenderness and good Manners as I do, I have the same thing at Heart that made me give so many Ill Names to the Reverse of it, *namely*, the Interest of the Public; for as the Avaricious does no good to himself, and is injurious to all the World besides, except his Heir, so the Prodigal is a Blessing to the whole Society, and injures nobody but himself. It is true, that as many of the first are Knaves, so the latter are all fools; yet they are delicious Morsels for the Public to feast on, and may with as much Justice as the <unclear>Gascoyns</unclear> call the Monks the Partridges of the Women, be styled the Woodcocks of the Society. Were it not for Prodigality, nothing could make us amends for the Rapine and Extortion of Avarice in leadership. When a Covetous Statesman is gone, who spent his whole Life in fattening himself with the Spoils of the Nation, and had by pinching and plundering heaped up an immense Treasure, it ought to rejoice every good Member of the Society, with Joy to behold the uncommon Profuseness of his Son who is refunding to the Public what was robbed from it. Resuming of Grants is a barbarous way of stripping; and it is ignoble to ruin a Man sooner than he does it himself, when he sets about it.

<summary>The author argues that pure prodigality, unlike the avarice-driven kind, benefits society by circulating wealth and supporting tradesmen, serving as a necessary corrective to the hoarding of greedy statesmen. He views the reckless spender as a "blessing" to the public because they return stolen or hoarded treasures back into the economy.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphorical argument that vice (avarice and prodigality) functions like the contrasting ingredients in a punch bowl to create a balanced and functional society.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>Remark (K.)</header>

Authors are always allowed to compare small things to great ones, especially if they ask permission first—*Si licet exemplis, etc.*—but to compare great things to mean, trivial ones is unbearable, unless it is for parody; otherwise, I would compare the body politic (I admit the simile is very low) to a bowl of punch. Avarice would be the sourness and prodigality the sweetness of it. The water I would call the ignorance, folly, and gullibility of the drifting, insipid multitude; while wisdom, honor, fortitude, and the rest of the sublime qualities of men—which, separated by art from the dregs of nature, the fire of glory has exalted and refined into a spiritual essence—would be equivalent to brandy. I don't doubt that a *Westphalian*, *Laplander*, or any other dull stranger who is unacquainted with the wholesome composition, if he were to taste the several ingredients separately, would think it impossible for them to make any tolerable liquor. The lemons would be too sour, the sugar too cloying, the brandy he will say is too strong ever to be drunk in any quantity, and the water he will call a tasteless liquid only fit for cows and horses: Yet experience teaches us that the ingredients I named, when judiciously mixed, will make an excellent liquor, liked and admired by men of exquisite palates.

As to our two vices in particular, I could compare avarice, which causes so much mischief and is complained of by everyone who is not a miser, to a gripping acid that sets our teeth on edge and is unpleasant to every palate that is not corrupted. I could compare the gaudy trimmings and splendid gear of a wasteful beau to the glistening brightness of the finest loaf sugar; for as the one, by correcting the sharpness, prevents the injuries which a gnawing sourness might do to the bowels, so the other is a pleasing balsam that heals and makes amends for the pain which the multitude
->always<-

<summary>The author uses a "bowl of punch" metaphor to argue that individual vices like avarice and prodigality, though unpleasant on their own, are necessary ingredients that balance each other to create a functional society. He compares the masses to water and the higher virtues to the refined spirit of brandy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the metaphor of the punch bowl, specifically comparing the interplay of avarice and prodigality to the balancing of opposite poisons in medicine. It then transitions into Remark (L), which discusses the definition of luxury.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>Remark (L.)</header>
<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: to</meta>

always suffers from the griping pains of the avaricious; while the substances of both melt away alike, and they consume themselves by being beneficial to the various compositions they belong to. I could carry on the simile regarding proportions and the exact precision to be observed in them, which would make it appear how little any of the ingredients could be spared in either of the mixtures. But I will not tire my reader by pursuing a ludicrous comparison too far, when I have other matters of greater importance to entertain him with; and to sum up what I have said in this and the preceding remark, I shall only add that I look upon avarice and prodigality in society as I do upon two contrary poisons in medicine, of which it is certain that their harmful qualities, being corrected by the mutual mischief in both, may assist each other and often make a good medicine between them.

If everything is to be considered luxury (as in strictness it ought to be) that is not immediately necessary to make man subsist as a living creature, there is nothing else to be found in the world—no, not even among the naked savages—of whom it is not probable that there are any who by this time have not made some improvements upon their former manner of living; and either in the preparation of their food, the arrangement of their huts, or otherwise, added something to what once sufficed them. <meta>This page continues the discussion from Remark (L), which argues that if luxury is not strictly defined as everything beyond what is necessary for survival, the concept becomes entirely subjective and impossible to define.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>Remark (L.)</header>

to abate one inch of this severity, I am afraid we shall not know where to stop. When people tell us they only desire to keep themselves "sweet and clean," there is no understanding what they aim at; if they used those words in their genuine, proper, literal sense, they might soon be satisfied without much cost or trouble, provided they did not lack water. But these two little adjectives are so comprehensive, especially in the dialect of some ladies, that nobody can guess how far they may be stretched. The comforts of life are likewise so various and extensive that nobody can tell what people mean by them, unless he knows what sort of life they lead. I observe the same obscurity in the words *decency* and *convenience*, and I never understand them unless I am acquainted with the rank of the persons who use them. People may go to church together and be as much of one mind as they please, but I am inclined to believe that when they pray for their "daily bread," the bishop includes several things in that petition which the sexton does not think of.

By what I have said so far, I only wish to show that if we once depart from calling everything "luxury" that is not absolutely necessary to keep a man alive, then there is no luxury at all; for if the wants of men are innumerable, then what ought to supply them has no bounds. What is called superfluous to one class of people will be thought requisite to those of higher quality; and neither the world nor the skill of man can produce anything so curious or extravagant that some "most Gracious Sovereign" or another—if it either eases or diverts him—will not count it among the necessaries of life; meaning not everyone’s life, but that of his sacred person.

It is a received notion that luxury is as destructive to the wealth of the whole body politic as <unclear>it is</unclear> to <unclear>that</unclear> of every individual person who is guilty of <unclear>it.</unclear>

He concludes that unless luxury is defined as anything beyond bare survival, the term loses all fixed meaning since human desires are infinite.</summary>

...and that a national frugality enriches a country in the same manner as a less general frugality increases the estates of private families. I confess that although I have found men with much better understanding than myself who hold this opinion, I cannot help dissenting from them on this point. They argue like this: "We send," they say, "for example to <unclear>Turkey</unclear>, woollen manufactures and other things of our own production worth a million every year; in exchange for this, we bring back silk, mohair, drugs, etc., to the value of twelve hundred thousand pounds, which are all consumed in our own country. By this," they say, "we gain nothing; but if most of us would be content with our own products, and so consume only half the quantity of these foreign commodities, then those in <unclear>Turkey</unclear>, who would still want the same quantity of our manufactures, would be forced to pay cash for the rest. Thus, by the balance of that trade alone, the nation would gain six hundred thousand pounds *per annum*."

To examine the strength of this argument, we will suppose (as they would have it) that only half the silk, etc., shall be consumed in England compared to what is consumed now. We will suppose likewise that those in Turkey—even though we refuse to buy more than half as much of their commodities as we used to—either cannot or will not do without the same quantity of our manufactures they had before, and that they will pay the balance in money. That is to say, they shall <unclear>give</unclear> us as much gold or silver as the value of what they buy from us exceeds the value of what we buy from them. Though what we suppose might perhaps be done for one year, it is impossible that it should last. Buying is bartering, and no nation <unclear>can</unclear> buy goods from others if it has none of its own to purchase them with. Spain and Portugal, which are annually supplied with new gold and silver from <unclear>their Mines</unclear>, may buy for cash forever as <unclear>long</unclear> as their yearly increase of gold or silver con-

Of this the *Dutch* and *French* will [reap] the benefit as much as ourselves; and if we continue to refuse taking their commodities in payment for our manufactures, they can trade no longer with us, but must content themselves [with] buying what they want from such nations as are willing to take what we refuse, even though their goods [are] much worse than ours; and thus our commerce [with] *Turkey* must in a few years be infallibly lost.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But they will say, perhaps, that to prevent the consequence I have shown, we shall take the Turkish merchandise as formerly, and only be so frugal as to consume but half the quantity of them ourselves, and send the rest abroad to be sold [to] others. --- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 76, which claims that if England were to reduce its consumption of Turkish imports like silk and mohair, it would inevitably lead to a decline in the export of English woollen goods, as the trade balance depends on mutual exchange.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>Remark (L.)</header>
<sig>E 3</sig>

Freight, insurance, provisions, and all other charges being deducted, merchants in general must lose much more on this half that is re-shipped than they gained from the half that is consumed here. For although the woollen manufactures are our own product, they cost the merchant who ships them to foreign countries just as much as they cost the shopkeeper here who retails them. Thus, if the returns for what he sends abroad do not repay him for what his goods cost him here—along with all other charges—until he has the money and a good interest on it in cash, the merchant must go bankrupt. The result would be that merchants in general, finding they lost money on the *Turkish* commodities they sent abroad, would ship no more of our manufactures than what would pay for as much silk, mohair, etc., as would be consumed here. Other nations would soon find ways to supply them with as much as we failed to provide, and find somewhere else to dispose of the goods we refused. So, all we would gain by this frugality would be that those in *Turkey* would take only half the quantity of our manufactures compared to what they do now, as long as we encourage and wear their merchandise, without which they are not able to purchase ours.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As I have had the frustration for several years of meeting many sensible people who opposed this opinion, and who always thought my calculation was wrong, I finally had the pleasure of seeing the wisdom of the nation adopt the same sentiments. This is clearly shown by an Act of Parliament passed in the year 1721, where the legislature goes against a powerful and valuable company and overlooks very significant domestic inconveniences to promote the interest of the *Turkey* trade. It not only encourages the consumption of silk and mohair, but forces the subjects, under penalty, to make use of them whether they want to or not.

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of luxury by addressing common criticisms. While page 77 focused on the specific trade balance with Turkey, this page shifts to a broader political and economic argument about the role of government in managing national wealth and luxury.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>Remark (L.)</header>

Furthermore, what is blamed on luxury is that it increases greed and plunder; and where these are the prevailing vices, offices of the greatest trust are bought and sold. The ministers who should serve the public, both high and low, are corrupted, and countries are at every moment in danger of being betrayed to the highest bidders. And lastly, it is argued that luxury makes the people weak and listless, so that nations become an easy prey for the first invaders. These are indeed terrible things; but what is attributed to luxury actually belongs to maladministration and is the fault of bad politics. Every government ought to be thoroughly acquainted with, and steadfastly pursue, the interest of the country. Good politicians, through skillful management—placing heavy taxes on some goods or totally prohibiting them, and lowering the duties on others—may always turn and divert the course of trade in whichever direction they please. Just as they will always prefer (if it is of equal importance) commerce with countries that can pay with money as well as goods over those that can only pay for what they buy with their own local commodities and manufactures, so they will always carefully prevent trade with nations that refuse the goods of others and will accept nothing but money for their own. But above all, they will keep a watchful eye over the balance of trade in general, and never allow the total value of all foreign commodities imported in one year to exceed the value of their own local growth or manufacture exported to others in the same year. <note>Note that I am speaking now of the interests of those nations that have no gold or silver of their own production; otherwise, this maxim would not need to be insisted upon so much.</note>

¶ If what I last urged is diligently looked after, and imports are never allowed to be greater than exports, no nation can ever be impoverished

<summary>The author argues that the social ills often blamed on luxury, such as corruption and national weakness, are actually the result of poor government management. He asserts that a well-run state must skillfully manage trade duties and maintain a positive balance of trade to ensure national prosperity.</summary>
<keywords>luxury, corruption, maladministration, balance of trade, imports, exports, mercantilism, political economy, duties, government management</keywords>

Trade is the principal, but not the only requirement to make a nation great: there are other things to be taken care of besides. The <term>Meum and Tuum</term> must be secured, crimes punished, and all other laws concerning the administration of justice wisely devised and strictly executed. Foreign affairs must likewise be prudently managed, and the ministry of every nation ought to have good intelligence abroad and be well acquainted with the public transactions of all those countries that, either by their neighborhood, strength, or interest, may be harmful or beneficial to them, in order to take the necessary measures accordingly—opposing some and assisting others, as policy and the balance of power dictate. The multitude must be kept in awe, no man's conscience forced, and the clergy allowed no greater share in state affairs than our Savior has bequeathed them in his Testament.

These are the arts that lead to worldly greatness; whatever sovereign power makes good use of them while governing any significant nation—whether it be a monarchy, a commonwealth, or a mixture of both—can never fail to make it flourish despite all the other powers on Earth, and no luxury or other vice is ever able to shake their constitution.

—But here I expect a loud outcry against me: What! Has <unclear>God</unclear> never punished and destroyed great nations <unclear>for</unclear> their sins? Yes, but not without means: by <unclear>in</unclear>fatuating their governors and allowing them to <unclear>de</unclear>part from either all or some of those general <unclear>ma</unclear>xims I have mentioned. And of all the famous <unclear>st</unclear>ates and empires the world has had to boast of <unclear>hi</unclear>therto, none ever came to ruin whose destructi<unclear>on</unclear> was not principally owing to the bad politics, <unclear>ne</unclear>glect, or mismanagement of the rulers.

There is no doubt that more health and vigor are to be expected among a people and their offspring from temperance and sobriety than from gluttony and drunkenness; yet I confess that, regarding luxury's tendency to make a nation effeminate and enervated, I do not have such frightful notions now as I had formerly. When we hear or read of things to which we are complete strangers, they commonly bring to our imagination such ideas of what we have seen as (according to our understanding) must come the nearest to them. And I remember that when I read of the luxury of *Persia*, *Egypt*, and other countries where it has been a reigning vice and which were made effeminate and enervated by it, it sometimes reminded me of the cramming and swilling of ordinary tradesmen at a city feast and the beastliness that often attends their over-gorging themselves. At other times, it has made me think of the distraction of dissolute sailors, as I had seen them in the company of half a dozen lewd women, roaring along with fiddles playing before them. Had I been carried into any of their great cities, I would have expected to find one-third of the people sick in bed with surfeits; <note>Excessive indulgence in food or drink.</note> another third laid up with the gout or crippled by a more ignominious distemper; <note>Likely referring to venereal disease.</note> and the rest, who could walk without being led, walking along the streets in petticoats.

It is fortunate for us to have fear as a keeper as long as our reason is not strong enough to govern our appetites. I believe that the great dread I had, particularly of the word *to enervate*, and some resulting thoughts on its etymology, did me a great deal of good when I was a schoolboy. But since I have seen something of the world, the consequences of luxury to a nation do not seem as dreadful to me as they once did. As long as men have the same appetites, the same vices will remain. In all large societies, some will

<meta>catchword: compos'd</meta>

...love whoring and others drinking. The lustful who cannot get handsome, clean women will content themselves with dirty drabs; and those who cannot purchase true *Hermitage* or *Pontack* <note>Types of high-quality wine; Hermitage is from the Rhone region and Pontack (Pontac) refers to a celebrated Bordeaux wine.</note> will be glad of more ordinary French claret. Those who cannot reach wine take up with worse liquors, and a foot soldier or a beggar may make himself as drunk with stale beer or malt spirits as a lord with *Burgundy*, *Champagne*, or *Tokay*. The cheapest and most slovenly way of indulging our passions does as much mischief to a person's constitution as the most elegant and expensive.

The greatest excesses of luxury are shown in buildings, furniture, equipages <note>A carriage with its horses and attendants.</note>, and clothes. Clean linen weakens a man no more than flannel; tapestry, fine painting, or good wainscot are no more unhealthy than bare walls; and a rich coach or a gilt chariot are no more enervating than the cold floor or a country cart. The refined pleasures of men of sense are seldom injurious to their constitution, and there are many great epicures who will refuse to eat or drink more than their heads or stomachs can bear. Sensual people may take as great care of themselves as any; and the errors of the most viciously luxurious do not consist so much in the frequent repetitions of their lewdness and their eating and drinking too much (which are the things that would most enervate them) as they do in the laborious contrivances, the profuseness, and the nicety with which they are served, and the vast expense they incur at their tables and in their amours.

But let us once suppose that the ease and pleasures the grandees and the rich people of every great nation live in render them unfit to endure hardships and undergo the toils of war. The hardships and fatigues of war that are personally suffered fall upon those who bear the brunt of everything: the lowest <term>indigent</term> part of the nation, the working, slaving people. For however excessive the plenty and luxury of a nation may be, someone must do the work. Houses and ships must be built, merchandise must be moved, and the ground tilled. Such a variety of labors in every great nation requires a vast multitude, in which there are always enough loose, idle, and extravagant fellows to spare for an army; and those who are robust enough to hedge and ditch, plow and thresh—or else are not too weakened to be smiths, carpenters, sawyers, cloth-workers, porters, or <term>Carmen</term>—will always be strong and hardy enough in a campaign or two to make good soldiers. Where good orders are kept, these men seldom have so much plenty and excess fall to their share as to do them any harm.

The mischief then to be feared from luxury among military personnel cannot extend beyond the officers. The greatest of them are either men of very high birth and princely education, or else possess extraordinary talents and no less experience. Whoever is chosen by a wise government to command an army in chief should have a consummate knowledge of martial affairs, <term>intrepidity</term> to keep him calm in the midst of danger, and many other qualifications that must be the work of time and application in men of quick penetration, a distinguished genius, and a great sense of honor. Where good sense is fashionable and genteel behavior is esteemed, gluttony and drunkenness can be no reigning vices. What officers of distinction chiefly aim at is not a beastly but a splendid way of living; and the wishes of the most luxurious, in their several degrees of rank, are to appear handsome and excel each other in the finery of their equipage, the politeness of their entertainments, and the reputation of having a judicious taste in everything around them.

But if there should be more dissolute reprobates among officers than there are among men of other professions—which is not true—yet the most debauched of them may be very serviceable if they only have a great share of honor. It is this that covers and makes up for a multitude of defects in them, and it is this that no one (no matter how abandoned they are to pleasure) dares pretend to be without. But as there is no argument so convincing as a matter of fact, let us look back on what so lately happened in our two last wars with *France*. How many puny young striplings have we had in our armies, tenderly educated, meticulous in their dress, and fastidious in their diet, who underwent all manner of duties with gallantry and cheerfulness?

Those who have such dismal apprehensions of luxury enervating and effeminating people might, in *Flanders* and *Spain*, have seen embroidered beaux with fine laced shirts and powdered wigs stand as much fire and lead up to the mouth of a cannon with as little concern as it was possible for the most stinking slovens to have done in their own hair—even if it had not been combed in a month—and met with an abundance of wild rakes who had actually impaired their health and broken their con-

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>Remark (L.)</header>
<warning>Heavy ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page makes some sections, particularly the poem, difficult to read.</warning>

constitutions with excesses of wine and women, who yet behaved themselves with conduct and bravery against their enemies. Robustness is the least important thing required in an officer; and if strength is sometimes useful, a firm resolution of mind—which the hope of promotion, competition, and the love of glory inspire in them—will, in a crisis, take the place of physical force.

Those who understand their business and have a sufficient sense of honor, as soon as they are used to danger, will always be capable officers. And their luxury, as long as they spend no one's money but their own, will never be harmful to a nation.

By all this, I think I have proved what I intended in this Remark on luxury. First, that in one sense everything may be called luxury, and in another, there is no such thing. Second, that with a wise administration, all people may enjoy as much foreign luxury as their products can purchase without being impoverished by it. And lastly, that where military affairs are handled as they ought to be, and the soldiers are well paid and kept in good discipline, a wealthy nation may live in all the ease and plenty imaginable; and in many parts of it, show as much pomp and refinement as human wit can invent, while at the same time being formidable to its neighbors and matching the character of the bees in the fable, of which I said:

> Flattered in peace, and feared in wars,
> They were the esteem of foreigners,
> And lavish of their wealth and lives,
> The balance of all other hives.

See what is further said concerning luxury in Remarks (M.) and (Q.)

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>Remark. (M.)</header>
<sig><unclear>L h</unclear></sig>
<warning>Page has significant ink bleed-through and stains from the reverse side.</warning>

---

->(M.) *And odious Pride a Million more.*<-

->Page 6. Line 10.<-

PRIDE is that natural faculty by which every mortal who has any understanding overvalues himself and imagines better things of himself than any impartial judge, thoroughly acquainted with all his qualities and circumstances, could allow him. We possess no other quality so beneficial to society, and so necessary to render it wealthy and flourishing as this, yet it is the one most generally detested. What is very peculiar to this faculty of ours is that those who are most full of it are the least willing to tolerate it in others; whereas the wickedness of other vices is most downplayed by those who are guilty of them themselves. The chaste man hates fornication, and drunkenness is most abhorred by the temperate; but none are so offended by their neighbor’s pride as the proudest of all, and if anyone can pardon it, it is the most humble. From this, I think we may justly infer that its being hateful to the whole world is a certain sign that the whole world is troubled by it. All people of sense are ready to confess this, and nobody denies that he has pride in general. But if you come to specifics, you will meet few who will admit that any action you can name of theirs proceeded from that principle. There are likewise many who will allow that among the sinful nations of the present time, pride and luxury are the great promoters of trade; but they refuse to acknowledge the necessity that in a more virtuous age (one that would be free from pride), trade would, to a great extent, decay.

<summary>The author defines pride as a natural human tendency toward self-overvaluation and argues that while it is universally detested, it is a primary driver of economic prosperity and trade.</summary>
<keywords>Pride, Luxury, Trade, Society, Virtue, Natural Faculty, Bernard Mandeville, self-interest, economics</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (M), exploring the counter-argument that a nation could flourish through the consumption of goods and the employment of laborers without the influence of pride or luxury.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>
<warning>Significant ink bleed-through and dark staining obscure portions of the text, particularly in the lower half and left margin.</warning>

The Almighty, they say, has endowed us with the dominion over all things which the earth and sea produce or contain; there is nothing to be found in either but what was made for the use of man. His skill and industry, superior to other animals, were given to him so that he might render both them and everything else within the reach of his senses more serviceable to him. Upon this consideration, they think it impious to imagine that humility, temperance, and other virtues should debar people from the enjoyment of those comforts of life which are not denied to the most wicked nations. They conclude, therefore, that without pride or luxury, the same things might be eaten, worn, and consumed; the same number of handicrafts and artisans employed; and a nation be every way as flourishing as where those vices are most predominant.

As to wearing apparel in particular, they will tell you that pride, which sticks much closer to us than our clothes, is only lodged in the heart, and that rags often conceal a greater portion of it than the most pompous attire. They argue it cannot be denied that there have always been virtuous princes who, with humble hearts, have worn their splendid diadems and swayed their envied scepters, void of ambition and for the good of others. Thus, it is very probable that silver and gold brocades and the richest embroideries may, without a thought of pride, be worn by many whose rank and fortune are suitable to them. May not (they say) a good man of extraordinary revenues make every year a greater variety of suits than it is possible he should wear out, and yet have no other ends than to set the poor to work, to encourage trade, and by employing many, to promote the welfare of his country? And considering food and clothing to be necessities, and the two chief <unclear>Objects</unclear> to which all our worldly cares are extended—

<meta>This page continues Remark (M), following the argument that a wealthy man might purchase many suits of clothes without being motivated by pride, but rather to support trade and fulfill social duties.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>

...ed, why may not all mankind set aside a considerable part of their income for the one as well as the other, without the least tincture of pride? Nay, is not every member of society in a manner obliged, according to his ability, to contribute toward the maintenance of that branch of trade on which the whole has so great a dependence? Besides that, to appear decently is a civility, and often a duty, which, without any regard to ourselves, we owe to those we converse with.

These are the objections generally made use of by haughty moralists, who cannot endure to hear the dignity of their species arraigned; but if we look narrowly into them, they may soon be answered.

If we had no vices, I cannot see why any man should ever make more suits than he has occasion for, though he were ever so desirous of promoting the good of the nation. For though in the wearing of a well-wrought silk, rather than a light fabric, and the preferring of exquisite fine cloth to coarse, he had no other view but setting more people to work, and consequently the public welfare, yet he could consider clothes no otherwise than lovers of their country do taxes now; they may pay them with alacrity, but nobody gives more than his due; especially where all are justly rated according to their abilities, as it could no otherwise be expected in a very virtuous age. Besides that, in such golden times, nobody would dress above his condition, nobody would pinch his family, or cheat or overreach his neighbor to purchase finery, and consequently there would not be half the consumption, nor a third part of the people employed as there are now. But to make this more plain and demonstrate that, for the support of trade, there can be nothing equivalent to pride, I shall examine the several views men have in outward apparel, and set forth what daily experience may teach everybody as to dress.

<meta>This page continues Remark (M), which critiques the motivations behind human dress and the relationship between vanity, social status, and trade.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>
<warning>Heavy ink bleed-through and staining on the left side of the page obscures some text.</warning>

Clothes were originally made for two ends: to hide our nakedness, and to protect our bodies against the weather and other outward injuries. To these, our boundless pride has added a third, which is ornament; for what else but an excess of stupid vanity could have prevailed upon our reason to imagine that as ornamental which must continually remind us of our needs and misery, beyond all other animals that are naturally clothed by nature herself? It is indeed to be wondered at how so sensible a creature as man, who claims so many fine qualities of his own, should condescend to value himself upon what is stolen from so innocent and defenseless an animal as a sheep, or what he is indebted for to the most insignificant thing upon earth, a dying worm; yet while he is proud of such trifling depredations, he has the folly to laugh at the *Hottentots* <note>A historical, now offensive, term for the Khoikhoi people of Southern Africa.</note> on the furthest promontory of *Africa*, who adorn themselves with the guts of their dead enemies, without considering that those are the emblems of their valor with which those barbarians adorn themselves, the true *term*spolia opima*</term> <note>Latin: "rich spoils," specifically the arms and armor taken by a Roman general from an opposing commander killed in single combat.</note>, and that if their pride be more savage than ours, it is certainly less ridiculous, because they wear the spoils of a more noble animal.

But whatever reflections may be made on this subject, the world has long since decided the matter; handsome apparel is a main point, fine feathers make fine birds, and people, where they are not known, are generally honored according to their clothes and other accoutrements they have about them; from the richness of these we judge their wealth, and by their arrangement of them we guess at their understanding. It is this which engages everyone who is conscious of his little merit, if he is in any way able, to wear clothes above his rank, especially in large and populous cities, where obscure men may hourly meet with <unclear>thirty</unclear> strangers for every one acquaintance, and consequently

<summary>The author argues that while clothes were intended for modesty and protection, human pride has turned them into ornaments derived from animals; he further notes that society judges individuals based on their attire, leading people to dress above their social station.</summary>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (M), focusing on the psychological motivations behind wearing fine clothing, specifically how it allows individuals of lower social standing to enjoy the unearned respect of others and fulfill a "golden dream" of higher status.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>
<warning>Staining and ink bleed; darkening at the top and right margins.</warning>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...quently have the pleasure of being esteemed by a vast majority, not for what they are, but for what they appear to be; this is a greater temptation than most people need to become vain.

Whoever takes delight in viewing the various scenes of low life may, on Easter, Whitsun <note>Whitsuntide, the festival of Pentecost and the week following it.</note>, and other great holidays, meet with scores of people, especially women of almost the lowest rank, who wear good and fashionable clothes. If, in talking with them, you treat them more courteously and with greater respect than they are conscious they deserve, they will commonly be ashamed of admitting what they are; and often you may, if you are a little inquisitive, discover in them a most anxious care to conceal the business they follow and the places where they live. The reason is plain: while they receive those civilities that are not usually paid to them, and which they think are due only to their betters, they have the satisfaction of imagining that they appear to be what they wish they were. To weak minds, this is a pleasure almost as substantial as they could reap from the actual fulfillment of their wishes. They are so unwilling to be disturbed in this golden dream, and—being sure that the meanness of their condition <note>Their low social or economic status.</note>, if it were known, would lower them very much in your opinion—they cherish their disguise. They take every imaginable precaution not to lose, through a useless discovery, the esteem that they flatter themselves their good clothes have drawn from you.

Though everyone agrees that, regarding apparel and manner of living, we ought to behave in a way suitable to our conditions and follow the examples of the most sensible and prudent among our equals in rank and fortune; yet how few, who are not either miserably covetous or else proud of being unique, have this discretion to boast of? We all look above ourselves and, as fast as we can, strive to imitate those who are in some way or another superior to us.
