<summary>The author argues that people of low social standing use fashionable clothing to deceive others into treating them with the respect reserved for the upper classes. This vanity leads individuals to hide their true trades and statuses to maintain the "golden dream" of appearing superior to what they actually are.</summary>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>
<warning>blurry and dark at the left edge; some bleed-through from the reverse page</warning>

The poorest laborer's wife in the parish, who scorns to wear a strong, wholesome frieze <note>A coarse, napped woolen cloth.</note>, as she might, will half starve herself and her husband to purchase a second-hand gown and petticoat that cannot provide her half the service; because, forsooth, it is more genteel. The weaver, the shoemaker, the tailor, the barber, and every lowly working fellow who can start a business with little, has the impudence, with the first money he earns, to dress himself like a substantial tradesman. The ordinary retailer, in the clothing of his wife, takes his pattern from his neighbor who deals in the same commodity by wholesale; and the reason he gives for it is that twelve years ago, the other did not have a larger shop than himself. The druggist, mercer <note>A dealer in expensive fabrics, especially silks.</note>, draper, and other reputable shopkeepers can find no difference between themselves and merchants, and therefore dress and live like them. The merchant's lady, who cannot bear the arrogance of those mechanics <note>Manual laborers or tradespeople.</note>, flies for refuge to the other end of town and scorns to follow any fashion except what she takes from there. This haughtiness alarms the Court; the women of quality are frightened to see merchants' wives and daughters dressed like themselves. This impudence of the City <note>The commercial district of London, as opposed to the aristocratic West End.</note>, they cry, is intolerable. Mantua-makers <note>Dressmakers.</note> are sent for, and the invention of fashions becomes their entire study, so that they may always have new modes <note>Current styles or fashions.</note> ready to adopt as soon as those saucy "cits" <note>Short for "citizens," used disparagingly for townspeople or tradespeople.</note> shall begin to imitate those currently in use. The same emulation continues through the various degrees of social rank at an incredible expense, until at last the Prince's great favorites and those of the very highest rank, having nothing else left to outstrip their inferiors, are forced to spend vast fortunes on pompous equipages <note>A carriage with its horses and attendants.</note>, magnificent furniture, sumptuous gardens, and princely palaces.

To this emulation and continual striving to outdo one another, it is due that after so many various

<summary>This page describes how social emulation drives constant changes in fashion and luxury spending, as lower classes imitate the dress of their superiors, forcing the aristocracy to seek increasingly expensive ways to maintain their status.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (M), detailing how the constant evolution of fashion and the inherent vanity of various social ranks—even those who appear indifferent—drive economic industry and innovation.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>Remark (M.)</header>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...various shiftings and changings of modes, in trumping up new ones and renewing old ones, there is still a *plus ultra* <note>Latin for "further beyond."</note> left for the ingenious; it is this, or at least the consequence of it, that sets the poor to work, adds spurs to industry, and encourages the skillful artificer to search after further improvements.

It may be objected that many people of good fashion, who have been used to being well dressed, wear rich clothes out of custom with all the indifference imaginable, and that the benefit to trade accruing from them cannot be ascribed to emulation or pride. To this I answer, that it is impossible that those who trouble their heads so little with their dress could ever have worn those rich clothes if both the fabrics and fashions had not been first invented to gratify the vanity of others, who took greater delight in fine apparel than they did. Besides, not everyone is without pride who appears to be so; all the symptoms of that vice are not easily discovered; they are manifold, and vary according to the age, humor, circumstances, and often the constitution of the people.

The choleric City Captain <note>A member of the London trained bands or local militia, often a tradesman by profession.</note> seems impatient to come to action, and expressing his warlike genius by the firmness of his steps, makes his pike—for want of enemies—tremble at the valor of his arm. His martial finery, as he marches along, inspires him with an unusual elevation of mind, by which, endeavoring to forget his shop as well as himself, he looks up at the balconies with the fierceness of a Saracen conqueror. Meanwhile the phlegmatic alderman, having now become venerable for both his age and his authority, contents himself with being thought a significant man; and knowing no easier way to express his vanity, looks important in his coach where, being known by his paltry livery <note>The distinctive clothing worn by a person's servants.</note>, he receives, in sullen state, the homage that is paid him by the lower sort of people.

<summary>Mandeville argues that the constant cycle of changing fashions stimulates trade and provides work for the poor, asserting that even the seemingly indifferent benefit from a system driven by the vanity of others. He illustrates how different social figures, like the militia captain and the alderman, express their pride through their public displays and status symbols.</summary>

<keywords>fashion, industry, vanity, social class, City Captain, alderman, emulation, trade, luxury</keywords>

   And just as in those people pride is overlooked because it is industriously concealed, so in others it is denied that they have any at all, even when they show (or at least seem to show) it in the most public manner. The wealthy parson, being—like the rest of his profession—barred from the gaudy dress of laymen, makes it his business to seek out an admirable black and the finest cloth that money can buy, and distinguishes himself by the fullness of his noble and spotless garment. His wigs are as fashionable as the form he is forced to comply with will allow; but though he is limited in their shape, he takes care that for the quality of the hair and the color, few noblemen shall be able to match them. His body is always clean, as are his clothes; his sleek face is kept constantly shaved, and his handsome nails are diligently trimmed. His smooth white hand and a <term>brilliant</term> <note>A diamond or other gemstone of great sparkle and quality.</note> of the highest quality, both suiting each other, honor one another with double grace. Whatever linen he reveals is transparently fine, and he scorns ever to be seen in public with a worse <term>beaver</term> <note>A hat made of felted beaver fur, a high-status luxury item.</note> than what a rich banker would be proud of on his wedding day. To all these refinements in dress he adds a majestic gait and expresses a commanding loftiness in his bearing; yet common civility, despite the evidence of so many agreeing symptoms, will not allow us to suspect any of his actions to be the result of pride. ->(N.) *Envy itself, and Vanity,
Were Ministers of Industry.*<-

->Page 6. Line 11.<-

ENVY is that baseness in our nature which makes us grieve and pine at what we conceive to be a happiness in others. I do not believe there is a human creature in his senses, arrived at maturity, who at one time or another has not been carried away by this passion in good earnest; and yet I never met with anyone who dared admit he was guilty of it, except in jest. That we are so generally ashamed of this vice is owing to that strong habit of hypocrisy, by the help of which we have learned from our cradle to hide even from ourselves the vast extent of self-love and all its different branches. It is impossible that man should wish better for another than he does for himself, unless where he supposes it impossible that he himself should attain those wishes; and from this we may easily learn in what manner this passion is raised in us. In order to understand it, we are to consider first that, as well as we think of ourselves, we often think just as poorly of our neighbor with equal injustice; and when we perceive that others do or enjoy what we think they do not deserve, it offends and makes us angry with the cause of that dis-

<header>Remark (N.)</header>
<meta>Catchword: humorsome. Significant ink bleed-through from the verso page obscures the text on the right side of the page.</meta>
<warning>Ink bleed-through from the reverse side makes some words on the right edge difficult to read.</warning>

Among the unrefined and unpolished masses, this passion is very blatant, especially when they envy others for their wealth. They rail against their superiors, dig up their faults, and take pains to misinterpret their most praiseworthy actions. They grumble against Providence and loudly complain that the good things of this world are mostly enjoyed by those who do not deserve them. The more vulgar among them are often affected so violently that, if they were not restrained by fear of the laws, they would go directly and beat those at whom their envy is aimed, for no other reason than what that passion suggests to them.

Educated men suffering from this disorder display quite different symptoms. When they envy a person for his talents and learning, their chief care is to diligently conceal their weakness, which is generally attempted by denying and belittling the good qualities they envy. They carefully study his works and are displeased by every fine passage they encounter; they look for nothing but his errors and wish for no greater pleasure than a major mistake. In their criticisms, they are nitpicky as well as harsh; they make mountains out of molehills and will not pardon the least shadow of a fault, but exaggerate the most insignificant omission into a major blunder.

Envy is visible in animals; horses show it in their efforts to outstrip one another, and the most spirited will run themselves to death before they will allow another to get ahead of them. In dogs, this passion is also clearly seen: those who are accustomed to being petted will never quietly tolerate such happiness in others. I have seen a lap-dog that would choke himself with food rather than leave anything for a competitor of his own kind; and we may often observe the same behavior in those creatures that we daily see in infants who are difficult, and by being over-fondled are made hu-

   Envy, as it is very common among painters, so
it is of great use for their improvement: I do not
mean that minor dabblers envy great masters, but
most of them are tainted with this vice against
those immediately above them. If the pupil of a
famous artist has a bright genius and uncommon
application, he first adores his master; but as his
own skill increases, he begins imperceptibly to envy
what he admired before. To learn the nature of
this passion, and that it consists in what I have named,
we have only to observe that, if a painter by ex-
erting himself comes not only to equal, but to ex-
ceed the man he envied, his sorrow is gone and all
his anger is disarmed; and if he hated him before,
he is now glad to be friends with him, if the other
will condescend to it.
   Married women, who are guilty of this vice—
which few are not—are always endeavoring to raise
the same passion in their spouses; and where they
have prevailed, envy and emulation have kept
more men in bounds and reformed more bad hus-
bands from sloth, from drinking, and other evil
courses than all the sermons that have been
preached since the time of the Apostles.
   As everyone would be happy, enjoy pleasure,
and avoid pain if he could, so self-love bids us look
on every creature that seems satisfied as a rival
in happiness; and the satisfaction we have in see-
ing that felicity disturbed—without any advantage
to ourselves but what springs from the pleasure we
have in beholding it—is called loving mischief for
mischief’s sake; and the motive from which that
frailty results is malice, another offspring derived
from the same original; for if there were no envy,
there could be no malice. When the passion is
dormant we have no awareness of them, and
often people think they do not have such a frail<unclear>ty</unclear>

nature, because at that moment they are not with it.

A gentleman well dressed, who happens to be bespattered all over by a coach or a cart, is laughed at by his inferiors much more than by his equals, because they envy him more; they know he is above it, and imagining him to be happier than themselves, they are glad to see him meet with misfortunes in his turn. But a young lady, if she is in a serious mood, instead of laughing at him, pities him; because a clean man is a sight she takes delight in, and there is no room for envy. At disasters we either laugh or pity those they befall, according to the stock we possess of either malice or compassion. If a man falls or hurts himself so slightly that it does not move the latter, we laugh; otherwise, our pity and malice shake us alternately: "Dear sir, I am very sorry for it; I beg your pardon for laughing; I am the silliest creature in the world;" then laugh again; and again, "I am indeed very sorry," and so on. Some are so malicious they would laugh if a man broke his leg, and others are so compassionate that they can heartily pity a man for the least spot on his clothes; but nobody is so savage that no compassion can touch him, nor is anyone so good-natured as never to be affected by a sly, malicious pleasure. How strangely our passions govern us! We envy a man for being rich, and often perfectly hate him; but if we come to be his equals, we are calm, and the least condescension in him makes us friends; but if we become sensibly superior to him, we can pity his misfortune. The reason why men of true good sense are less prone than others is because they admire themselves with less hesitation than fools and silly people; for though they do not show this to others, yet the solidity of their thinking gives them an assurance of their real worth, which men of weak understanding can never feel within, though they often counterfeit it.

<vocab>Malice, Compassion, Envy, Pity, Passions, Reason, Worth, Sense</vocab>

<summary>This page explores how human reactions to others' misfortunes, such as laughter or pity, are dictated by the interplay of envy, malice, and compassion. It also suggests that individuals with true self-assurance are less likely to take malicious pleasure in the failures of others.</summary>
<keywords>psychology, envy, malice, compassion, social class, laughter, self-worth, human nature, emotions</keywords>

<header>Remark (N.)</header>
<warning>The bottom of the page is slightly blurred and damaged, affecting the legibility of the final lines.</warning>
<meta>Catchword: for</meta>

The Ostracism of the Greeks was a sacrifice of valuable men made to epidemic envy, and often applied as an infallible remedy to cure and prevent the mischiefs of popular spleen and rancor. A victim of state often appeases the murmurs of a whole nation, and later ages frequently wonder at barbarities of this nature, which under the same circumstances they would have committed themselves. They are compliments to the people's malice, which is never better gratified than when they can see a great man humbled. We believe that we love justice and to see merit rewarded; but if men continue long in the highest posts of honor, half of us grow weary of them, look for their faults, and if we can find none, we suppose they hide them; and it is quite likely that the majority of us wish them discarded. This foul play the best of men ought ever to expect from all who are not their immediate friends or acquaintances, because nothing is more tiresome to us than the repetition of praises in which we have no share.

The more a passion is a compound of many others, the more difficult it is to define; and the more tormenting it is to those who suffer under it, the greater the cruelty it is capable of inspiring in them against others. Therefore, nothing is more whimsical or mischievous than jealousy, which is made up of love, hope, fear, and a great deal of envy. The last has been sufficiently treated already, and what I have to say of fear, the reader will find under *Remark (R.)*. So, to better explain and illustrate this odd mixture, the ingredients I shall further speak of here are hope and love.

Hoping is wishing with some degree of confidence that the thing wished for will come to pass. *The strength and weakness of our hope depend entirely on the greater or lesser degree of our confidence, and all hope includes doubt; for*

<header>Remark (N.)</header>
<warning>Heavy ink bleed-through on the left side of the lower half of the page affects legibility of some words.</warning>

...our confidence has arrived at such a height that it excludes all doubts, it becomes a certainty, and we take for granted what we only hoped for before. A silver inkhorn may be acceptable in speech because everyone knows what we mean by it, but a "certain hope" cannot. For a man who makes use of an epithet that destroys the essence of the substantive he joins it to can have no meaning at all; and the more clearly we understand the force of the epithet and the nature of the substantive, the more obvious is the nonsense of the heterogeneous compound. Therefore, the reason why it is not as shocking to some to hear a man speak of "certain hope" as if he should talk of "hot ice" or "liquid oak," is not because there is less nonsense contained in the first than there is in either of the latter; but because the word "hope"—I mean the essence of it—is not as clearly understood by the generality of the people as the words and essences of ice and oak are.

Love, in the first place, signifies affection, such as parents and nurses feel for children, and friends for one another; it consists in a liking and well-wishing toward the person beloved. We interpret his words and actions favorably and feel a tendency to excuse and forgive his faults, if we see any; we make his interest our own in every respect, even to our own disadvantage, and receive an inward satisfaction from sympathizing with him in his sorrows as well as his joys. What I said last is not impossible, whatever it may seem to be; for when we are sincere in sharing another's misfortunes, self-love makes us believe that the suffering we feel must alleviate and lessen that of our friend; and while this fond reflection is soothing our pain, a secret pleasure arises from our grieving for the person we love.

Secondly, by love we understand a strong inclination, in its nature distinct from all other af-

...affections of friendship, gratitude, and consanguinity <note>blood relations or kinship</note> that persons of different sexes, after liking, bear to one another. It is in this sense that love enters into the compound of *jealousy*, and is the effect as well as the happy disguise of that passion that prompts us to labor for the preservation of our species. This latter appetite is innate in both men and women who are not defective in their formation, just as much as hunger or thirst, though they are seldom affected by it before the years of puberty. Could we undress nature and pry into her deepest recesses, we would discover the seeds of this passion before it exerts itself, as plainly as we see the teeth in an embryo before the gums are formed. There are few healthy people of either sex upon whom it has made no impression before twenty. Yet, as the peace and happiness of civil society require that this should be kept a secret, never to be talked of in public, so among well-bred people it is considered highly criminal to mention before company anything in plain words that relates to this mystery of succession <note>the generation of offspring or procreation</note>. By this means, the very name of the appetite, though the most necessary for the continuance of mankind, has become odious, and the proper epithets commonly joined to lust are *filthy* and *abominable*.

This impulse of nature in people of strict morals and rigid modesty often disturbs the body for a considerable time before it is understood or known to be what it is; and it is remarkable that the most polished and best instructed are generally the most ignorant regarding this affair. And here I can only observe the difference between man in the wild state of nature and the same creature in civil society. In the first, men and women, if left rude and untaught in the sciences of <unclear>modesty</unclear> and manners, would quickly find out the cause <unclear>of</unclear> that disturbance and be at no more of a loss <unclear>than</unclear> other animals for a present remedy. # Remark (N.)

Healthy and robust people of a bilious temperament and a sanguine complexion never entertain any love so spiritual as to exclude all thoughts and wishes that relate to the body. But if the most seraphic lovers would like to know the origin of their inclination, let them only imagine that another person should have the physical enjoyment of the person they love; by the tortures they suffer from that reflection, they will soon discover the nature of their passions. Whereas, on the contrary, parents and friends receive a satisfaction in reflecting on the joys and comforts of a happy marriage to be enjoyed by those they wish well.

The curious, who are skilled in analyzing the invisible part of man, will observe that the more sublime and exempt this love is from all thoughts of sensuality, the more false it is, and the more it deviates from its honest origin and primitive simplicity. The power and wisdom, as well as the labor and care, of the politician in civilizing society, has nowhere been more obvious than in the clever invention of playing our passions against one another. By flattering our pride and constantly increasing the good opinion we have of ourselves on the one hand, and inspiring us on the other with an intense dread and deadly hatred of shame, the artful moralists have taught us cheerfully to encounter ourselves—and if not subdue, at least so to conceal and disguise our favorite passion, lust, that we scarcely recognize it when we meet with it in our own breasts. Oh, the mighty prize we seek for all our self-denial! Can any man be so serious as to keep from laughing when he considers that for so much deceit and insincerity practiced upon ourselves as well as others, we have no other reward than the empty satisfaction of making our species appear more noble and distant from other animals than it really is, and than we in our consciences know it to be?

<summary>The author argues that all love, no matter how "spiritual" it claims to be, is fundamentally rooted in physical desire, which is proven by the pain of jealousy. He claims that moralists and politicians use pride and shame to trick humans into disguising their lustful nature so they can feel superior to other animals.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, love vs. lust, seraphic love, jealousy, social engineering, pride, shame, moralists, self-denial</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that what is called "love" is actually a socially conditioned version of lust, further exploring how this artificial construction leads to complex behaviors like jealousy.</meta>

# Remark (N.)

Yet this is a fact, and in it we plainly see the reason why it was necessary to make every word or action hateful by which we might reveal the <unclear>innate</unclear> desire we feel to reproduce; and why tamely submitting to the violence of a furious appetite (which is painful to resist) and innocently obeying the most pressing demand of nature without deceit or hypocrisy, like other creatures, should be labeled with the shameful name of "brutality."

What we call love, then, is not a genuine but a corrupted appetite—or rather a compound, a heap of several contradictory passions blended into one. As it is a product of nature warped by custom and education, its true origin and first motive, as I have suggested already, is stifled in well-bred people and almost hidden from themselves. This is the reason why, since those affected by it vary in age, strength, determination, temperament, circumstances, and manners, its effects are so different, whimsical, surprising, and unpredictable.

It is this passion that makes jealousy so troublesome and the envy resulting from it often so fatal; those who imagine that there can be jealousy without love do not understand that passion. Men may not have <unclear>the least</unclear> affection for their wives and yet be angry with them for their conduct and suspicious of them, either with or without a cause. But what affects them in such cases is their pride—the concern for their reputation. They feel a hatred against them without remorse; when they are out <unclear>of sight</unclear>, they can tolerate them and go to sleep contentedly. Such husbands may watch their wives <unclear>themselves</unclear> and have them observed by others; <unclear>but their</unclear> vigilance is not so intense. They are not <unclear>so inquisitive</unclear> or diligent in their searches, nor do they feel that heart-felt anxiety at the fear <unclear>of a</unclear> discovery as they do when love is mixed with the <unclear>rest</unclear>.

# Remark (O:)
<page-num>109</page-num>

<note>What Epicurus mea</note>nt, was being virtuous; so *Erasmus* in his *Colloquies* tells us, that there are no greater *Epicures* than pious *Christians*. Others who reflected on the loose manners of the greatest part of his followers would have it that by pleasures, he could be understood to mean nothing but sensual ones and the gratification of our passions. I shall not decide this quarrel, but am of the opinion that whether they be good or bad, what they take delight in is their pleasure; and not to look for any further philology <note>or etymology</note> from the learned languages, I believe a man may justly call everything a pleasure that pleases him. According to this definition, we ought to dispute no more about men's pleasures than their tastes: *Trahit sua quemque voluptas* <note>Latin: "Each is led by his own pleasure."</note>

The worldly-minded, voluptuous, and ambitious man, notwithstanding he is void of merit, shows impudence everywhere and desires to be dignified above his betters. He aims at spacious palaces and delicious gardens; his chief delight is in exceeding others in stately horses, magnificent coaches, numerous attendants, and expensive furniture. To gratify his lust, he wishes for genteel and beautiful women of different charms and complexions who shall adore his greatness and be really in love with his person. He will have his cellars stored with the flower of every country that produces excellent wines. He demands that his table be served with many courses, and each can contain a choice variety of dainties not easily purchased, and ample evidence of elaborate and delicious cookery; while harmonious music and well-phrased flattery entertain his hearing by turns. He employs, even in the most minor trifles, only the ablest and most ingenious workmen, so that his judgment and fancy may as evidently appear in the smallest things that belong to him as his birth and quality are manifested in those of greater importance.

...greater value. He desires to have several sets of witty, facetious, and polite people to converse with, and among them he would have some famous for learning and universal knowledge. For his serious affairs, he wishes to find men of skill and experience who should be diligent and faithful. Those who are to wait on him, he would have be handy, well-mannered, and discreet, of a comely aspect and a graceful mien; what he requires in them besides is a respectful care of everything that is his, nimbleness without hurry, dispatch without noise, and an unlimited obedience to his orders. He thinks nothing is more troublesome than speaking to servants; therefore, he will only be attended by such as, by observing his looks, have learned to interpret his will from his slightest motions. He loves to see an elegant precision in everything that approaches him, and in whatever is to be employed about his person, he desires a superlative cleanliness to be religiously observed. The chief officers of his household he would have to be men of birth, honor, and distinction, as well as order, ingenuity, and economy; for though he loves to be honored by everybody, and receives the respect of the common people with joy, yet the homage that is paid him by persons of quality is ravishing to him in a more transcendent manner.

While thus wallowing in a sea of lust and vanity, he is wholly occupied in provoking and indulging his appetites; he desires the world should think him altogether free from pride and sensuality, and put a favorable construction upon his most glaring vices: Nay, if his authority can purchase it, he covets to be thought wise, brave, generous, good-natured, and endowed with all the virtues he thinks worth having. # Remark (Q.)
<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>Remark (Q.)</header>
<warning>Heavy bleed-through from the verso side makes several passages difficult to read, particularly along the left margin.</warning>

burden which, to his sorrow, is inseparable from the high sphere he moves in; that his noble mind, so much exalted above common capacities, aims at higher ends and cannot relish such worthless enjoyments; that the height of his ambition is to promote the public welfare, and his greatest pleasure is to see his country flourish and everyone in it made happy. These are called real pleasures by the vicious and earthly-minded; and whoever is able, either by his skill or fortune, to practice this refined manner of at once enjoying the world and the good opinion of it, is considered extremely happy by all the most fashionable people.

But on the other hand, most ancient philosophers and grave moralists, especially the Stoics, would not allow anything to be a real good that was liable to be taken from them by others. They wisely considered the instability of fortune and the favor of princes; the vanity of honor and popular applause; the precariousness of riches and all earthly possessions; and therefore placed true happiness in the calm serenity of a contented mind free from guilt and ambition. This is a mind that, having subdued every sensual appetite, despises the smiles as well as the frowns of fortune; and, taking delight only in contemplation, desires nothing except what everyone is able to provide for themselves. A mind that is armed with fortitude and resolution has learned to sustain the greatest losses without concern, to endure pain without suffering, and to bear injuries without resentment. Many have claimed to have reached this height of self-denial, and then, if we may believe them, they were raised above common mortals, and their strength extended vastly beyond the level of their <unclear>Human</unclear> nature: They could behold the anger of a foaming tyrant and the most imminent dangers without terror, and preserved their tran-

# Remark (Q.)
<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>Remark (Q.)</header>
<warning>Right margin is tightly cropped, resulting in some truncated words at the end of lines.</warning>

tranquility in the midst of torments; they could meet death with intrepidity, and left the world with no greater reluctance than they showed fondness at their entrance into it.

These views among the ancients have always carried the greatest weight; yet others, who were no fools either, have rejected those precepts as impracticable, called their notions romantic, and endeavored to prove that what these Stoics asserted of themselves exceeded all human force and possibility, and that therefore the virtues they boasted of could be nothing but haughty pretense, full of arrogance and hypocrisy. Yet notwithstanding these censures, the serious part of the world and the generality of wise men who have lived ever since to this day agree with the Stoics in the most material points: such as that there can be no true felicity in what depends on perishable things; that peace within is the greatest blessing, and no conquest is like that over our passions; that knowledge, temperance, fortitude, humility, and other embellishments of the mind are the most valuable acquisitions; that no man can be happy but he who is good; and that the virtuous alone are capable of enjoying real pleasures.

I expect to be asked why, in the Fable, I have called those pleasures "real" that are directly opposite to those which I acknowledge the wise men of all ages have extolled as the most valuable. My answer is because I do not call things "pleasures" based on what men say are best, but based on what they seem to be most pleased with. How can I believe that a man's chief delight is in the embellishments of the mind, when I see him constantly employed about and daily pursuing the pleasures that are contrary to them? other business but to subdue their appetites. What can be a greater evidence of perfect chastity, and a superlative love for immaculate purity in men and women, than that in the prime of their age, when lust is most raging, they should actually seclude themselves from each other's company, and by a voluntary renunciation bar themselves for life, not only from uncleanness, but even the most lawful embraces? Those that abstain from meat, and often all manner of food, one would think are in the right way to conquer all carnal desires; and I could almost swear, that he does not consult his ease, who daily mauls his bare back and shoulders with excessive lashes, and is constantly roused at midnight from his sleep, leaving his bed for his devotion. Who can despise riches more, or show himself less avaricious than he, who will not so much as touch gold or silver, no, not even with his feet? Or can any mortal show himself less luxurious or more humble than the man, that making poverty his choice, contents himself with scraps and fragments, and refuses to eat any bread but what is bestowed upon him by the charity of others?

Such fair instances of self-denial would make me bow down to virtue, if I was not deterred and warned from it by so many persons of eminence and learning, who unanimously tell me that I am mistaken, and all I have seen is farce and hypocrisy; that whatever *seraphic love* they may pretend to, there is nothing but discord among them, and that however penitential the nuns and friars may appear in their several convents, they none of them sacrifice their darling lusts: That among the women they are not all virgins that pass for such, and that if I was to be let into their secrets, and examine some of their subterranean privacies, I should soon be convinced by scenes of horror, that some of them must have been mothers. # Remark (O.)
<meta>catchword: uneasy</meta>

notwithstanding the enjoyment of so much luxurious ease, they may set upon themselves whatever value their pride and abilities allow them.

I have nothing against all of this, but I see no self-denial, without which there can be no virtue. Is it such a mortification not to desire a greater share of worldly blessings than what every reasonable man ought to be satisfied with? Or is there any great merit in not being wicked and in forbearing indecencies that are repugnant to good manners, and which no prudent man would be guilty of, even if he had no religion at all?

I know I shall be told that the reason why the clergy are so violent in their resentments whenever they are even slightly affronted, and show themselves so void of all patience when their rights are invaded, is their great care to preserve their calling—their profession—from contempt, not for their own sakes, but to be more serviceable to others. It is the same reason that makes them solicitous about the comforts and conveniences of life; for should they allow themselves to be insulted, be content with a coarser diet, and wear more ordinary clothes than other people, the multitude (who judge by outward appearances) would be apt to think that the clergy were no more the immediate care of Providence than other folks, and so would not only undervalue their persons, but also despise all the reproofs and instructions that came from them. This is an admirable plea, and as it is frequently used, I will test the worth of it.

I am not of the learned Dr. *Eachard’s* opinion that poverty is one of those things that bring the clergy into contempt, any further than as it may be an occasion for discovering their weak side. For when men are always struggling with their low condition and are unable to bear the burden of it without reluctance, it is then they show how

# Remark (O.)

...how uncomfortably their poverty sits upon them, how glad they would be to have their circumstances improved, and what a real value they have for the good things of this world. The man who harangues on the contempt of riches and the vanity of earthly enjoyments while wearing a shabby, threadbare gown because he has no other, and who would wear his old greasy hat no longer if anyone would give him a better one; who drinks small beer <note>A weak, low-quality, and inexpensive beer.</note> at home with a gloomy expression but leaps at a glass of wine if he can catch it elsewhere; who with little appetite feeds upon his own coarse meal but eats greedily where he can please his palate and expresses an uncommon joy at an invitation to a splendid dinner—it is he who is despised, not because he is poor, but because he does not know how to be so with that contentment and resignation which he preaches to others, and so reveals his inclinations to be contrary to his doctrine.

But when a man, out of the greatness of his soul (or an obstinate vanity, which serves just as well), resolves to subdue his appetites in good earnest, refuses all the offers of ease and luxury that can be made to him, and, embracing a voluntary poverty with cheerfulness, rejects whatever may gratify the senses and actually sacrifices all his passions to his pride in acting this part, the common people, far from despising him, will be ready to deify and adore him. How famous have the *Cynic Philosophers* made themselves simply by refusing to pretend and by making no use of luxuries? Did not the most *Ambitious Monarch* <note>Alexander the Great.</note> the world ever produced condescend to visit *Diogenes* in his tub, and receive in return a studied rudeness—the highest compliment a man of such pride was able to give?

Mankind is very willing to take one another's word when they see circumstances that support what is told to them; but when our actions directly contradict what we say, it is counted

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding clerical hypocrisy, moving from "Remark (O)" to "Remark (Q)." It contrasts the sincerity of those who truly practice self-denial with those whose physical behavior betrays their worldly desires.</meta>

<page-num>318</page-num>

# Remark (Q.)

It is impudent to expect to be believed. If a cheerful, healthy man with glowing cheeks and warm hands, recently returned from some vigorous exercise or a cold bath, tells us in freezing weather that he does not care for a fire, we are easily persuaded to believe him—especially if he actually turns away from it and we know from his circumstances that he lacks neither fuel nor clothes. But if we were to hear the same thing from the mouth of a poor, starving wretch with swollen hands and a pale face, dressed in a thin, ragged garment, we would not believe a word he said—especially if we saw him shaking and shivering as he crept toward a sunny bank. We would conclude, no matter what he might say, that warm clothes and a good fire would be very welcome to him. 

The application is straightforward: if there are any clergy on earth who wish to be thought of as not caring for the world and as valuing the soul above the body, they should simply stop showing a greater concern for their physical pleasures than they generally do for their spiritual ones. They may then be certain that no amount of poverty, as long as they endure it with fortitude, will ever bring them into contempt, no matter how humble their circumstances may be.

Let us imagine a pastor who has a small flock entrusted to him, for whom he is very caring. He preaches, visits, encourages, and corrects his people with zeal and wisdom, and performs all the kind services within his power to make them happy. There is no doubt that those under his care must be very grateful to him. Now let us suppose once more that this good man, through a little self-denial, is content to live on his income, accepting only twenty pounds a year instead of the forty he could claim; and furthermore, that he loves his parishioners so well that he will never leave them for any promotion whatsoever—not even a bishopric, even if it were offered

# Remark (O!)

I cannot see why all this might not be an easy task for a man who professes self-denial and has no value for worldly pleasures. Yet I dare promise that such a selfless clergyman—despite the great degeneracy of mankind—will be loved, esteemed, and spoken well of by everyone. Indeed, I would swear that even if he should exert himself further—giving more than half of his small income to the poor, living on nothing but oatmeal and water, sleeping on straw, and wearing the coarsest cloth that could be made—his humble way of living would never be criticized or be a disgrace either to himself or to the religious order he belonged to. On the contrary, his poverty would never be mentioned except to his glory, as long as his memory should last.

But (says a charitable young gentlewoman), "Though you have the heart to starve your parson, do you have no feelings of compassion for his wife and children? Please, what would remain of forty pounds a year after it has been twice so unmercifully divided? And would you have the poor woman and the innocent babies also live on oatmeal and water, and sleep on straw, you unconscionable wretch, with all your suppositions and self-denials? Furthermore, is it possible, even if they should all live at your own meager rate, that less than ten pounds a year could maintain a family?"

Do not be in a passion, good Mrs. Abigail; I have a greater regard for your sex than to prescribe such a lean diet to married men. But I confess I forgot the wives and children. The main reason was that I thought the priests could have no need for them. Who could imagine that the parson, who is to teach others by example as well as by instruction, was not able to withstand those desires which the wicked world itself calls unreasonable? One may exercise as much patience and be as full of self-denial on a few <note>Flocks: mattresses stuffed with tufts of wool or cotton scraps.</note> flocks, without curtains or a <note>Tester: a canopy over a bed.</note> tester, as in a velvet bed that is sixteen feet high. <page-num>123</page-num>

# Remarks (Q)

And, consequently, the argument that all in high stations should have badges of honor and signs of power to be distinguished from the common people is a frivolous objection. This, in the first place, can only be of use to poor princes and weak and precarious governments that, being actually unable to maintain the public peace, are obliged to use a pageant-like show to make up for what they lack in real power. So the Governor of Batavia in the East Indies is forced to maintain a sense of grandeur and live in a magnificence above his status to strike terror into the natives of <unclear>Java</unclear>, who, if they had the skill and leadership, are strong enough to destroy ten times the number of their masters; but great princes and states that keep large fleets at sea and numerous armies in the field have no need for such stratagems; for what makes them formidable abroad will never fail to be their security at home. Secondly, what must protect the lives and wealth of people from the attempts of wicked men in all societies is the severity of the laws and the diligent administration of impartial justice. Theft, house-<unclear>breaking</unclear>, and murder are not to be prevented by the scarlet gowns of the aldermen, the gold chains of the sheriffs, the fine trappings of their horses, or any gaudy show whatever. These pa-<unclear>geant</unclear> ornaments are beneficial in another way: they <unclear>are</unclear> eloquent lessons to apprentices, and their use is to inspire, not to deter; but men of abandoned principles must be awed by rugged offi-<unclear>cers</unclear>, strong prisons, watchful jailers, the hangman, and the gallows. If London were for one week des-<unclear>titute</unclear> of constables and watchmen to guard the <unclear>Streets</unclear> at night, half the bankers would be ruined <unclear>in that</unclear> time; and if the Lord Mayor had nothing <unclear>to defend</unclear> himself but his great two-handed sword, <unclear>his</unclear> <term>Cap of Maintenance</term>, and his gilded mace, <unclear>he'd</unclear> soon be stripped in the very streets of the <unclear>City of</unclear> his finery in his stately coach.

# Remark (O.)

of a parish. *Cato*, during his government of *Spain*, in which he acquitted himself with so much glory, had only three servants to attend him. Do we hear that any of his orders were ever slighted because of this, notwithstanding that he loved his bottle? And when that great man marched on foot through the scorching sands of *Libya*, and, parched with thirst, refused to touch the water that was brought to him before all his soldiers had drunk, do we ever read that this heroic forbearance weakened his authority or lessened him in the esteem of his army? But why must we look so far away? For many ages, there has not been a prince less inclined toward pomp and luxury than the ‡ present <margin>‡ This was written in 1714.</margin> *King of Sweden*, <note>Charles XII of Sweden, known for his austere lifestyle and military campaigns.</note> who, enamored with the title of *Hero*, has not only sacrificed the lives of his subjects and the welfare of his dominions, but (what is more uncommon in sovereigns) his own ease and all the comforts of life to an implacable spirit of revenge; yet he is obeyed to the ruin of his people while obstinately maintaining a war that has almost utterly destroyed his kingdom.

Thus I have proved that the real pleasures of all *men in nature* are worldly and sensual, if we judge from their practice. I say all *men in nature* because *devout Christians*—who alone are to be excepted here, being reborn and preternaturally assisted by divine grace—cannot be said to be "in nature." How strange it is that they should all so unanimously deny it! Ask not only the theologians and moralists of every nation, but likewise all who are rich and powerful about real pleasure, and they will tell you, along with the *Stoics*, that there can be no true happiness in things worldly and corruptible; but then look at their lives, and you will find they take delight in nothing else. What must we do in this dilemma? Shall we be so uncharitable as to judge from men's actions and say,

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the gap between human profession and practice. say, that all the world prevaricates, and that it is not their opinion, let them talk what they will. Or shall we be so silly as to rely on what they say, to think them sincere in their sentiments, and not believe our own eyes? Or shall we rather endeavor to believe ourselves and them too, and say with *Montaigne*, that they imagine and are half-persuaded that they believe what they don't actually believe? These are his words: "*Some impose upon the world and would be thought to believe what they don't; but much the greater number impose upon themselves, not considering nor thoroughly apprehending what it is to believe.*" But this is making all mankind either fools or impostors; to avoid which, there is nothing left for us but to say what Mr. *Bayle* has endeavored to prove at length in his *Reflections on Comets*: that man is so unaccountable a creature as to act very commonly against his principle. This is so far from being injurious that it is a compliment to human nature, for we must say either this or with...

This contradiction in the frame of man is the reason that the theory of virtue is so well understood, and the practice of it so rarely to be met with. If you ask me where to look for those beautiful, shining qualities of prime ministers and the great favorites of princes that are so finely lauded in dedications, addresses, epitaphs, funeral sermons, and inscriptions, I answer: there and nowhere else. Where would you look for the excellence of a statue but in that part which you see of it? It is the polished outside only that has the skill and labor of the sculptor to boast of; what is out of sight is untouched. Would you break the head or cut open the breast to look for the lungs or the heart, you would only show your ignorance and destroy the workmanship. This has often made me compare the virtues of great men to your fine China jars; they make a fine show and are ornamental to a chimney; one would by the bulk...

(P.) ->The very Poor<-
->*Lived better than the Rich before.*<-
->Page 6. Line 29.<-

If we trace the most flourishing nations to their origin, we shall find that in the remote beginnings of every society, the richest and most considerable men among them were for a long time destitute of a great many comforts of life that are now enjoyed by the meanest and most humble wretches. So that many things which were once looked upon as the invention of luxury are now allowed even to those who are so miserably poor as to become the objects of public charity—indeed, they are counted so necessary that we think no human creature ought to lack them.

In the first ages, man, without doubt, fed on the fruits of the earth without any previous preparation, and rested himself naked, like other animals, on the lap of their common parent. Whatever has contributed since to make life more comfortable, as it must have been the result of thought, experience, and some labor, so it more or less deserves the name of luxury, depending on the more or less trouble it required and how much it deviated from primitive simplicity. Our admiration extends no further than to what is new to us, and we all overlook the excellence of things we are used to, however remarkable they may be. A man would be laughed at who should discover luxury in the plain dress of a poor creature that walks along in a thick

# Remark (P.)

->The very Poor<-
->*Lived better than the Rich before.*<-
->Page 6. Line 29.<-

...parish gown and a coarse shirt underneath it; yet what a number of people, how many different trades, and what a variety of skill and tools must be employed to have the most ordinary low cloth? What depth of thought and ingenuity, what toil and labor, and what length of time must it have cost before man could learn from the first seed to raise and prepare so useful a product as linen?

Must that society not be vainly curious, among whom this admirable commodity—after it is woven—shall not be thought fit to be used even by the poorest of all, before it is brought to a perfect whiteness? This whiteness is not to be procured but by the assistance of all the elements joined to a wonderful industry and patience. I have not done yet: if we reflect not only on the cost laid out upon this luxurious invention, but likewise on the little while the whiteness of it continues—in which its particular beauty consists—so that every six or seven days at least it requires cleaning, and while it lasts is a constant charge to the wearer; can we, I say, reflect upon this, and not think it an extravagant piece of nicety, that even those who receive alms of the parish should not only have whole garments made of this laborious manufacture, but likewise that as soon as they are soiled, to restore them to their pristine purity, they should make use of one of the most judicious as well as difficult compounds that chemistry can boast of? With this compound, dissolved in water by the help of fire, the most detersive and yet innocent *lixivium* <note>a liquid solution of alkaline salts, such as lye, used for cleaning</note> is prepared that human industry has hitherto been able to invent.

It is certain, there was a time when the things I speak of would have borne those lofty expressions, and when everyone would have reasoned in the same manner; but the age we live in would call a man a fool who should talk of extravagance or nicety if he saw a poor woman, after her...

# Remark (P.)

<warning>Heavy ink bleed or staining on the bottom left corner obscuring parts of the text.</warning>

The arts of brewing and making bread have, by slow degrees, been brought to the perfection they are now in; but to have invented them at once, and *a priori* <note>from theoretical deduction rather than experience</note>, would have required more knowledge and a deeper insight into the nature of fermentation than the greatest philosopher has hitherto been endowed with. Yet the fruits of both are now enjoyed by the humblest of our species, and a starving wretch knows not how to make a more humble or a more modest petition than by asking for a bit of bread or a draught of small beer <note>weak or low-quality beer</note>.

Man has learned by experience that nothing was softer than the small plumes and down of birds, and found that, heaped together, they would by their elasticity gently resist any weight resting on them, and heave up again of themselves as soon as the pressure is over. To make use of them to sleep upon was, no doubt, first invented to compliment the vanity as well as ease of the wealthy and powerful; but they have long since become so common that almost everybody lies upon featherbeds, and to substitute flocks <note>coarse tufts of wool or cotton used for stuffing</note> in their place is counted a miserable shift of the most needy. To what a vast height must luxury have arrived before it could be considered a hardship to rest upon the soft wool of animals!

From caves, huts, hovels, tents, and barracks, which mankind made do with at first, we have come to warm and well-built houses, and the humblest dwellings to be seen in cities are regular buildings designed by persons skilled in proportions and architecture. If the ancient *Britons* and *Gauls* should come out of their graves, with what amazement would they gaze on the mighty structures everywhere raised for the poor! Should they behold the magnificence of a *Chelsea College*, a *Greenwich Hospital*, or what surpasses them all, a *Des Invalides*—

# Remark (P.)

<warning>Heavy ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page makes the text difficult to read in some sections.</warning>

...hospitals at Paris, and see the care, the plenty, the superfluities, and pomp with which people who have no possessions at all are treated in those stately palaces, those who were once the greatest and richest of the land would have reason to envy the most destitute of our species now.

Another piece of *luxury* the *poor* enjoy, which is not looked upon as such, and which there is no doubt that the wealthiest in a *Golden Age* <note>a mythical era of primordial peace and simplicity</note> would abstain from, is their use of the flesh of animals for food. Regarding the fashions and manners of the ages men live in, they never examine the real worth or merit of the cause, and generally judge things not as their reason directs them, but as custom does. There was a time when funeral rites for disposing of the dead were performed by *fire*, and the *corpses* of the greatest emperors were burned to ashes. At that time, burying the corpse in the ground was a funeral for *slaves*, or was made a punishment for the worst of *criminals*: now, nothing is decent or honorable but burial, and burning the body is reserved for crimes of the darkest nature. At some times we look upon trifles with *horror*, while at other times we can behold enormities without *concern*. If we see a man walk with his hat on in a *church*, even outside of service time, it shocks us; but if on a *Sunday night* we see half a dozen fellows drunk in the *street*, the sight makes little or no impression on us. If a woman at a merry-making dresses in men’s clothes, it is considered a *frolic* among friends, and he who finds too much fault with it is counted *judgmental*: on the *stage* it is done without reproach, and the most virtuous ladies will tolerate it in an actress, though everyone has a full view of her legs and thighs; but if the same woman, as soon as she has *petticoats* on again, should show her leg to a man as high as her knee, it would be a very immodest action, and everyone would call her impudent for it.

# Remark (P.)

<warning>Significant ink staining and bleed-through throughout the page, obscuring portions of the text, particularly in the lower half.</warning>

I have often thought that, if it were not for the tyranny which custom usurps over us, men of any tolerable good nature could never be reconciled to the killing of so many animals for their daily food, as long as the bountiful Earth so plentifully provides them with varieties of vegetable delicacies. I know that reason excites our compassion only faintly, and therefore I do not wonder how men should so little pity such imperfect creatures as crayfish, oysters, cockles, and indeed all fish in general. Since they are mute, and their internal formation, as well as their outward figure, is vastly different from ours, they express themselves unintelligibly to us; and therefore it is not strange that their grief should not affect our understanding, which it cannot reach. For nothing stirs us to pity so effectively as when the symptoms of misery strike immediately upon our senses; and I have seen people moved at the noise a live lobster makes upon the spit, who could have killed half a dozen fowls with pleasure. But in such perfect animals as sheep and oxen, in which the heart, the brain, and the nerves differ so little from ours, and in which the separation of the spirits from the blood <note>a reference to the historical physiological theory that "animal spirits" were refined from the blood to facilitate sensation</note>, the organs of sense, and consequently feeling itself are the same as they are in human creatures, I cannot imagine how a man not hardened in blood and massacre is able to see a violent death, and the pangs of it, without concern.

In answer to this, most people will think it sufficient to say that all things are allowed to be made for the service of man, and there can be no cruelty in putting creatures to the use they were designed for; but I have heard men make this reply while their inner nature has reproached them with the falsehood of the assertion. There is not among the multitude more than one man in ten but whom the <unclear>sight</unclear> of, <unclear>if he have</unclear> not <unclear>been</unclear> brought up in a slaughter-house,

Some people cannot be persuaded to taste any creatures they have daily seen and been acquainted with while they were alive; others extend their scruples no further than to their own poultry, and refuse to eat what they themselves took care of; yet all of them will feed heartily and without remorse on *beef*, *mutton*, and *fowls* when they are bought in the *market*. In this behavior, it seems to me, there appears something like a consciousness of *guilt*; it looks as if they endeavored to save themselves from the imputation of a *crime* (which they know sticks somewhere) by removing the cause of it as far as they can from themselves; and I can discover in it strong remains of *primitive pity* and *innocence*, which all the arbitrary power of *custom* and the violence of *luxury* have not yet been able to conquer.

What I build upon, I shall be told, is a *folly* that *wise men* are not guilty of; I admit it; but if it proceeds from a real *passion* inherent in our nature, it is sufficient to demonstrate that we are born with a repugnancy to the killing, and consequently the eating, of *animals*; for it is impossible that a natural appetite should ever prompt us to do, or desire others to do, what we have an aversion to, be it as foolish as it will.

Every body knows that *surgeons* in the cure of dangerous *wounds* and *fractures*, the extirpation of *limbs*, and other dreadful *operations*, are often compelled to put their *patients* to extraordinary *torments*; and the more desperate and calamitous *cases* occur to them, the more the miseries and bodily sufferings of others must become familiar to them; for this reason our *English* laws, out of a most affectionate regard to the

Now, if we ought to have no concern for what we do to brute beasts, and it was not imagined that there was any cruelty in killing them, why should *butchers*—out of all callings, and only they along with *surgeons*—be excluded from being jurors by the same law?

I shall urge nothing of what *Pythagoras* and many other wise men have said concerning this barbarity of eating flesh; I have gone too much out of my way already, and shall therefore beg the reader—if he would like any more of this—to run over the following fable; or else, if he is tired, to let it alone, with an assurance that in doing either, he shall equally oblige me.

A *Roman* merchant in one of the *Carthaginian Wars* was shipwrecked upon the coast of *Africa*: he and his slave got safely ashore with great difficulty; but while going in search of relief, they were met by a *lion* of a mighty size. It happened to be one of the breed that ranged in *Aesop's* days, and one that could not only speak several languages but also seemed very well acquainted with human affairs. The slave climbed a tree, but his master—not thinking himself safe there and having heard much of the generosity of *lions*—fell prostrate before him with all the signs of fear and submission. The *lion*, who had lately filled his belly, bids him rise and for a while lie by his side, assuring him as well that he should not be <unclear>hurt</unclear> if he could give him any tolerable reason why he should not be devoured. The merchant obeyed; and having now received some glimmering hopes of safety, gave a dismal account of the shipwreck he had suffered, and endeavored...

# Remark (P. 9)

<warning>The image is low resolution with significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side and dark spotting throughout the text area.</warning>

...from there, to raise the lion’s pity, he pleaded his cause with an abundance of good rhetoric; but observing by the expression of the beast that flattery and fine words made very little impression, he turned to arguments of greater solidity. Reasoning from the excellence of man’s nature and abilities, he argued how improbable it was that the gods should not have designed him for a better use than to be eaten by savage beasts. At this, the lion became more attentive and granted a reply every now and then, until at last the following dialogue ensued between them.

“Oh, vain and covetous animal,” said the lion, “whose pride and avarice can make him leave his native soil—where his natural wants might be plentifully supplied—and brave rough seas and dangerous mountains to find luxuries; why should you value your species above ours? And if the gods have given you a superiority over other creatures, then why do you beg from an inferior?”

“The superiority,” answered the merchant, “consists not in bodily force but in the strength of understanding; the gods have endowed us with a rational soul, which, though invisible, is the much better part of us.”

“I desire to touch no part of you but what is good to eat,” the lion replied, “but why do you value yourself so much for that part which is invisible?”

“Because it is immortal and shall receive rewards after death for the actions of this life; the just shall enjoy eternal bliss and tranquility with heroes and demigods in the Elysian Fields.”

“What life have you led?”

“I have honored the gods and been beneficial to man.”

“Then why do you fear death, if you think the gods are as just as you have been?”

“I have a wife and five small children who will fall into poverty if they lose me.”

“I have two cubs that are big enough to fend for themselves, who are hungry now and will actually be starved if I can find nothing for them. Your children will be provided for one way or another—at least as well once I have eaten you as if you had been drowned.”

# Remark (P.)

<warning>Heavy bleed-through from the reverse side and thick ink saturation throughout make some characters appear blurred or distorted.</warning>

As for the excellence of either species, the value of things among you has always increased with their scarcity; and for every million men, there is hardly one *lion*. Besides that, in the great veneration *man* pretends to have for his *kind*, there is little sincerity beyond the share that everyone’s pride has in it for himself. It is a folly to boast of the tenderness shown and care given to your young ones, or the excessive and lasting trouble bestowed on the *education* of them: *man* being born the most needy and most helpless *animal*, this is only an instinct of *nature*, which in all creatures has always proportioned the care of the parents to the wants and weaknesses of the offspring. But if a *man* had a real value for his *kind*, how is it possible that ten thousand of them, and sometimes ten times as many, should be destroyed in a few hours for the whim of two? All ranks of *men* despise those who are inferior to them; and if you could enter into the hearts of *kings* and *princes*, you would hardly find any who do not have less value for the majority of the multitudes they rule over than those people have for the cattle that belong to them. Why should so many pretend to derive their lineage, even if falsely, from the immortal *gods*? Why should all of them allow others to kneel before them and, more or less, take delight in having divine honors paid to them, except to suggest that they themselves are of a more exalted nature, and a species superior to that of their subjects?

I am savage, but no creature can be called cruel unless he, either by malice or insensibility, extinguishes his natural pity. The *lion* was born without compassion; we follow the instinct of our *nature*. The *gods* have appointed us to live upon the wilderness and prey of other animals, and as long as we can find dead ones, we never hunt after the

# Remark (P.)

<warning>Heavy ink bleed or damage on the bottom right corner obscuring fragments of the right margin.</warning>

the living. It is only man, mischievous man, who can make a sport of death. Nature taught your stomach to crave nothing but vegetables; but your violent fondness for change and greater eagerness for novelties have prompted you to the destruction of animals without justice or necessity, perverting your nature and warping your appetites whichever way your pride or luxury have called them. The lion has a ferment within him that consumes the toughest skin and hardest bones as well as the flesh of all animals without exception; your squeamish stomach, in which the digestive heat is weak and inconsiderable, will not even admit the most tender parts of them unless more than half the digestion has been performed by artificial fire beforehand. And yet, what animal have you spared to satisfy the caprices of a languid appetite? Languid, I say; for what is man's hunger compared to the lion's? Yours, when it is at its worst, makes you faint; mine makes me mad. I have often tried with roots and herbs to allay the violence of it, but in vain; nothing but large quantities of flesh can in any way appease it.

Yet, despite the fierceness of our hunger, lions have often repaid benefits received; but ungrateful and treacherous man feeds on the sheep that clothes him and does not spare her innocent young ones, whom he has taken into his care and custody. If you tell me the gods made man master over all other creatures, what tyranny was it then to destroy them out of wantonness? No, fickle, timorous animal, the gods have made you for society and designed that millions of you, when well joined together, should compose the strong <term>Leviathan</term>. A single lion bears some sway in the creation, but what is a single man? A small and inconsiderable part, a trifling atom of this great beast. What nature designs, she executes, and it is not safe to judge of what she purposes,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Remark (Q), specifically defining frugality and arguing that national habits of saving are driven by necessity rather than policy or inherent character.</meta>

<warning>Severe ink bleed-through from the reverse side of the page obscures the text significantly, particularly at the beginning of the lines.</warning>

<unclear>...</unclear> a medium between profuseness and avarice, rather aiming toward the latter. Just as this prudent economy, which some people call *saving*, is the most certain method in private families to increase an estate, so some imagine that whether a country be barren or fruitful, the same method—if generally pursued (which they think practicable)—will have the same effect upon a whole nation. They believe, for example, that the *English* might be much richer than they are if they would be as frugal as some of their neighbors. This, I think, is an error; to prove this, I shall first refer the reader to what has been said on this topic in Remark (L) and then reason as follows.

Experience teaches us first that as people differ in their views and perceptions of things, so they vary in their inclinations: one man is given to covetousness, another to prodigality, and a third is simply *saving*. Secondly, that men are never, or at least very seldom, reclaimed from their darling passions by either reason or precept; and if anything ever draws them from what they are naturally inclined toward, it must be a change in their circumstances or their fortunes. If we reflect upon these observations, we shall find that to render the majority of a nation lavish, the products of the country must be considerable in proportion to the inhabitants, and those things they use profusely must be cheap. On the contrary, to make a nation generally frugal, the necessities of life must be scarce and, consequently, expensive. Therefore, no matter what the best politician does, the profuseness or frugality of a people in general must always depend upon—and will, in spite of him, be ever proportioned to—the fruitfulness and productivity of the country, the number of inhabitants, and the taxes they must bear. If anyone would refute what I have said, let them only prove from history that there ever was in any country a national frugality without a national necessity.

<summary>The author argues that national frugality is not a choice made by politicians or citizens, but a result of economic necessity and resource scarcity. He contends that people's natural inclinations toward spending or saving are only altered by changes in their external circumstances.</summary>
<keywords>frugality, saving, national economy, avarice, English economy, Remark (L), national necessity, resource management</keywords>

the best policy is to preserve men in their native

improve their understanding.

Great wealth and foreign treasure will ever
scorn to come among men, unless you will admit
their inseparable companions, avarice and
luxury: Where trade is significant, fraud will
intrude. To be at once well-bred and sincere, is no
less than a contradiction; and therefore while man
advances in knowledge, and his manners are
polished, we must expect to see at the same time his
desires enlarged, his appetites refined, and his
vices increased.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the factors that allow a nation to become a great power, specifically focusing on the historical example of the United Provinces (the Netherlands) and their rise through trade, religious tolerance, and the necessity of frugality during their war for independence from Spain.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>Remark (59)</header>
<warning>Heavy ink staining and bleed-through throughout the page; the right margin is partially clipped in the source image, necessitating inferred completions in brackets.</warning>

...[princi]pal powers of Europe, has been their political wisdom in subordinating everything to merchandise and navigation, the unlimited liberty of conscience that is enjoyed among them, and the unwearied application with which they have always made use of the most effectual means to encourage and increase trade in general.

They were never noted for frugality until Philip II of Spain began to rage over them with unheard-of tyranny. Their laws were trampled upon, their rights and large immunities taken from them, and their constitution torn to pieces. Several of their chief nobles were condemned and executed without a legal form of process. Complaints and remonstrances were punished as severely as resistance, and those who escaped being massacred were plundered by ravenous soldiers. As this was intolerable to a people who had always been used to the mildest of governments and enjoyed greater privileges than any of the neighboring nations, they chose instead to take up arms rather than perish by cruel executioners. If we consider the strength Spain had then, and the circumstances these distressed states were in, there never was heard of a more unequal strife; yet such was their fortitude and resolution that seven of those provinces, uniting themselves together, maintained against the greatest and best-disciplined nation in Europe the most tedious and bloody war that is to be found in ancient or modern history.

Rather than become a victim of the Spanish fury, they were content to live on a small part of their revenues and spend by far the greatest part of their income in defending themselves against their merciless enemies. These hardships and calamities of a war within their own borders forced them into that extraordinary frugality, the continuance of which under the same difficulties...

The country is so small and so populous, that there is not land enough (though hardly an inch of it is unimproved) to feed the tenth part of the inhabitants: Holland itself is full of large rivers, and lies lower than the sea, which would run over it every tide, and wash it away in one winter, if it were not kept out by vast banks and huge walls; the repairs of those, as well as their sluices, their mills, and other necessaries they are forced continually to be at, to keep themselves from being drowned, is a greater expense to them one year with another, than could be raised by a general land tax of four shillings in the pound, if to be collected from the net produce of the landlord's income.

Is it a wonder that people under such circumstances, and loaded with greater taxes besides, than any other nation, should be obliged to be frugal? But why must they be a pattern to others, who besides that they are more happily situated, are much richer within themselves; and have, for the same number of people, above ten times the extent of ground? The Dutch and we often buy & sell at the same markets, and so far our views may be said to be the same. Otherwise the interest and political reasons of the two nations, and the private economy of either, are very different. It is their interest to be frugal and spend little, because they must have everything from abroad, except butter, cheese and fish, and therefore of them, especially the latter, they consume

He argues that England should not necessarily follow the Dutch model of extreme frugality, as England possesses far more land and resources.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the laboring class is motivated to work primarily by necessity rather than ambition. It notes that while the poor should be kept from starving, they should not be allowed to accumulate savings that would discourage them from labor. It then transitions into a discussion on how excessive wealth can be detrimental to a nation's productivity, using Spain as a historical example. [They] should almost never be idle, and yet continually spend what they get.

All men, as *Sir William Temple* observes very well, are more prone to ease and pleasure than they are to labor, when they are not prompted to it by pride or avarice; and those who earn their living by their daily labor are seldom powerfully influenced by either. Therefore, they have nothing to stir them up to be useful but their wants, which it is wise to relieve, but foolish to cure. The only thing, then, that can make the laboring man industrious is a moderate amount of money; for as too little will, according to his temperament, either dispirit him or make him desperate, so too much will make him insolent and lazy.

A man would be laughed at by most people if he maintained that too much money could undo a nation; yet this has been the fate of *Spain*, as the learned *Don Diego Savedra* ascribes the ruin of his country to this. The fruits of the earth in former ages had made *Spain* so rich that *King Lewis* of *France*, having come to the court of *Toledo*, was astonished at its splendor and said that he had never seen anything to be compared to it, either in *Europe* or *Asia*—he who, in his travels to the *Holy Land*, had passed through every province of them. In the Kingdom of *Castille* alone (if we may believe some writers), there were gathered together from <unclear>all</unclear> parts of the world for the *Holy War* one hundred thousand infantry, ten thousand cavalry, and sixty thousand carriages for baggage, which *Alonso III* maintained at his own expense and paid every day—the soldiers as well as officers and princes—everyone according to his rank and dignity. Indeed, down to the time of *Ferdinand* and *Isabella* (who equipped *Columbus*) and some time after, *Spain* was a fertile country where trade and manufactures flourished, and it had a knowledgeable, industrious people to boast of; but as soon as that mighty treasure, which was <unclear>found</unclear>
