<meta>This page continues the economic critique of how precious metals affected European powers like Spain and Portugal, then transitions to "Remark (R)" concerning the nature of "Honor."</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>
<meta>catchword: a Man</meta>

The product is *Portugal*, and the figure which that kingdom makes in *Europe* with all its gold is, I think, not much to be envied.

The great art, then, to make a nation happy and what we call flourishing, consists in giving everybody an opportunity to be employed; to achieve this, let a government's first care be to promote as great a variety of manufactures, arts, and handicrafts as human wit can invent; and the second to encourage agriculture and fishery in all their branches, so that the whole earth may be forced to exert itself as well as man; for as the one is an infallible maxim to draw vast multitudes of people into a nation, so the other is the only method to maintain them.

It is from this policy, and not the trifling regulations of lavishness and frugality (which will always take their own course according to the circumstances of the people), that the greatness and happiness of nations must be expected; for whether the value of gold and silver rises or falls, the enjoyment of all societies will always depend upon the fruits of the earth and the labor of the people; both of which, joined together, are a more certain, more inexhaustible, and a more real treasure than the gold of *Brazil* or the silver of *Potosi*.

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition and social analysis of "Honor" as a principle of conduct, following the introduction to Remark (R) on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>

A man of honor enters into a conspiracy with others to murder a king; he is obliged to go through with it; and if, overcome by remorse or good nature, he recoils at the enormity of his purpose, reveals the plot, and turns witness against his accomplices, he then forfeits his honor—at least among the party he belonged to. The excellence of this principle is that the common people are destitute of it, and it is only to be met with in people of the better sort; just as some oranges have seeds and others do not, though the outside looks the same. In great families, it is like the gout—generally considered hereditary—and all lords' children are born with it. In some who never felt any of it, it is acquired by conversation and reading (especially of romances); in others, through promotion; but there is nothing that encourages its growth more than a sword, and upon first wearing one, some people have felt considerable sprouts of it within twenty-four hours.

The chief and most important care a man of honor ought to have is the preservation of this principle; rather than forfeit it, he must lose his job and estate—nay, life itself. For this reason, whatever humility he may show through good breeding, he is allowed to place an inestimable value upon himself as a possessor of this invisible ornament. The only method to preserve this principle is to live up to the rules of honor, which are the laws he is to follow. He himself is always obliged to be faithful to his trust, to prefer the public interest to his own, not to tell lies, nor defraud or wrong anyone; and from others, he must suffer no affront—which is a technical term for any action intentionally designed to undervalue him.

The men of ancient honor—of whom I consider Don Quixote to have been the last on record—were very meticulous observers of all these laws, and many more than I have named; but the moderns...

<sig>H 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: moderns</meta>

<summary>The author describes "Honor" as an exclusive, often hereditary principle among the upper classes that dictates strict loyalty to one's peers and self-worth, even above morality or law. He notes that while it requires integrity, it also mandates that a man must never tolerate a personal affront.</summary>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues Remark (R), further analyzing the social principles of the "man of honor," specifically focusing on how modern interpretations prioritize personal courage and the demand for satisfaction over other moral obligations.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>

moderns seem to be more remiss; they have a profound veneration for the last of them, but they do not pay an equal obedience to any of the others; and whoever will but strictly comply with the one I hint at shall have an abundance of offenses against all the rest overlooked.

A man of honor is always considered impartial, and a man of sense as a matter of course; for nobody ever heard of a man of honor who was a fool. For this reason, he has nothing to do with the law and is always allowed to be a judge in his own case; and if the least injury be done either to himself or his friend, his relative, his servant, his dog, or anything which he is pleased to take under his honorable protection, satisfaction must be demanded immediately; and if it proves to be an affront, and he that gave it is likewise a man of honor, a battle must ensue. From all this it is evident that a man of honor must be possessed of courage, and that without it his other principle would be no more than a sword without a point. Let us therefore examine what courage consists in, and whether it be—as most people would have it—a real something that valiant men have in their nature distinct from all their other qualities, or not.

There is nothing so universally sincere upon Earth as the love which all creatures, that are capable of any, bear to themselves; and as there is no love but what implies a care to preserve the thing beloved, so there is nothing more sincere in any creature than his will, wishes, and endeavors to preserve himself. This is the law of nature, by which no creature is endowed with any appetite or passion except what either directly or indirectly tends to the preservation of either himself or his species.

The means by which nature obliges every creature continually to act in this business of self-preservation are grafted in him, and (in man) <unclear>his</unclear>

<summary>The author explains that modern men of honor prioritize the defense against "affronts" through dueling above all other rules, asserting that courage is the essential foundation of honor. He then begins a philosophical inquiry into courage, identifying it as an extension of the natural instinct for self-preservation.</summary>
<keywords>man of honor, courage, self-preservation, law of nature, dueling, affront, satisfaction, social conduct</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into courage and human nature within Remark (R), transitioning from the general law of self-preservation to a specific analysis of the passions, particularly fear and its relationship to reason.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>

Desires, which either compel him to crave what he thinks will sustain or please him, or command him to avoid what he imagines might displease, hurt, or destroy him. These desires or passions all have their different symptoms by which they manifest themselves to those they disturb; and from that variety of disturbances they make within us, their various names have been given to them, as has been shown already regarding pride and shame.

The passion that is raised in us when we apprehend that mischief is approaching us is called fear. The disturbance it makes within us is always more or less violent in proportion, not to the danger, but to our apprehension of the mischief dreaded, whether real or imaginary. Our fear, then, being always proportional to the apprehension we have of the danger, it follows that while that apprehension lasts, a man can no more shake off his fear than he can shake off a leg or an arm. In a fright, it is true, the apprehension of danger is so sudden and attacks us so vividly (as sometimes to take away reason and the senses) that when it is over, we often do not remember that we had any apprehension at all. But from the outcome, it is plain we had it; for how could we have been frightened if we had not apprehended that some evil or other was coming upon us?

Most people are of the opinion that this apprehension is to be conquered by reason, but I confess I am not. Those who have been frightened will tell you that as soon as they could recollect themselves—that is, make use of their reason—their apprehension was conquered. But this is no conquest at all; for in a fright, the danger was either altogether imaginary, or else it has passed by the time they can make use of their reason. Therefore, if they find there is no danger, it is no wonder that they should not apprehend any. But when the danger is imminent, let them then make use of their rea-

He argues that reason does not truly conquer fear, as fear only subsides once the perceived threat is gone or recognized as imaginary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific analysis of Remark (R), specifically exploring how Reason interacts with Fear and introducing the role of Anger, Hunger, and Lust in the drive for Self-Preservation.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header># Remark (R.)</header>

<warning>The text on this page is cut off on both the left and right margins, resulting in incomplete words at the beginning and end of many lines.</warning>

reason, and they will find that it may serve them to examine the greatness and reality of the danger; and if they find it less than they imagined, their apprehension will be lessened accordingly. If the danger proves real, and remains the same in every circumstance as they first took it to be, then reason, instead of diminishing, will rather increase their apprehension. While this fear lasts, no creature can fight offensively; and yet we see them daily fight obstinately and worry one another to death. Therefore, some other passion must be able to overcome this fear, and the one most contrary to it is anger; to trace this to its bottom, I must ask leave to make another digression.

No creature can subsist without food, nor can any species of them (I speak of the more perfect animals) continue for long unless young ones are continually born as fast as the old ones die. Therefore, the first and fiercest appetite that nature has given them is hunger, and the next is lust—the one prompting them to procreate, as the other makes them eat. Now, if we observe that anger is a passion which is raised in us when we are frustrated or disturbed in our desires, and that as it furnishes strength in creatures, it was given to them so that by it they might exert themselves more vigorously in endeavoring to remove, overcome, or destroy whatever obstructs them in the pursuit of self-preservation; we shall find that brutes—when they themselves, or what they love, or the liberty of either are threatened or attacked—have nothing within our notice that can move them to anger but hunger or lust. It is these that make them more fierce; for we must observe that the appetites of creatures are just as actually frustrated while they want and cannot meet with what they desire (though perhaps with less violence) as when they are hindered from enjoying what they have in view. What I have said will appear more plainly if we only consider what...

He identifies hunger and lust as the primary appetites that drive this anger and ferocity in creatures when their desires are frustrated.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the passions that drive animals, specifically focusing on how hunger and instinct contribute to the anger and aggression seen in beasts of prey, while lust motivates combat in other species.</meta>

<header>Remark (R.)</header>
<warning>Significant ink bleed and staining along the left margin and bottom left corner, partially obscuring the start of several lines.</warning>

...can be ignorant of, which is this: All creatures upon earth live either upon its fruits and products, or else the flesh of other animals, their fellow creatures. The latter, which we call beasts of prey, nature has armed accordingly, and given them weapons and strength to overcome and tear asunder those whom she has designed for their food; she has also given them a much keener appetite than she has given to other animals that live upon herbs, etc. For as to the first, if a cow loved mutton as well as she does grass—being built as she is, having no claws or talons, and having but one row of teeth in the front that are all of equal length—she would be starved even among a flock of sheep. Secondly, as to their voraciousness, if experience did not teach us, our reason might. In the first place, it is highly probable that the hunger which can make a creature fatigue, harass, and expose himself to danger for every bit he eats is more piercing than the hunger which only bids him eat what stands before him, and which he may have just by stooping down. In the second place, it is to be considered that, just as beasts of prey have an instinct by which they learn to crave, track, and discover those creatures that are good food for them, so the others likewise have an instinct that teaches them to shun, conceal themselves from, and run away from those that hunt after them. From this it must follow that beasts of prey, though they could almost eat forever, yet go with empty bellies more often than other creatures whose food neither flies from nor opposes them. This must perpetuate as well as increase their hunger, which thereby becomes constant fuel for their anger.

But if you ask me what stirs up this anger in bulls and cocks—which will fight to the death and yet are neither animals of prey nor very voracious—I answer: lust. Those creatures whose rage proceeds from hunger, both male and female, attack every

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of courage and aggression, moving from animal observations to human psychology and anatomy.</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>
<meta>catchword: Rapine</meta>

This may be partly demonstrated from what is observable in ourselves; for though our hunger is infinitely less violent than that of wolves and other ravenous creatures, yet we see that people who are in health and have a decent appetite are more fretful, and more easily annoyed by trifles when they wait for their food beyond their usual hours, than at any other time. And again, though lust in man is not as raging as it is in bulls and other lustful creatures, yet nothing provokes both men and women sooner or more violently to anger than what thwarts their love affairs when they are deeply in love; even the most fearful and gently raised of either sex have disregarded the greatest dangers and set aside all other considerations to achieve the destruction of a rival.

Up to this point, I have endeavored to demonstrate that no creature can fight offensively as long as its fear lasts; that fear cannot be conquered except by another passion; that the passion most contrary to it and most effective in overcoming it is anger; that the two principal appetites which, when frustrated, can stir up this last-named passion are *hunger* and *lust*; and that in all brute beasts, the tendency toward anger and obstinacy in fighting generally depends upon the violence of either or both of those appetites together. From this it must follow that what we call prowess or natural courage in creatures is nothing but the effect of anger, and that all fierce animals must be either very ravenous or very lustful, if not both.

Let us now examine what, by this rule, we ought to judge regarding our own species. From the tenderness of human skin and the great care that is required for many years together to raise a person; from the structure of his jaws, the evenness of his teeth, the breadth of his nails, and the slightness of both, it is not probable that nature designed him for

consist in an opposite method of the same self-preservation for which nature before had furnished him with anger, as well as the rest of his passions.

The only useful passion then that man possesses toward the peace and quiet of a society is his fear, and the more you work upon it the more orderly and governable he will be; for however useful anger may be to man as he is a single creature by himself, yet society has no need for it whatsoever: But nature being always the same in the formation of animals, produces all creatures as like to those that beget and bear them as the place she forms them in, and the various influences from without, will allow her; and consequently all men, whether they are born in courts or forests, are susceptible to anger. When this passion overcomes (as among all degrees of people it sometimes does) the whole set of fears man has, he has true courage, and will fight as boldly as a lion or a tiger, and at no other time; and I shall endeavor to prove that whatever is called courage in man when he is not angry is spurious and artificial.

It is possible by good government to keep a society always quiet within itself, but nobody can ensure peace from without forever. The society may have occasion to extend their limits further and enlarge their territories, or others may invade theirs, or something else will happen so that man must be brought to fight; for however civilized men may be, they never forget that force goes beyond reason. The politician now must alter his measures and take off some of man's fears; he must strive to persuade him that all that was told him before of the barbarity of killing men ceases as soon as these men are enemies to the public, and that their adversaries are neither so good nor so strong as themselves. <meta>This page continues the discussion of how politicians recruit the most aggressive individuals for combat, transitioning from the limitations of natural anger to the necessity of a social equivalent for courage.</meta>

<header>Remark (R)</header>

hardiest, the most quarrelsome, and the most mischievous into combat; but unless they are better qualified, I won't answer for their behavior there. If you can once make them undervalue their enemies, you may soon stir them up to anger, and while that lasts they will fight with greater obstinacy than any disciplined troops. But if anything happens that was unforeseen—a sudden great noise, a storm, or any strange or uncommon accident that seems to threaten them—fear seizes them, disarms their anger, and makes every single one of them run away.

Therefore, as soon as people begin to have more wit, this natural courage must be quickly rejected. In the first place, those who have felt the sting of the enemy's blows will not always believe what is said to undervalue him, and they are often not easily provoked to anger. Secondly, since anger consists of an ebullition of the spirits, it is a passion of short duration (<note>Anger is a brief madness</note>), and if the enemies withstand the first shock of these angry people, they commonly get the better of them. Thirdly, as long as people are angry, all advice and discipline are lost on them, and they can never be brought to use skill or strategy in their battles. Anger, then—without which no creature has natural courage—is altogether useless in a war that must be managed by strategy and conducted as a formal art. Therefore, the government must find an equivalent for courage that will make men fight.

Whoever would civilize men and establish them into a <term>Body Politic</term> must be thoroughly acquainted with all the passions and appetites, strengths and weaknesses of their nature, and understand how to turn their greatest frailties to the advantage of the public. In the *Enquiry into the Origin of Moral Virtue*, I have shown how easily men were induced to believe anything said in their praise. If, therefore, a lawmaker is shrewd,

whom they greatly venerate, should tell them that the majority of men have within them a principle of valor distinct from anger, or any other passion, that made them despise danger and face death itself with intrepidity, and that those who had the most of it were the most valuable of their kind, it is very likely, considering what has been said, that most of them—though they felt nothing of this principle—would accept it as truth. Furthermore, the proudest among them, feeling themselves moved by this piece of flattery and not being well-versed in distinguishing between the passions, might imagine that they felt it heaving in their breasts, mistaking pride for courage. If only one in ten can be persuaded to openly declare that he possesses this principle and maintain it against all who deny it, there will soon be half a dozen more who will assert the same.

Whoever has once admitted to it is committed; the politician then has nothing to do but take all imaginable care to flatter the pride of those who brag of it, and are willing to stand by it, in a thousand different ways. The same pride that first drew him in will ever after oblige him to defend the assertion, until at last the fear of discovering the reality of his heart becomes so great that it surpasses the fear of death itself. Simply increase a man’s pride, and his fear of shame will always be proportional to it; for the greater value a man sets upon himself, the more effort he will take and the greater hardships he will undergo to avoid shame.

The great art, then, to make a man courageous is first to make him admit to this principle of valor within, and afterward to inspire him with as much horror of shame as nature has given him of death. That there are things to which man has, or may have, a stronger aversion than he has to death is evident from *suicide*. He who makes death his choice must look upon it as less terrible than what he avoids by it; for whether the

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that what is called "valor" is actually a social construct rooted in pride and the fear of shame, rather than an innate lack of fear.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>
<meta>catchword: con-</meta>

Whether the evil dreaded is present or to come, real or imaginary, no body would kill himself willfully except to avoid something. *Lucretia* held out bravely against all the attacks of the ravisher, even when he threatened her life; which shows that she valued her virtue beyond it. But when he threatened her reputation with eternal infamy, she fairly surrendered, and then killed herself—a certain sign that she valued her virtue less than her glory, and her life less than either. The fear of death did not make her yield, for she resolved to die before she did it, and her compliance must only be considered a bribe to make *Tarquin* refrain from sullying her reputation; so that life held neither the first nor second place in the esteem of *Lucretia*. The courage then which is only useful to the *body politic*, and what is generally called true valor, is artificial, and consists in a *superlative horror of shame, infused through flattery into men of exalted pride.*

As soon as the notions of honor and shame are accepted within a society, it is not difficult to make men fight. First, ensure they are persuaded of the justice of their cause; for no man fights heartily who thinks himself in the wrong. Then show them that their altars, their possessions, wives, children, and everything that is near and dear to them is at stake in the present quarrel, or at least may be influenced by it hereafter. Then put feathers in their caps and distinguish them from others; talk of public-spiritedness, the love of their country, facing an enemy with intrepidity, despising death, the "bed of honor," and such high-sounding words, and every proud man will take up arms and fight himself to death before he will turn tail, provided it is by daylight. One man in an army acts as a check upon another; and a hundred of them who would all be cowards if alone and without witnesses are made valiant by being together, for fear of incurring one another's contempt.

He explains that social leaders can motivate men to fight by appealing to their pride, justice, and the protective instincts of family and property.</summary>

<header>Remark (R.)</header>

continue and heighten this artificial courage, all who run away ought to be punished with ignominy; those who fought well, whether they won or were beaten, must be flattered and solemnly commended; those who lost their limbs rewarded, and those who were killed ought, above all, to be noticed, artfully lamented, and have extraordinary praises bestowed upon them; for to pay honors to the dead will always be a sure method to make <term>Bubbles</term> <note>dupes or fools</note> of the living.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When I say that the courage used in the wars is artificial, I do not imagine that by the same art all men may be made equally valiant: As men do not have an equal share of pride, and differ from one another in shape and internal structure, it is impossible they should all be equally fit for the same uses. Some men will never be able to learn music, and yet make good mathematicians; others will play excellently well upon the violin, and yet be <term>Coxcombs</term> <note>conceited fools</note> as long as they live, let them converse with whom they please. But to show that there is no evasion, I shall prove that—setting aside what I have already said of artificial courage—what the greatest hero differs in from the worst coward is altogether physical, and depends upon the inner makeup of man. What I mean is called *constitution*; by which is understood the orderly or disorderly mixture of the *fluids* in our body: That constitution which favors courage consists in the natural strength, elasticity, and proper arrangement of the finer <term>spirits</term> <note>animal spirits, then thought to be vital fluids in the nerves</note>, and upon them wholly depends what we call steadfastness, resolution, and obstinacy. It is the only ingredient that is common to natural and artificial bravery, and is to either what <term>Size</term> <note>a glue-like binder used in paint</note> is to whitewashed walls, which prevents them from peeling off and makes them lasting. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of courage, specifically how it can be cultivated through practice and driven by pride and vanity, building on the previous discussion of the physical "spirits" and "constitution."</meta>

<page-num>187</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>

There is nothing that more improves the useful martial courage I treat of, and at the same time shows it to be artificial, than practice. For when men are disciplined and come to be acquainted with all the tools of death and engines of destruction—when the shouts, the outcries, the fire and smoke, the groans of the wounded, and the ghastly looks of dying men, with all the various scenes of mangled carcasses and bloody limbs torn off, begin to be familiar to them—their fears diminish quickly. This is not because they are now less afraid to die than before, but because, being used to seeing the same dangers so often, they perceive the reality of them less than they once did. As they are deservedly valued for every siege they attend and every battle they are in, it is inevitable that the various actions they participate in must continually become many solid steps by which their pride ascends. Thus, their fear of shame—which, as I said before, will always be proportioned to their pride—increases as the perception of danger decreases. It is no wonder, then, that most of them learn to show little or no fear; and some great generals are able to preserve a presence of mind and feign a calm serenity in the midst of all the noise, horror, and confusion that accompany a battle.

Man is such a foolish creature that, intoxicated with the fumes of vanity, he can feast on the thoughts of the praise that shall be paid to his memory in future ages with such ecstasy as to neglect his present life; indeed, he may seek and desire death if he only imagines that it will add to the glory he had acquired before. There is no level of self-<unclear>denial</unclear> that a man of pride and constitution cannot reach, nor any passion so violent that he will not sacrifice it to another which is superior to it. I cannot help but marvel at the simplicity of some good men who, when they hear of the joy and serenity with which holy men in persecutions have suffered

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that human actions, often attributed to higher virtues like valor or piety, are frequently rooted in pride and natural temperament (constitution). Having previously compared religious martyrs to atheists, he now moves to the military as a prime example of how pride is systematically manipulated.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>
<header>Remark (R.)</header>

I have made this digression chiefly to show the strength of human nature, and what mere man may perform by pride and constitution alone. A man may certainly be as violently roused by his vanity as a lion is by his anger. Not only this, but avarice, revenge, ambition, and almost every passion—pity not excepted—when they are extraordinary, may, by overcoming fear, serve him instead of valor, and be mistaken for it even by himself; as daily experience must teach everyone who will examine and look into the motives from which some men act. But so that we may more clearly perceive what this pretended principle is really built upon, let us look into the management of military affairs, and we shall find that pride is nowhere so openly encouraged as there. As for clothes, even the lowest of the commission officers have them richer, or at least more gay and splendid, than are generally worn by other people with four or five times their income. Most of them, and especially those who have families and can hardly subsist, would be very glad, all over *Europe*, to be less expensive in that way; but it is a force put upon them to uphold their pride, which they do not realize.

But the ways and means to rouse a man's pride, and catch him by it, are nowhere more grossly conspicuous than in the treatment which the common soldiers receive, whose vanity is to be worked upon (because there must be so many of them) at the cheapest rate imaginable. We do not mind things we are accustomed to; otherwise, what mortal who had never seen a soldier could look without laughing upon a man accoutred with so much paltry gaudiness and affected finery? The coarsest fabric that can be made of wool, dyed a brick-dust color, is acceptable to him because it is an imitation of scarlet or crimson cloth; and to make him think himself as like his officer as is possible with little or no cost, instead of silver or gold lace, his

Knights-errant, of course. They did a great deal of
good throughout the world by taming monsters,

but since the wings of all the dragons have been clipped,

the Order of Chivalry, to which the standard of
ancient honor belonged, has been laid aside for some
time. It was like their armor, very massive and
heavy; the many virtues attached to it made it very
troublesome, and as ages grew wiser and wiser, the
principle of honor at the beginning of the last
century was melted down again and brought to a
new standard; they put in the same weight of
courage, half the quantity of honesty, and a very
little justice, but not a scrap of any other virtue,
which has made it very easy and portable compared to what
it was. However, such as it is, there would be no
living without it in a large nation; it is the tie of
society, and though we are indebted to our frailties
for the chief ingredient of it, there is no virtue—
at least that I am acquainted with—that has
been half so instrumental to the civilizing of mankind,
who in large societies would soon degenerate
into cruel villains and treacherous slaves were honor
to be removed from among them.

As to the dueling part which belongs to it, I
pity the unfortunate whose lot it is; but to say
that those who are guilty of it follow false rules
or mistake the notions of honor is ridiculous;
for either there is no honor at all, or it teaches
men to resent injuries and accept challenges;
you might as well deny that what you see everyone

# Remark (R.)

<page-num>172</page-num>

[...whatever] language he pleased, without being called to account for it, all conversation would be spoiled. Some serious people tell us that the *Greeks* and *Romans* were such valiant men, and yet knew nothing of dueling except in their country’s quarrel. This is very true, but for that reason the kings and princes in *Homer* gave one another worse language than our porters and hackney coachmen would be able to bear without resentment.

If you would hinder dueling, pardon nobody who offends in that way, and make the laws against it as severe as you can, but do not take away the thing itself—the custom of it. This will not only prevent the frequency of it, but likewise, by rendering the most resolute and most powerful cautious and circumspect in their behavior, will polish and brighten society in general. Nothing civilizes a man as much as his fear, and if not all (as my Lord *Rochester* said), at least most men would be cowards if they dared. The dread of being called to account keeps many in awe, and there are thousands of well-mannered and accomplished gentlemen in *Europe* who would have been insolent and unbearable coxcombs without it. Besides, if it were out of fashion to seek satisfaction for injuries which the law cannot take hold of, there would be twenty times the mischief done than there is now, or else you would need twenty times the constables and other officers to keep the peace. I confess that although it happens but seldom, it is a calamity to the people, and generally to the families it falls upon; but there can be no perfect happiness in this world, and all felicity has an alloy. The act itself is uncharitable, but when more than thirty in a nation destroy themselves in one year, and not half that number are killed by others, I do not think the people can be said to love their neighbors less than themselves. It is strange that a nation should grudge to see perhaps half a dozen men sacrificed

# Remark (R.)

<page-num>173</page-num>
<sig>I 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

in a year to obtain such a valuable blessing as the politeness of manners, the pleasure of conversation, and the happiness of company in general—a society that is often so willing to expose, and sometimes loses, as many thousands in a few hours without knowing whether it will do any good or not.

I would have no one who reflects on the lowly origin of honor complain of being gulled and made into a tool by cunning politicians, but rather desire everyone to be satisfied that the governors of societies and those in high stations are greater dupes of pride than any of the rest. If some great men did not have a superlative pride, and everyone understood the true enjoyment of life, who would be a Lord Chancellor of *England*, a Prime Minister of State in *France*, or—what causes more fatigue and offers not a sixth part of the profit of either—a Grand Pensionary of *Holland*? The reciprocal services which all men pay to one another are the foundation of society. The great ones are not flattered with their high birth for nothing; it is to rouse their pride and excite them to glorious actions that we extol their lineage, whether it deserves it or not. Some men have been complimented with the greatness of their family and the merit of their ancestors when, in the whole generation, you could not find two who were anything but uxorious fools, silly bigots, noted cowards, or debauched womanizers. The established pride that is inseparable from those who already possess titles often makes them strive as much not to seem unworthy of them as the active ambition of others who are yet without them renders them industrious and indefatigable in deserving them. When a gentleman is made a Baron or an Earl, it is as great a restraint upon him in many respects as a gown and cassock are to a young student who has been newly ordained.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the social mechanisms of pride and honor. # Remark (R.)

<page-num>174</page-num>
<sig>(S)</sig>

The only significant thing that can be said against modern honor is that it is directly opposite to religion. The one commands you to bear injuries with patience; the other tells you that if you do not resent them, you are not fit to live. Religion commands you to leave all revenge to God; honor bids you trust your revenge to nobody but yourself, even where the law would do it for you. Religion plainly forbids murder; honor openly justifies it. Religion commands you not to shed blood for any reason whatsoever; honor commands you to fight for the slightest trifle. Religion is built on humility, and honor upon pride. How to reconcile them must be left to wiser heads than mine.

The reason why there are so few men of real virtue, and so many of real honor, is because the only reward a man has for a virtuous action is the pleasure of doing it, which most people consider poor pay. However, the self-denial a man of honor submits to in one appetite is immediately rewarded by the satisfaction he receives from another; and what he reduces of his avarice, or any other passion, is doubly repaid to his pride. Besides, honor allows for a great deal of leeway, and virtue none. A man of honor must not cheat or tell a lie; he must punctually repay what he borrows at gambling, though the creditor has nothing to show for it; but he may drink, and swear, and owe money to all the tradesmen in town without taking notice of their demands for payment. A man of honor must be true to his prince and country while he is in their service; but if he thinks himself not well treated, he may quit and do them all the harm he can. A man of honor must never change his religion for self-interest, but he may be as debauched as he pleases and never practice any. He must make no attempts upon his friend's wife, daughter, sister, or anyone else trusted to his care, but he may sleep with all the rest of the world.

<summary>The author contrasts the worldly code of honor with religious teachings, arguing that honor is rooted in pride and allows for selective morality while religion demands humility and total restraint. He suggests honor is more popular than virtue because it provides immediate gratification to one's ego through its social permissions and double standards.</summary>
<keywords>honor, religion, virtue, pride, humility, revenge, murder, self-denial, avarice, social morality, gambling debts, loyalty</keywords>

# Remark (S.)

<page-num>175</page-num>

->(S) *No Painter for his art is famed,*<-

---

->Page 10. Line 27.<-

It is, without doubt, that among the consequences of national honesty and frugality, one would be to not build any new houses or use new materials as long as there were enough old ones to serve the purpose. Because of this, three-quarters of masons, carpenters, bricklayers, and others would lack employment; and the building trade being once destroyed, what would become of <term>limning</term> <note>Painting, specifically the decoration of buildings or manuscripts.</note>, carving, and other arts that serve luxury, and which have been carefully forbidden by those lawgivers who preferred a good and honest society to a great and wealthy one, and endeavored to make their subjects **virtuous** rather than **rich**? By a law of Lycurgus, it was enacted that the ceilings of Spartan houses should only be worked with an axe, and their gates and doors only smoothed by a saw; and this, says Plutarch, was not without a deeper meaning. For if Epaminondas could say with such good grace, while inviting some of his friends to his table: *“Come, gentlemen, be at ease; treason would never come to such a poor dinner as this,”* why might not this great lawgiver, in all probability, have thought that such plain houses would never be capable of receiving luxury and excess?

It is reported, as the same author tells us, that King Leotichidas, the first of that name, was so little used to the sight of carved work that, while being entertained in a stately room in Corinth, he was much surprised to see the timber and ceiling so finely worked, and asked his host whether the trees grew that way in his country.

<margin>I.</margin>Mistress never could have persuaded them to. I have shown already that the worst of women and most profligate of the sex did contribute to the consumption of superfluities, as well as the necessities of life, and consequently were beneficial to many peaceable drudges who work hard to maintain their families and have no worse intention than an honest livelihood. — Let them be banished regardless, says a good man: when every strumpet is gone and the land is wholly freed from lewdness, God Almighty will pour such blessings upon it as will vastly exceed the profits that are now earned by harlots. — This perhaps would be true; but I can make it evident that, with or without prostitutes, nothing could make amends for the damage trade would sustain if all those of that sex who enjoy the happy state of matrimony should act and behave themselves as a sober, wise man could wish them to.

The variety of work that is performed, and the number of hands employed to gratify the fickleness and luxury of women, is prodigious; and if only the married ones would listen to reason and just remonstrances <note>Formal statements of disapproval or objection.</note>, think themselves sufficiently answered with the first refusal, and never ask a second time for what had been once denied them: if, I say, married women would do this, and then lay out no money but what their husbands knew of and freely allowed, the consumption of a thousand things they now make use of would be lessened by at least a fourth part. If by this pattern we should proportionally judge the highest ranks, where the richest clothes are but a trifle compared to their other expenses—and not forget the furniture of all sorts, carriages, jewels, and buildings of persons of quality—we would find the "fourth part" I speak of to be a vast article in trade. The pleasure lies in the consciousness of an uncontrollable possession and what follows from it: the reflection on the mighty thoughts he imagines others have of his happiness. I beg pardon for this detour, and ask the experienced reader to weigh what has been said regarding the main purpose; and after that, remember the worldly blessings which men daily hear not only toasted and wished for when people are merry and idle, but also gravely and solemnly prayed for in churches and other religious assemblies by clergymen of all sorts and sizes. I never said, nor imagined, that man could not be virtuous just as well in a rich and mighty kingdom as in the most paltry commonwealth; but I admit it is my belief that no society can be raised into such a rich and mighty kingdom, or, once so raised, subsist in their wealth and power for any considerable time, without the vices of man.

This I imagine is sufficiently proved throughout the book; and as human nature still continues the same as it has always been for so many thousand years, we have no great reason to suspect a future change in it while the world endures. Now I cannot see what immorality there is in showing a man the origin and power of those passions which so often, even unknown to himself, hurry him away from his reason; or that there is any impiety in putting him on his guard against himself and the secret stratagems of self-love, and teaching him the difference between such actions as proceed from a victory over the passions and those that are only the result of a conquest which one passion obtains over another; that is, between real and counterfeited virtue. It is an admirable saying of a worthy clergyman, *that though many discoveries have been made in the world of self-love, there is yet an abundance of Terra Incognita left behind.* What harm do I do to man if I make him more known to himself than he was before? But we are all so desperately in love with flattery that we can never relish a truth that is mortifying, and I don’t believe that the immortality of the soul—a truth introduced long before Christianity—would have ever found such a general acceptance in hu-

<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s satirical argument regarding the "reform" of society. Having previously suggested that national wealth and power rely on "vices," he now ironically describes the drastic measures required to return a nation to a state of primitive "virtue" and agrarian simplicity.</meta>

<header>Remark (T.)</header>

...burn all the books on the island, except those at the universities, where they may remain undisturbed, and allow no volume in private hands but a Bible. Eliminate foreign trade, prohibit all commerce with foreigners, and permit no ships to go to sea that will ever return, beyond fishing boats. Restore to the clergy, the king, and the barons their ancient privileges, prerogatives, and possessions. Build new churches, and convert all the coins you can find into sacred utensils. Erect monasteries and alms-houses in abundance, and let no parish be without a charity school. Enact sumptuary laws <note>Laws designed to regulate consumption and restrain luxury</note>, and let your youth be accustomed to hardship. Inspire them with all the subtle and most refined notions of honor and shame, of friendship and of heroism, and introduce among them a great variety of imaginary rewards. Then let the clergy preach abstinence and self-denial to others, while taking whatever liberty they please for themselves; let them hold the greatest influence in the management of state affairs, and let no man be made Lord Treasurer but a bishop.

By such pious endeavors and wholesome regulations, the scene would soon be altered; the greatest part of the greedy, the discontented, the restless, and the ambitious villains would leave the land, and vast swarms of cheating rogues would abandon the city and be dispersed throughout the country. Craftsmen would learn to hold the plow, merchants would turn into farmers, and the sinful, overgrown Jerusalem <note>Likely used here as a metaphorical name for London</note>, without famine, war, plague, or compulsion, would be emptied in the easiest manner, and ever after cease to be a threat to her sovereigns. The happily reformed kingdom would, by this means, be crowded in no part of it, and everything necessary for the sustenance of man would be cheap and abundant. On the contrary, the root of so many thousand evils, money, would be very scarce, and just as little needed, where every man should enjoy

It is impossible that such a change of circumstances should not influence the manners of a nation and render them temperate, honest, and sincere; and from the next generation, we might reasonably expect a more healthy and robust offspring than the present—a harmless, innocent, and well-meaning people who would never dispute the doctrine of Passive Obedience <note>The political doctrine of total submission to the authority of a monarch</note>, nor any other orthodox principles, but be submissive to superiors and unanimous in religious worship.

Here I imagine myself interrupted by an *Epicure*, who, so as not to lack a restorative diet in case of necessity, is never without live *ortolans* <note>A small songbird considered a great delicacy</note>. I am told that goodness and probity are to be had at a cheaper rate than the ruin of a nation and the destruction of all the comforts of life; that liberty and property may be maintained without wickedness or fraud, and men can be good subjects without being slaves, and religious though they refused to be "priest-ridden"; that to be frugal and saving is a duty incumbent only on those whose circumstances require it, but that a man of a good estate does his country a service by living up to his income. As for himself, he says he is so much a master of his appetites that he can abstain from anything when necessary; that where true *Hermitage* <note>A prestigious French wine</note> was not to be had, he could content himself with plain *Bordeaux*, if it had a good body; that many a morning, instead of *St. Lawrence*, he has made do with *Fronteniac*, and after dinner served *Cyprus* wine, and even *Madeira*, when he has had a large company and thought it extravagant to treat them to *Tokay*; but that all voluntary mortifications are *superstitious*, belonging only to blind zealots and *enthusiasts* <note>In this era, "enthusiast" usually referred to a religious fanatic</note>. He will quote my Lord *Shaftesbury* against me and tell me that people may be virtuous and sociable.

And moreover, he will ask whether the most devout and most learned of our prelates, in their greatest concern for our conversion, when they beseech the Deity to turn their own hearts as well as ours from the world and all carnal desires, do not in the same prayer just as loudly solicit Him to pour all earthly blessings and temporal happiness on the kingdom to which they belong.

These are the apologies, the excuses, and common pleas—not only of those who are notoriously vicious, but of the generality of mankind—when you touch the copyhold <note>A form of land tenure; here used metaphorically to mean a person’s legal or established interest in their own habits and inclinations.</note> of their inclinations; and trying the real value they have for spiritual matters, you would actually strip them of what their minds are wholly bent upon. Ashamed of the many frailties they feel within, all men endeavor to hide themselves—their ugly nakedness—from each other; and wrapping up the true motives of their hearts in the specious cloak of sociability and their concern for the public good, they hope to conceal their filthy appetites and the deformity of their desires. They do this while they are inwardly conscious of their fondness for their darling lusts and their incapacity to tread the arduous, rugged path of virtue openly.

As to the two last questions, I admit they are very puzzling. To what the Epicure asks, I am obliged to answer in the affirmative; and unless I would (which God forbid!) challenge the sincerity of the bishops and the entire legislative power, the objection stands firm against me. All I can say for myself is that, in the connection of these facts, there is a mystery beyond human understanding; and to convince the reader that this is no evasion, I will illustrate its incomprehensibility in the following parable.

In old heathen times, they say there was a whimsical country where the people talked much of religion, and the majority, in outward appearance, seemed really devout. The chief moral evil among them was thirst, and to quench it was a damnable sin; yet they unanimously agreed that everyone was born thirsty, more or less. Some beer in moderation was allowed to all, and he was counted a hypocrite, a cynic, or a madman who pretended that one could live altogether without it; yet those who owned they loved it and drank it to excess were counted wicked. Meanwhile, the beer itself was reckoned a blessing from heaven, and there was no harm in using it; all the enormity lay in the abuse—the motivation of the heart that made them drink it. He who drank the least drop of it to quench his thirst committed a heinous crime, while others drank large quantities without any guilt, provided they did it indifferently, and for no other reason than to amend their complexion <note>In this context, to "amend their complexion" likely refers to drinking for the sake of health or physical appearance rather than for the pleasure of quenching thirst.</note>.

They brewed for other countries as well as their own, and for the small beer <note>Small beer is a term for weak or low-quality beer, often consumed by all ages in this period.</note> they sent abroad, they received large returns of Westphalia hams, ox tongues <note>The original text uses "Neats-Tongues," referring to the tongues of cattle.</note>, hung-beef, and Bologna sausages, red herrings, pickled sturgeon, caviar, anchovies, and everything that was suitable to make their beer go down with pleasure. # Remark (R.)

stores of small beer by them without making use of it, were generally envied and, at the same time, very odious to the public; and nobody was easy who did not have enough of it come to his own share. The greatest calamity they thought could befall them was to keep their hops and barley on their hands, and the more they consumed of them annually, the more they reckoned the country to flourish.

The government had made very wise regulations concerning the returns that were made for their exports, encouraged the importation of salt and pepper very much, and laid heavy duties on everything that was not well-seasoned and might in any way obstruct the sale of their own hops and barley. Those at the helm, when they acted in public, showed themselves on all accounts exempt and wholly divested of thirst, made several laws to prevent the growth of it, and punished the wicked who openly dared to quench it. If you examined them in their private persons, and pried narrowly into their lives and conversations, they seemed to be more fond of—or at least drank larger drafts of—small beer than others, but always under the pretense that mending their complexions required greater quantities of liquor for them than it did for those they ruled over; and that what they chiefly had at heart, without any regard for themselves, was to procure a great plenty of small beer among the subjects in general and a great demand for their hops and barley.

As nobody was debarred from small beer, the clergy made use of it as well as the laity, and some of them very plentifully; yet all of them desired to be thought less thirsty because of their function than others, and would never own that they drank any except to mend their complexions. In their religious assemblies they were more sincere; for as soon as they came there, they all openly confessed,

What was remarkable is, that to have laid hold of those truths to anyone's prejudice, and made use of those confessions afterwards out of their temples would have been counted very impertinent, and everyone thought it a heinous affront to be called <unclear>a</unclear> drinker, though you had seen him drink small beer by the gallons. The chief topics of their preachers were the great evil of thirst, and the folly their followers showed in quenching it. They exhorted their hearers to resist the temptations of it, inveighed against small beer, and often told them it was poison, if they drank it with pleasure, or any other design than to mend their complexions.

In their acknowledgments to the gods, they thanked them for the plenty of comfortable small beer they had received from them, notwithstanding they had so little deserved it, and continually quenched their thirst with it; whereas they were so thoroughly satisfied, that it was given them for a better use. Having begged pardon for those offenses, they desired the gods to lessen their thirst and give them strength to resist the importunity of it; yet, in the midst of their sorest repentance and most humble supplications, they never lacked small beer, and prayed that they might continue to have it in great plenty, with a solemn promise that how neglectful soever they might hitherto have been in this point, they would for the future not drink a drop of it with any other design than to mend their complexions.

These were standing petitions put together at last; and having continued to be made use of without any alterations for several hundred years...

## (V.) *Contentment, the Bane of Industry.*

->Page 11. Line 24.<-

I have been told by many that the bane of industry is laziness, and not contentment; therefore, to prove my assertion, which seems like a paradox to some, I shall treat laziness and contentment separately, and afterwards speak of industry, so that the reader may judge which of the two former is most opposite to the latter.

Laziness is an aversion to work, generally accompanied by an unreasonable desire to remain inactive; and everyone is lazy who, without being hindered by any other justifiable employment, refuses or puts off any work that he ought to do for himself or others. We seldom call anyone lazy except those we consider inferior to us and from whom we expect some service. Children do not think their parents are lazy, nor do servants think so of their masters; and if a gentleman indulges his ease and sloth so shamefully that he will not put on his own

Mr. *Dryden* has given us a very good idea of superlative slothfulness in the person of a lazy King of *Egypt*. His Majesty, having bestowed considerable gifts on several of his favorites, was attended by some of his chief ministers with parchment which he was to sign to confirm <unclear>those</unclear> grants. First, he walks a few turns <unclear>to and fro</unclear> with a heavy uneasiness in his looks; then he <unclear>throws</unclear> himself down like a man who is tired, and <unclear>with</unclear> an abundance of reluctance for what he was about to do, he takes up the pen and begins complaining very seriously about the length of the name *Ptolemy*. He expresses a great deal of concern <unclear>that</unclear> he did not have some short monosyllable for his name, which he thought would save him a world of trouble.

We often reproach others for laziness <unclear>because</unclear> we are guilty of it ourselves. Some days <unclear>ago</unclear>, two young women sat knotting <note>A decorative form of handiwork involving making knots in thread to create fringe or lace</note> together. <unclear>One says</unclear> to the other, "There comes a wicked cold <unclear>through</unclear> that door; you are the nearest to it, sister, <unclear>please</unclear> shut it." The other, who was the younger, deigned indeed to cast an eye toward the door, <unclear>but</unclear> sat still and said nothing. The eldest spoke <unclear>once</unclear>, two, or three times, and at last, when the other <unclear>made</unclear> her no answer nor offered to stir, she got <unclear>up in a</unclear> pet <note>A fit of irritation or ill humor</note> and shut the door herself. Coming back <unclear>and sitting</unclear> down again, she gave the younger a very hard <unclear>look</unclear> and said, "Lord, Sister Betty, I would not be as <unclear>lazy as</unclear> you are for all the world!" She spoke this so earnestly that it brought a color to her face. I admit <unclear>the</unclear> younger should have risen; but if the <unclear>eldest</unclear> had not overvalued her own effort, she <unclear>would</unclear> have shut the door herself as soon as the <unclear>cold</unclear> was offensive to her, without making any words about it. She was not more than a step farther from the door.

...than her sister, and as to age, there was not eleven months' difference between them, and they were both under twenty. I thought it a difficult matter to determine which was the lazier of the two.

There are a thousand wretches who are always working the marrow out of their bones for next to nothing because they are unthinking and ignorant of what the pains they take are worth; while others, who are cunning and understand the true value of their work, refuse to be employed at lower rates, not because they are of an inactive temperament, but because they will not beat down the price of their labor. A country gentleman sees at the back side of the Exchange <note>The Royal Exchange in London, a center for commerce</note> a porter walking to and fro with his hands in his pockets. "Pray," says he, "friend, will you step for me with this letter as far as Bow Church, and I'll give you a penny." "I'll go with all my heart," says the other, "but I must have two-pence, Master"; which the gentleman refusing to give, the fellow turned his back and told him he would rather play for nothing than work for nothing.

The gentleman thought it an unaccountable piece of laziness in a porter to rather saunter up and down for nothing than to be earning a penny with so little trouble. Some hours later, he happened to be with some friends at a tavern in Threadneedle Street, where one of them, calling to mind that he had forgotten to send for a bill of exchange <note>A written order to pay a specific sum of money</note> that was to go away with the post that night, was in great perplexity and immediately wanted someone to go for him to Hackney with all the speed imaginable. It was after ten, in the middle of winter, a very rainy night, and all the porters thereabouts had gone to bed. The gentleman grew very uneasy and said that whatever it cost him, he must send someone; at last, one of the drawers <note>Tavern servants who draw liquor for guests</note>, seeing him so very insistent, told him that he knew a porter who would get up if it was a job worth his while. "Worth his while?" said the gentleman very eagerly,

### Remark (V.)

Contentment I call that calm serenity of the mind which men enjoy while they think themselves happy and rest satisfied with the station they are in: it implies a favorable interpretation of our present circumstances and a peaceful tranquility, which men are strangers to as long as they are anxious about improving their condition. This is a virtue for which praise is very precarious and uncertain: for according to how men's circumstances vary, they will either be blamed or commended for possessing it.

A single man who works hard at a laborious trade has a hundred a year left to him by a relation: this change of fortune soon makes him weary of working, and not having enough industry to advance himself in the world, he resolves to do nothing at all and live upon his income. As long as he lives within his means, pays for what he has, and offends nobody, he shall be called an honest, quiet man. The victualler <note>A person licensed to sell food and drink; an innkeeper.</note>, his landlady, the tailor, and others divide what he has between them, and society is every year the better for his revenue; whereas, if he should follow his own or any other trade, he would hinder others, and somebody would have less for what he should earn; and therefore, though he should be the laziest fellow in the world, lie in bed fifteen hours out of twenty-four, and do nothing but saunter up and down all the rest of the time, nobody would criticize him, and his inactive spirit is honored with the name of **Content**.

But if the same man marries, gets three or four children, and still continues with the same easy temperament, remains satisfied with what he has, and without endeavoring to earn a penny, indulges his former sloth: first his relations, and afterwards all his acquaintances, will be alarmed at his negligence; they foresee that his income will not be sufficient to bring up so many children respectably, and are

When these fears have been whispered about from one to another for some time, his Uncle Gripe takes him to task and accosts him in the following moralizing talk: *“What, nephew, no business yet! Shame on you! I cannot imagine how you manage to spend your time; if you won't work at your own trade, there are fifty ways that a man may earn a penny. You have a hundred a year, it is true, but your expenses increase every year, and what will you do when your children are grown up? I have a larger estate than you myself, and yet you don't see me leave off my business; indeed, I declare it, if I could have the world I could not lead the life you do. It is no business of mine, I admit, but everybody says it is a shame for a young man like you, who has his limbs and his health, not to turn his hands to something or other.”* If these admonitions do not reform him in a short time, and he continues half a year longer without employment, he will become the talk of the whole neighborhood. For the same qualities that once earned him the name of a quiet, contented man, he shall be called the worst of husbands and the laziest fellow on earth. From this it is manifest that when we pronounce actions good or evil, we only regard the harm or benefit the society receives from them, and not the person who performs them. (See <unclear>Fable</unclear>...)

<warning>Heavy ink blotting and fading along the left margin obscures the first few letters of many lines; text has been reconstructed based on context.</warning>

industrious; and they must be either ingenious enough to find out uncommon, and yet justifiable methods to increase their business or their profit, or else make up for that deficiency by a variety of occupations. If a tradesman takes care to stock his shop and gives proper attention to those who come to it, he is a diligent man in his business; but if, besides that, he takes particular pains to sell a better product than the rest of his neighbors for the same profit, or if by his eagerness to please, or some other good quality, gaining a large circle of acquaintances, he uses all possible efforts to draw customers to his house, he then may be called industrious. A cobbler, even if he is not employed half of his time, is a diligent man if he neglects no business and works quickly when he has any; but if he runs errands when he has no work, or makes nothing but shoe-pegs, and serves as a watchman at night, he deserves the name of industrious.

If what has been said in this Remark is duly weighed, we shall find either that laziness and contentment are very closely related, or if there is a great difference between them, that the latter is more contrary to industry than the former.

I have heard people speak of the mighty figure the Spartans made above all

<meta>This page continues Remark (X), illustrating the argument that national greatness often requires a lack of individual luxury by using the example of ancient Sparta.</meta>

the Commonwealths of *Greece*, despite their unusual frugality and other exemplary virtues. But certainly, there was never a nation whose greatness was emptier than theirs: the splendor in which they lived was inferior to that of a theater, and the only thing they could be proud of was that they enjoyed nothing. They were indeed both feared and esteemed abroad: they were so famous for valor and skill in military affairs that their neighbors not only sought their friendship and assistance in their wars, but were satisfied and thought themselves certain of victory if they could only get a *Spartan* general to command their armies. But then their discipline was so rigid, and their manner of living so austere and void of all comfort, that the most temperate man among us would refuse to submit to the harshness of such strange laws. There was a perfect equality among them: gold and silver coins were forbidden; their current money was made of iron, to make it bulky and of little value. To store twenty or thirty pounds required a fairly large room, and to move it, nothing less than a yoke of oxen. Another remedy they had against luxury was that they were obliged to eat the same food in common, and they so rarely allowed anyone to dine or sup by himself at home that *Agis*, one of their kings, having conquered the *Athenians* and sending for his rations upon his return home (because he desired to eat privately with his queen), was refused by the *Polemarchi*.

In training their youth, their chief concern, says *Plutarch*, was to make them good subjects, to <unclear>teach</unclear> them to endure the fatigues of long and tedious marches, and never to return without victory <unclear>from</unclear> the field. When they were twelve years old, <unclear>they</unclear> lived in small groups on beds made of <unclear>the</unclear> rushes which grew by the banks of the <unclear>River</unclear> *Eurotas*; and because their points were sharp, <unclear>they</unclear>

From all these circumstances, it is plain that no nation on earth was less effeminate; but being barred from all the comforts of life, they could have nothing for their efforts but the glory of being a warlike people accustomed to toils and hardships, which was a happiness that few people would have cared for on the same terms. And even though they had been masters of the world, as long as they enjoyed no more of it than that, *Englishmen* would hardly have envied them their greatness. What people desire nowadays has been sufficiently shown in Remark (O), where I have treated real pleasures.

# Remark (Y.)

...expects in eight or ten years' time to keep his coach; notwithstanding this, he hopes that after he has slaved (as he calls it) for twenty-two or twenty-three years, he shall be worth at least a thousand a year for his eldest son to inherit, and two or three thousand pounds for each of his other children to start their lives with; and when men in such circumstances pray for their "daily bread" and mean nothing more extravagant by it, they are counted as quite modest people. Call this pride, luxury, superfluity, or whatever you please, it is nothing but what ought to be in the capital of a flourishing nation: those of inferior condition must content themselves with less costly conveniences, just as others of higher rank will be sure to make theirs more expensive. Some people call it merely decency to be served on silver plate and reckon a coach and six horses among the necessary comforts of life; and if a peer has no more than three or four thousand a year, his lordship is considered poor.

Since the first edition of this book, several people have attacked me with demonstrations of the certain ruin which excessive luxury must bring upon all nations; yet they were soon answered when I showed them the limits within which I had confined it. Therefore, so that no reader in the future may misconstrue me on this point, I shall point out the cautions I have given and the provisos I have made in the former as well as this present edition; if these are not overlooked, they must prevent all rational censure and remove several objections that might otherwise be made against me. I have laid down as maxims never to be abandoned: that the † poor should be kept strictly at work, and that it was prudent to relieve their wants, but folly to cure them; <unclear>and that</unclear> <unclear>as they ought to be kept from starving, so they ought to receive nothing</unclear> <unclear>but what they can earn by their daily labor.</unclear>

Agriculture * and fishing should be promoted in all their branches in order to make provisions, and consequently labor, cheap. I have named ‡ ignorance as a necessary ingredient in the mixture of society; from all of this, it is clear that I could never have imagined that luxury was to be spread generally through every part of a kingdom. I have likewise required † that property should be well-secured, justice impartially administered, and that the interest of the nation be taken care of in everything. But what I have insisted on the most, and repeated more than once, is the great regard that must be given to the balance of trade, and the care the legislature ought to take so that yearly || imports never exceed exports; and where this is observed, and the other things I mentioned are not neglected, I still continue to assert that no foreign luxury can ruin a country. The height of luxury is never seen except in nations that are vastly populous, and then only in the upper classes; and the larger that class is, the larger still in proportion must be the lowest class—the basis that supports everything: the multitude of the working poor.

Those who try to imitate too closely others of superior fortune have only themselves to thank if they are ruined. This is no argument against luxury, for whoever cannot support himself and yet lives beyond his income is a fool. Some people of high rank may keep three or four coaches and six horses and, at the same time, save money for their children; while a young shopkeeper is ruined by keeping one poor horse. It is impossible for there to be a rich nation without prodigals; yet I never knew a city so full of spendthrifts that there were not also enough covetous people to

He emphasizes that individual financial ruin caused by overspending is a personal failure, not a systemic flaw of luxury itself.</summary>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical and economic argument regarding the necessity of shifts in fortune and the balance of vices within a society. It concludes a point from the previous page concerning the financial risks taken by those of different social stations and begins a summary of how individual losses contribute to the collective movement of the "body politic."</meta>

# Remark (Y.)

...pay for their number. Just as an old merchant goes bankrupt after having been extravagant or careless for a long time, so a young beginner entering the same business acquires a fortune by being frugal or more industrious before he is forty years old. Besides that, the frailties of men often work in opposite ways: some small-minded people can never thrive because they are too stingy, while shrewder minds amass great wealth by spending their money freely and appearing to despise it.

But the shifts in fortune are necessary, and even the most lamentable ones are no more detrimental to society than the deaths of its individual members. Christenings are a proper balance to burials. Those who immediately lose by the misfortunes of others are very sorry; they complain and make a noise. But others who profit from them—as there are always such people—remain silent, because it is offensive to be thought better off for the losses and calamities of our neighbor. 

The various ups and downs compose a wheel that, by always turning, gives motion to the whole machine. Philosophers, who dare to extend their thoughts beyond the narrow scope of what is immediately before them, view the alternate changes in civil society no differently than they do the rising and falling of the lungs; the latter is as much a part of respiration in the more perfect animals as the former. Thus, the fickle breath of never-stable fortune is to the body politic the same as floating air is to a living creature.

Avarice, then, and prodigality are equally necessary to society. That men are generally more lavish in some countries than in others results from the difference in circumstances that dispose them to either vice; these arise from the condition of the social body as well as the temperament of the natural one. I beg pardon of the attentive reader if, here on behalf of those with short memories, I repeat some...

<summary>The author argues that the constant fluctuations of individual wealth and the existence of both avarice and prodigality are essential for the movement and health of society. He compares these social shifts to the biological necessity of breathing, where every loss for one individual serves as a gain for another and keeps the "machine" of the body politic in motion.</summary>
<keywords>economic cycles, bankruptcy, fortune, body politic, avarice, prodigality, social balance, philosophy of trade</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's analysis of the economic forces of avarice and prodigality, following the discussion of how individual financial shifts drive the "body politic." It specifically addresses the conditions that lead to these behaviors and the futility of national frugality in a wealthy nation.</meta>

# Remark (Y.)

<sig>K 5</sig>

...things, the substance of which they have already seen in Remark (Q.). More money than land, heavy taxes and scarcity of provisions, industry, laboriousness, and an active and stirring spirit, ill nature and a saturnine <note>gloomy or sluggish</note> temper; old age, wisdom, trade, riches acquired by our own labor, and well-secured liberty and property are all things that lead toward avarice. On the contrary, indolence, contentment, good nature, a jovial temper, youth, folly, arbitrary power, money easily obtained, plenty of provisions, and the uncertainty of possessions are circumstances that make men prone to prodigality. Where the first set of traits predominates, the prevailing vice will be avarice; where the others tip the scale, it will be prodigality. However, a national frugality has never existed, nor will it ever exist, without a national necessity.

Sumptuary laws <note>laws that limit private expenditure on food and luxury items</note> may be of use to an impoverished country after the great calamities of war, pestilence, or famine, when work has stopped and the labor of the poor has been interrupted; but to introduce them into an opulent kingdom is the wrong way to look out for its interests. I shall end my remarks on the *Grumbling Hive* by assuring the champions of national frugality that it would be impossible for the *Persians* and other *Eastern* people to purchase the vast quantities of fine *English* cloth they consume if we were to load our women with smaller cargoes of *Asiatic* silks.
