## AN
# ESSAY
#### ON
# CHARITY,
##### AND
# CHARITY-SCHOOLS

Charity is that virtue by which part of that sincere love we have for ourselves is transferred, pure and unmixed, to others who are not tied to us by the bonds of friendship or blood relation, and even to mere strangers to whom we have no obligation, nor from whom we hope for or expect anything. If we diminish the rigor of this definition in any way, part of the virtue must be lost. What we do for our friends and relatives, we do partly for ourselves: when a man acts on behalf of nephews or nieces and says, "They are my brother's children; I do it out of charity," he deceives you. For if he is capable, it is expected of him, and he does it partly for his own sake; if he values the world’s esteem and is particular regarding honor and reputation, he is obliged to have a <unclear>regard</unclear>

<summary>Bernard Mandeville defines charity as a purely disinterested love for strangers, arguing that helping family or friends is driven by self-interest and social expectation rather than true virtue.</summary>
<keywords>charity, virtue, self-love, kinship, friendship, social reputation, altruism, Bernard Mandeville</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues Bernard Mandeville's "An Essay on Charity, and Charity-Schools," further exploring the definition of charity in both thought and action, while distinguishing it from the emotion of pity.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools</header>
<warning>Significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side and high contrast distortion obscuring text readability throughout the page.</warning>

greater regard for them than for strangers, or else his character will suffer.

The exercise of this virtue relates either to opinion or to action, and is manifested in what we think of others or what we do for them. To be charitable, then, in the first place, we ought to put the best possible interpretation on everything that others do or say. If a man builds a fine house (even if he does not have a single symptom of humility), furnishes it richly, and spends a large fortune on silverware and paintings, we should not think that he does it out of vanity, but to encourage artists, employ laborers, and set the poor to work for the good of his country. And if a man sleeps in church, as long as he does not snore, we should think he closes his eyes to increase his attention. The reason is that, in our turn, we desire that our own extreme greed should pass for frugality, and that what we know to be hypocrisy should pass for religion. Secondly, that virtue is conspicuous in us when we bestow our time and labor for nothing, or use our influence with others on behalf of those who stand in need of it, yet could not expect such assistance from our friendship or family ties. The last branch of charity consists in giving away (while we are alive) what we value as much as the things I have already named; being content to possess and enjoy less, rather than fail to relieve those in need who are the objects of our choice.

This virtue is often counterfeited by a passion of ours called pity or compassion, which consists of a shared feeling and sympathy for the misfortunes and calamities of others. All mankind is more or less affected by it, but the weakest minds generally the most. It is stirred within us when the suffering and misery of other creatures make such a powerful impression upon us that they make us uneasy. It enters through either the eye or the ear, or both; and working

<summary>Mandeville describes charity as putting the best possible interpretation on others' actions and giving resources to strangers. He also distinguishes true charity from pity, which he characterizes as an emotional impulse that primarily affects "weak minds."</summary>
<keywords>charity, pity, compassion, social virtue, hypocrisy, public good, Mandeville, altruism</keywords>

the nearer and more violently the object of com-
passion strikes those senses, the greater disturbance

Should any one of us be locked up in a ground-
floor room, where in a yard joining to it there was a
thriving, good-humored child at play, of two or
three years old, so near us that through the grates
of the window we could almost touch it with our
hand; and if while we took delight in the harm-
less diversion and imperfect prattle of the
innocent babe, a nasty, overgrown sow should come
in upon the child, set it to screaming, and frighten it
out of its wits; it is natural to think that this would
make us uneasy, and that by crying out and mak-

happen to be a half-starved creature that, mad

shadow of self-interest, as this pity would be cleared and distinct from all other passions. There would be no need of virtue or self-denial to be moved at such a scene; and not only a man of humanity, of good morals and compassion, but likewise a highwayman, a housebreaker, or a murderer would feel anxiety on such an occasion. However calamitous a man’s circumstances might be, he would forget his misfortunes for the time, and the most troublesome passion would give way to pity; not one of our species has a heart so hardened or preoccupied that it would not ache at such a sight, for which no language has an epithet to fit it.

Many will wonder at what I have said of pity—that it enters through the eye or ear—but the truth of this will be known when we consider that the nearer the object is, the more we suffer, and the more remote it is, the less we are troubled by it. To see people executed for crimes, if it is from a great distance, moves us but little in comparison to what it does when we are near enough to see the movement of the soul in their eyes, observe their fears and agonies, and are able to read the pangs in every feature of the face. When the object is quite removed from our senses, the account of the calamities or the reading of them can never raise in us the passion called pity. We may be concerned by bad news, the loss and misfortunes of friends, and those whose cause we support, but this is not pity; it is grief or sorrow, the same as we feel for the death of those we love or the destruction of what we value.

When we hear that three or four thousand men, strangers to us, are killed with the sword or forced into some river where they are drowned, we say, and perhaps believe, that we pity them. If humanity bids us have compassion for the sufferings of others, and reason tells us that whether the thing is far off or done in our sight, our senti-

"He is a cruel man; he has no bowels of compassion": all these descriptions are the effects of reason and humanity, but nature makes no polite gestures. When the object is not seen, the body does not feel it; and when men talk of pitying people who are out of sight, they should be believed in the same way as when they say that they are our humble servants. In paying the usual civilities upon first meeting, those who do not see one another every day are often very glad and very sorry alternately five or six times within less than two minutes, and yet at parting, they carry away not a bit more grief or joy than they arrived with. It is the same with pity, which is no more a matter of choice than fear or anger. Those who have a strong and lively imagination, and can create representations of things in their minds as they would be if they were actually before them, may work themselves up into something that resembles compassion; but this is done by art—and often with the help of a little enthusiasm—and is only an imitation of pity. The heart feels little of it, and it is as faint as what we suffer while watching a tragedy performed, where our judgment (the <unclear>best</unclear> part of the mind) remains <unclear>unconcerned</unclear>; to indulge a lazy playfulness, judgment allows itself to be led into a state of terror, which is necessary to raise a passion. The slight touches of this terror are not unpleasant to us when the soul is in an idle, inactive mood.

As pity is often mistaken by ourselves and in our own cases for charity, so it is sometimes the case that it borrows the very name of it. A beggar <unclear>bids</unclear> you to exercise that virtue for Jesus Christ’s sake, but all the while, his great design is to arouse pity. He presents to your view the <unclear>worst</unclear> of his <unclear>ailments</unclear> and bodily infirmities; in a <unclear>few</unclear> words, he gives you a summary of his calamities.

How oddly are we managed by *self-love*! It is ever watching in our defense, and yet, to soothe a predominant *passion*, it obliges us to act against our *interest*: For when *pity* seizes us, if we can but imagine that we contribute to the *relief* of him with whom we have *compassion*, and are instrumental in lessening his *sorrows*, it eases us, and therefore pitiful *people* often give *alms* when they really feel that they would rather not.

When *sores* are very exposed or seem otherwise afflicting in an extraordinary manner, and the *beggar* can bear to have them exposed to the cold air, it is very shocking to some *people*; "it is a *shame*," they cry, "that such sights should be allowed"; the main *reason* is, it touches their *pity* feelingly, and at the same time they are resolved—either because they are *covetous*, or count it a useless *expense*—to give nothing, which makes them more uneasy. They turn their *eyes*; and where the *cries* are dismal, some would willingly stop their *ears*, if they were not ashamed. The only thing the industrious beggar has left to conquer these fortified hearts—if he can walk either with or without crutches—is to follow closely and, with uninterrupted noise, tease and importune them, or try if he can make them buy their peace. Thus, thousands give money to beggars from the same motive as they pay their corn-cutter <note>A person who treats corns or calluses on the feet.</note>: to walk easily. And many a halfpenny is given to impudent and intentionally persecuting rascals whom, if it could be done handsomely, a man would cane with much greater satisfaction. Yet all this, by the courtesy of the country, is called charity.

The reverse of pity is malice; I have spoken of it where I treat of envy. Those who know what it is to examine themselves will soon admit that it is very difficult to trace the root and origin of this passion. It is one of those we are most ashamed of, and therefore its harmful part is easily subdued and corrected by a judicious education. When anyone near us stumbles, it is natural—even before reflection—to stretch out our hands to prevent or at least break the fall, which shows that while we are calm, we are rather inclined toward pity. But though malice by itself is little to be feared, yet when assisted by pride, it is often mischievous, and it becomes most terrible when egged on and heightened by anger. There is nothing that more readily or more effectively extinguishes pity than this mixture, which is called cruelty. From this we may learn that to perform a meritorious action, it is not sufficient merely to conquer a passion unless it is also done from a laudable principle; and consequently, how necessary that clause was in my definition of virtue: that our endeavors must proceed from a rational ambition of being good, as

Pity, as I have said somewhere else, is the most amiable of all our passions, and there are not many occasions on which we ought to conquer or curb it. A surgeon may be as compassionate as he pleases, provided it does not make him omit or refrain from performing what he ought to do. Judges and juries likewise may be influenced by pity, if they take care that plain laws and justice itself are not infringed and do not suffer by it. No pity does more mischief in the world than that which is excited by the tenderness of parents, which hinders them from managing their children as their rational love for them would require, and as they themselves could wish. The influence that this passion also holds over the affections of women is more significant than is commonly imagined; they daily commit faults that are altogether ascribed to lust, yet are in a great measure caused by pity.

What I named last is not the only passion that mocks and resembles charity; pride and vanity have built more hospitals than all the virtues together. Men are so tenacious of their possessions, and selfishness is so ingrained in our nature, that whoever can in any way conquer it shall have the applause of the public, and every imaginable encouragement to conceal his weakness and soothe any other appetite he may wish to indulge. The man who provides from his private fortune what the whole community must otherwise have provided for places every member of society under an obligation; therefore, the whole world is ready to pay him their acknowledgment and thinks itself duty-bound to pronounce all such actions virtuous, without examining or even looking into the motives from which they were performed. Nothing is more destructive to virtue or religion itself than to make men believe that giving money to the poor—even if they do not part with it until after death—will make a full atonement in the

...next world, for the sins they have committed in this. A villain who has been guilty of a barbarous murder may, by the help of false witnesses, escape the punishment he deserved. He prospers, we say, heaps up great wealth, and by the advice of his father confessor leaves all his estate to a monastery, and his children beggars. What amends has this good Christian made for his crime, and what an honest man was the priest who directed his conscience? He who parts with all he has in his lifetime, whatever principle he acts from, only gives away what was his own; but the rich miser who refuses to assist his nearest relations while he is alive, though they never intentionally disobliged him, and disposes of his money for what we call charitable uses after his death, may imagine whatever he pleases regarding his goodness, but he robs his posterity. I am now thinking of a recent instance of charity, a prodigious gift that has made a great noise in the world: I have a mind to set it in the light I think it deserves, and beg leave, if not to please pedants, to treat it somewhat rhetorically.

That a man with small skill in <term>physick</term> <note>medicine</note>, and hardly any learning, should by vile arts get into practice and lay up great wealth, is no great wonder; but that he should so deeply work himself into the good opinion of the world as to gain the general esteem of a nation, and establish a reputation beyond all his contemporaries, with no other qualities but a perfect knowledge of mankind and a capacity for making the most of every <unclear>thing, is</unclear> something extraordinary. Charitable opinions are often stupidly false; when men are dead and gone, we ought to judge their actions as we do books, and neither wrong their understanding nor our own. The *British Aesculapius* <note>A reference to the wealthy physician Dr. John Radcliffe</note> was undeniably a man of sense, and if he had been influenced by charity, a public spirit, or the love of learning, and had aimed at the good of mankind in general, or that of his own profession in particular, and acted from any of these principles, he could never have made such a will; because so much wealth might have been better managed, and a man of much less capacity would have found out several better ways of laying out the money. 

But if we consider that he was as undeniably a man of vast pride as he was a man of sense, and give ourselves leave only to surmise that this extraordinary gift might have proceeded from such a motive, we shall presently discover the excellence of his abilities <note>parts</note> and his consummate knowledge of the world: for, if a man would render himself immortal, be ever praised and deified after his death, and have all the acknowledgment, the honors, and compliments paid to his memory that vainglory herself could wish for, I do not think it is within human skill to invent a more effective method. <meta>This page continues the argument that the physician’s large legacy to the university was a calculated move to secure eternal fame and adoration, regardless of his actual personal virtues.</meta>

# An Essay on Charity

Of all this, our subtle benefactor was not ignorant; he understood universities, their character, and their politics, and from that, he foresaw and knew that the incense to be offered to him would not cease with the present or a few succeeding generations. He knew that it would not last only for the trifling space of three or four hundred years, but that it would continue to be paid to him through all changes and revolutions of government and religion, as long as the nation exists and the island itself remains.

It is deplorable that the proud should have such temptations to wrong their lawful heirs. But when a man in ease and affluence, brimful of vainglory and humored in his pride by the greatest of a polite nation, has such an infallible security <term>in petto</term> <note>meaning "in his own heart" or "in secret"</note> for an everlasting homage and adoration to be paid to his <term>manes</term> <note>the spirit of a deceased person</note> in such an extraordinary manner, he is like a hero in battle, who, in feasting on his own imagination, tastes all the felicity of enthusiasm. It buoys him up in sickness, relieves him in pain, and either guards him against or keeps from his view all the terrors of death and the most dismal apprehensions of the future.

Should it be said that to be thus censorious, and to look into matters and men's consciences with that scrutiny, will discourage people from laying out their money in this way; and that, regardless of the money and the motive of the donor, he who receives the benefit is the gainer, I will not deny the charge. However, I am of the opinion that this is no injury to the public if one should prevent men from crowding too much treasure into the "dead stock" of the kingdom. There ought to be a vast disproportion between the active and inactive part of the society to make it happy; where this is not regarded, the multitude of <unclear>Colleges</unclear> and endowments may soon be excessive and <unclear>detrimental</unclear> to a nation. Charity, where it is <unclear>misapplied</unclear>

<summary>The author argues that massive charitable bequests to universities are often motivated by the donor's desire for eternal fame rather than true virtue. He warns that such endowments can turn productive wealth into "dead stock" and potentially harm the nation if they become excessive.</summary>

How perverse must be the judgment of those who would not rather see children decently dressed, with clean linen at least once a week, who follow their master to church in an orderly manner, than in every open place meet with a company of blackguards without shirts or anything whole about them, who, insensible of their misery, are continually increasing it with oaths and imprecations! Can anyone doubt that these are the great nursery of thieves and pickpockets? What numbers of felons and other criminals have we tried and convicted every session! This will be prevented by charity schools, and when the children of the poor receive a better education, society will in a few years reap the benefit of it, and the nation be cleared of so many miscreants as now this great city and all the country about it are filled with.

This is the general cry, and he that speaks the least word against it is called an uncharitable, hard-hearted, and inhuman—if not a wicked, profane, and atheistical—wretch. As to the comeliness of the sight, nobody disputes it, but I would not have a nation pay too dear for so transient a pleasure; and if we might set aside the finery of the show, everything that is material in this popular oration might soon be answered.

As to religion, the most knowledgeable and refined <unclear>Part</unclear> of a nation everywhere has the least of it; <unclear>Craft</unclear> has a greater hand in making rogues than stupidity, and vice in general is nowhere more predominant than where arts and sciences flourish. <unclear>Ig-</unclear>norance is, according to the proverb, counted to be the <unclear>Mother</unclear> of devotion, and it is certain that we shall find innocence and honesty nowhere more general than among the most illiterate, the poor simple <unclear>Country</unclear> people. I admit that, in my opinion, possessing what I mentioned to any degree is a trivial, if not harmful, trait—at least, nothing is less necessary for the hardworking poor. It is not compliments we want from them, but their labor and diligence. But I yield this point wholeheartedly; we will say that good manners are necessary for everyone. But in what way will they be provided with them in a charity school? Boys there might be taught to tip their caps indiscriminately to everyone they meet, unless it is a beggar; but I cannot imagine they would acquire any civility beyond that.

The teacher is not highly qualified, as can be guessed by his salary, and even if he could teach them manners, he does not have the time for it. While they are at school, they are either learning or reciting their lessons to him, or occupied with writing or arithmetic; and as soon as school is over, they have as much freedom as the children of other poor people. It is the instruction and the example of parents, and those they eat, drink, and socialize with, that influence the minds of children. Immoral parents who follow bad paths and are negligent toward their children will not have polite or civilized offspring, even if they attended a charity school until they were married. Honest, hardworking people, no matter how poor they are, if they have any sense of goodness and decency themselves, will keep their children disciplined and never allow them to roam the streets or stay out at night. Those who work themselves and have any authority over their children will make them do something or other that becomes profitable as soon as they are able, however small it may be. As for those who are so unruly that neither words nor blows can influence them, no charity school will reform them. Indeed, experience teaches us that among charity-school boys, there are plenty of bad ones who swear and

# An Essay on Charity

I have now come to the enormous crimes and the vast multitude of criminals that are all blamed on the lack of this notable education. It is undeniable that an abundance of thefts and robberies are daily committed in and about the city, and that great numbers yearly suffer death for those crimes. But because this point is always dragged in whenever the usefulness of charity schools is questioned—as if there were no dispute that they would greatly remedy and, in time, prevent those disorders—I intend to examine the real causes of those mischiefs so justly complained of. I do not doubt that I will make it appear that charity schools, and everything else that promotes idleness and keeps the poor from working, are more accessory to the growth of villainy than the lack of reading and writing, or even the grossest ignorance and stupidity.

Here I must interrupt myself to address the outcries of some impatient people who, upon reading what I just said, will cry out that far from encouraging idleness, they bring up their charity children to learn <term>handicrafts</term>, as well as trades and all manner of honest labor. I promise them that I shall take notice of that hereafter and answer it without suppressing the slightest thing that can be said in their favor.

In a populous city, it is not difficult for a young rascal who has pushed himself into a crowd to use a small hand and nimble fingers to snatch away a handkerchief or snuffbox from a man who is thinking of business and unmindful of his pocket. Success in small crimes seldom fails to lead to greater ones; and he who picks pockets with <unclear>impunity</unclear> at twelve is likely to be a <term>housebreaker</term> at sixteen, and a thorough villain long before

Those who are cautious as well as bold, and no drunkards, may do a world of mischief before they are discovered; and this is one of the greatest inconveniences of such vast, overgrown cities as *London* or *Paris*, that they harbor rogues and villains as granaries do vermin; they afford a perpetual shelter to the worst of people, and are places of safety to thousands of criminals, who daily commit thefts and burglaries, and yet by often changing their places of abode, may conceal themselves for many years, and will perhaps forever escape the hands of justice, unless by chance they are apprehended in the act. And when they are taken, the evidence perhaps lacks clarity or is otherwise insufficient, the depositions are not strong enough, and juries and often judges are touched with compassion; prosecutors, though vigorous at first, often relent before the time of trial comes on: Few men prefer the public safety to their own ease; a man of good nature is not easily reconciled to taking away another man’s life, though he has deserved the gallows. To be the cause of anyone’s death, though justice requires it, is what most people are startled at, especially men of conscience and probity, when they lack judgment or resolution; as this is the reason that thousands escape who deserve to be capitally punished, so it is likewise the cause that there are so many offenders, who boldly venture in hopes, that if they are taken they shall have the same good fortune of getting off.

But if men did imagine and were fully persuaded, that as surely as they committed an act that deserved hanging, so surely they would be hanged, executions would be very rare, and the most desperate felon would almost as soon hang himself as he would break open a house. To be stupid and ignorant is seldom the character of a thief. Robberies on the highway and other bold crimes are

# An Essay on Charity

generally perpetrated by rogues of spirit and talent, and villains of any renown are commonly subtle, cunning fellows who are well-versed in the method of trials and acquainted with every quirk in the law that can be of use to them. They do not overlook the smallest flaw in an indictment and know how to take advantage of the least slip of evidence and everything else that can serve their turn to get them off.

It is a popular saying that it is better that five hundred guilty people should escape than that one innocent person should suffer. This maxim is only true regarding the future and in relation to another world, but it is very false in regard to the temporal welfare of society. It is a terrible thing that a man should be put to death for a crime he is not guilty of; yet circumstances may meet so oddly in the infinite variety of accidents that it is possible it should come to pass, despite all the wisdom that judges and conscientiousness that juries may possess. But where men endeavor to avoid this with all the care and precaution human prudence is able to take, should such a misfortune happen perhaps once or twice in ten years, on the condition that during all that time justice should be administered with all strictness and severity, and not one guilty person suffered to escape with impunity, it would be a vast advantage to a nation. This would be beneficial not only for securing everyone's property and the peace of society in general, but it would likewise save the lives of hundreds, if not thousands, of needy wretches who are daily hanged for trifles, and who would never have attempted anything against the law, or at least would not have ventured on capital crimes, if the hope of getting off, should they be caught, had not been one of the motives that animated their resolve. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the causes of crime, transitioning from the failures of the justice system and the negligence of victims to the behavior and education of the poor, specifically arguing that lack of schooling is not the root of villainy.</meta>

<header>An Essay on Charity</header>

Children who are idle, even the best of either sex, are bad company to one another whenever they meet.

It is not, then, the lack of reading and writing, but the concurrence and a complication of more substantial evils that are the perpetual nursery of abandoned profligates in great and opulent nations. Whoever would accuse ignorance, stupidity, and cowardice as the first cause—what physicians call the <term>Procatartic Cause</term>—let him examine the lives and narrowly inspect the conversations and actions of ordinary rogues and our common felons. He will find the reverse to be true, and that the blame ought rather to be laid on the excessive cunning and subtlety, and too much knowledge in general, which the worst of miscreants and the scum of the nation possess.

Human nature is everywhere the same: genius, wit, and natural talents are always sharpened by application, and may be as much improved in the practice of the meanest villainy as they can in the exercise of industry or the most heroic virtue. There is no station of life where pride, emulation, and the love of glory may not be displayed. A young pickpocket who makes a jest of his angry prosecutor, and dexterously wheedles the old justice into an opinion of his innocence, is envied by his equals and admired by all the fraternity. Rogues have the same passions to gratify as other men, and value themselves on their honor and faithfulness to one another, their courage, intrepidity, and other manly virtues, just as much as people of better professions; and in daring enterprises, the resolution of a robber may be as much supported by his pride as that of an honest soldier who fights for his country.

The evils we complain of, then, are owing to quite other causes than those we assign for them. Men must be very wavering in their sentiments

have so many servants, coaches, and horses. They can spend hundreds, and some of them thousands of pounds on jewels and furniture, but cannot spare a shilling for a poor creature who needs it. When trends and fashions are discussed, they can listen with great attention, but they are willfully deaf to the cries of the poor. "Indeed, neighbor," replies the first, "you are quite right; I don't believe there is a worse parish in England for charity than ours. It is people like you and me who would do good if it were in our power, but of those who are able, there are very few who are willing."

Others, more aggressive, attack specific people and attach slander to every man of wealth they dislike; a thousand idle stories in the name of charity are raised and passed around to defame their social superiors. While this is happening throughout the neighborhood, the one who first proposed the pious thought rejoices to hear so many join in, and he takes no small credit for being the primary cause of so much talk and bustle. But because neither he nor his close associates are influential enough to start such a project, someone must be found who has greater standing. This person must be approached and shown the necessity, the goodness, the usefulness, and the Christianity of such a design. Next, he must be flattered: "Indeed, sir, if you would support it, nobody has a greater influence over the best people of the parish than yourself. One word from you, I am sure, would engage such a person. If you once take it to heart, sir, I would consider the matter as good as done, sir." If by this kind of rhetoric they can draw in some old fool or conceited busybody who is rich—or at least rumored to be so—the project begins to seem feasible and is discussed among the higher classes. The parson, or his curate, and the lecturer are everywhere praising the pious project. The original promoters, meanwhile, are tireless; if they were guilty of any obvious vice, they

The number of these diminutive patriots increasing, they form themselves into a society and appoint regular meetings, where everyone, concealing his vices, has the liberty to display <unclear>his</unclear> talent. Religion is the theme, or else the misery of the times occasioned by atheism and profaneness. Men of worth who live in splendor, and thriving people who have a great deal of business of their own, are seldom seen among them. Men of sense and education likewise, if they have nothing to do, generally look out for better diversion. All those who have a higher aim shall have their attendance easily excused, but they must contribute or else lead a weary life in the parish. Two sorts of people come in voluntarily: staunch churchmen, who have good reasons for it <term>in petto</term> <note>in secret or in one's heart</note>, and your sly sinners who look upon it as meritorious, and hope that it will expiate their guilt, and that Satan will be <term>nonsuited</term> <note>dismissed or defeated in a legal sense</note> by it at a small expense. Some join it to save their credit, others to retrieve it, according to whether they have either lost it or are afraid of losing it; others again do it prudently to increase their trade and gain acquaintances, and many would admit to you, if they dared to be sincere and speak the truth, that they would never have been concerned in it except to be better known in the parish. Men of sense who see the folly of it and have nobody to fear are persuaded into it so as not to be thought eccentric or to run counter to all <unclear>the</unclear> world; even of those who are resolute at first in denying it, it is ten to one that at last they are <unclear>tired</unclear> and importuned into compliance. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the motivations behind charity schools, specifically examining why people from the middle and lower classes seek to become "governors" of these institutions.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>

the insignificance of it is another argument that prevails greatly, and many are drawn in to be contributors who, without that, would have stood out and strenuously opposed the whole scheme.

The governors are chosen from the middle class, and many inferior to that class are utilized if the eagerness of their zeal can only outweigh the lowliness of their condition. If you should ask these worthy rulers why they take so much trouble upon themselves to the detriment of their own affairs and loss of time—either individually or as a group—they would all unanimously answer that it is the regard they have for religion and the Church, and the pleasure they take in contributing to the good and eternal welfare of so many poor innocents who, in all probability, would run into perdition in these wicked times of scoffers and free-thinkers. They claim to have no thought of self-interest; even those who trade in and provide these children with what they need have not the least design of profiting from what they sell for their use. Although in everything else their avarice and greed for profit are glaringly obvious, in this matter they are wholly divested of selfishness and have no worldly goals. One motive above all—which is by no means the least for most of them—is to be carefully concealed: I mean the satisfaction found in ordering and directing. There is a melodious sound in the word *governor* that is charming to common people. Everyone admires power and superiority; even <term>Imperium in Belluas</term> <note>rule or command over beasts</note> has its delights. There is a pleasure in ruling over anything, and it is this chiefly that sustains human nature through the tedious slavery of being a schoolmaster. But if there is the least satisfaction in governing the children, it must be delightful to govern the schoolmaster himself. What fine things are said, and perhaps written, to a governor when a schoolmaster is to be chosen! How

Those who can examine human nature will always find that what these people most pretend to is what they have least of, and what they utterly deny is their greatest motive. No habit or quality is more easily acquired than hypocrisy, nor is anything sooner learned than to deny the feelings of our hearts and the principles we act from. But the seeds of every passion are innate to us, and nobody comes into the world without them. If we observe the pastimes and recreations of young children, we shall notice nothing more common among them than that all who are allowed to do so take delight in playing with kittens and little puppies. The reason they are always dragging and pulling the poor creatures about the house proceeds from nothing else but that they can do with them what they please, and put them into whatever posture and shape they wish; and the pleasure they receive from this is originally due to the love of dominion and that usurping temperament with which all mankind is born.

When this great work is brought to fruition, and actually accomplished, joy and serenity seem to overspread the face of every inhabitant; to account for this, I must make a short digression. Everywhere there are slovenly, wretched fellows who are used to being seen always ragged and dirty. We look upon these people as miserable creatures in general, and unless they are very remarkable, we take little notice of them; yet among these, there are handsome and well-shaped men just as there are among their social superiors. But if one of these becomes a soldier, what a vast improvement is observed in him as soon as he is put in his red coat, and we see him looking smart with his grenadier's cap and a large service sword! All who knew him before are struck with different ideas

<header>and Charity Schools.</header>

There is something analogous to this in the sight of charity children; there is a natural beauty in uniformity which most people delight in. It is pleasing to the eye to see children well-matched, whether boys or girls, marching two by two in good order; and to have them all tidy and well-dressed in the same clothes and trimmings must add to the comeliness of the sight. What makes it still more generally entertaining is the imaginary share that even servants and the poorest in the parish have in it, though it costs them nothing: "Our parish church, our charity children." In all this, there is a shadow of ownership that pleases everyone who has a right to use those words, but especially those who actually contribute and had a great hand in advancing the pious work.

It is hardly conceivable that men should so little know their own hearts, and be so ignorant of their inward condition, as to mistake frailty, passion, and enthusiasm for goodness, virtue, and charity; yet nothing is more true than that the satisfaction, the joy, and the transports they feel for the reasons I named pass, with these miserable judges, for principles of piety and religion. Whoever will consider what I have said for two or three pages, and allow his imagination to wander a little further on what he has heard and seen concerning this subject, will be furnished with sufficient reasons—separate from the love of God and true Christianity—why charity schools are in such uncommon vogue, and so unanimously approved of and admired among all sorts and conditions of people. It is a theme which everyone can talk about and understands thoroughly; there is not a more inexhaustible fund for gossip and a variety of low conversation in hoy-boats <note>A hoy-boat was a small, heavy coastal sailing vessel used for passengers or cargo.</note> and stage-coaches. Whoever on behalf of the school or the sermon has exerted himself more than usual and happens to be in company, notice how he is praised by the women, and how his zeal and charitable disposition are extolled to the skies! "Upon my word, sir," says an old lady, "we are all very much obliged to you; I don’t think any of the other governors could have had enough influence to get us a bishop; I am told his lordship came on your account, even though he was not very well." To which the other replies very gravely that it is his duty, but that he values no trouble nor fatigue as long as he can be of service to the children, "poor lambs." "Indeed," says he, "I was determined to get a pair of <term>Lawn Sleeves</term> <note>A reference to the fine linen sleeves of a bishop's gown, here used to mean the bishop himself.</note>, even if I had to ride all night for it, and I am very glad I was not disappointed."

Sometimes the school itself is discussed, and which person in all the parish is most expected to build a new one. The old room where it is now held is ready to collapse; so-and-so had a vast estate left to him by his uncle, along with a great deal of money; a thousand pounds would be nothing to him.

At other times, the great crowds seen at some churches are talked about, along with the significant sums collected; from there, by an easy transition, they move on to the abilities, the various talents, and the orthodoxy of clergymen. Dr. <unclear>——</unclear> is a man of great talents and learning, and I believe he is very dedicated to the church, but I don't like him for a charity sermon. There is no better man in the world than <unclear>————</unclear>; he forces the money out of people's pockets. When he preached last for our children, I am sure there were many people who gave more than they intended when they came to church. I could see it in their faces and rejoiced at it heartily.

<header>*An Essay on Charity*</header>
<meta>catchword: fallibili</meta>

But if all these were not sufficient inducements to make men stand up in defense of the idol I speak of, there is another that will infallibly bribe most people to be advocates for it. We all naturally love triumph, and whoever engages in this cause is sure of conquest—at least in nine groups out of ten. Let him dispute with whomever he will: considering the speciousness of the pretext and the majority he has on his side, it is a castle, an impregnable fortress he can never be beaten out of. Even if the most sober, virtuous man alive were to produce all the arguments to prove the detriment that charity schools—at least the multiplicity of them—do to society (which I shall provide hereafter), and even if those arguments were stronger in a debate against the greatest scoundrel in the world, who should only make use of the common cant of charity and religion, public favor would be against the former, and he would lose his cause in the opinion of the common people.

The rise and origin, then, of all the bustle and clamor that is made throughout the kingdom on behalf of charity schools is chiefly built on frailty and human passion. At least, it is more than possible that a nation should have the same fondness and feel the same zeal for them as are shown in ours, and yet not be prompted to it by any principle of virtue or religion. Encouraged by this consideration, I shall with greater liberty attack this common error and endeavor to make it evident that, far from being beneficial, this forced education is pernicious to the public. The welfare of the public demands from us a regard superior to all other laws and considerations; thus, it shall be the only apology I intend to make for differing from the current sentiments of the learned and reverend body of our divines, and for venturing plainly to deny what I have just now admitted to be openly asserted by most of our bishops as well as the inferior clergy. --- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the fallibility of institutions in worldly affairs and begins a discussion on the economic necessity of a hardworking, low-consuming laboring class for the maintenance of a civil society.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>

...fallibility even in spiritual matters, her proper province, so it cannot be an affront to her to imagine that she may err in temporal matters, which are not so much under her immediate care. But to my task.

The whole earth being cursed, and no bread to be had except what we eat in the sweat of our brows, vast toil must be undergone before man can provide himself with the necessities for his sustenance and the bare support of his corrupt and defective nature as a single creature; but infinitely more is required to make life comfortable in a civil society, where men have become taught animals, and great numbers of them have by mutual compact framed themselves into a body politic. The more man’s knowledge increases in this state, the greater will be the variety of labor required to make him comfortable. It is impossible that a society can long subsist while allowing many of its members to live in idleness and enjoy all the ease and pleasure they can invent, without having at the same time great multitudes of people who, to compensate for this imbalance, will stoop to be quite the reverse, and through habit and patience inure their bodies to work for others as well as themselves.

The plenty and cheapness of provisions depends in great measure on the price and value set upon this labor. Consequently, the welfare of all societies—even before they are tainted with foreign luxury—requires that it should be performed by those members who, in the first place, are sturdy and robust and never used to ease or idleness; and in the second place, are easily satisfied regarding the necessities of life. These are people who are glad to accept the coarsest manufactured goods in everything they wear, and whose only aim in their diet is to feed their bodies when their stomachs prompt them to eat; with little regard for taste or relish, they refuse no wholesome nourishment that can be swallowed when men are hungry, nor ask anything for their thirst but to quench it.

<vocab>Charity Schools, Spiritual matters, Temporal matters, Civil Society, Body Politic, Labor, Foreign Luxury, Necessities of Life</vocab>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s economic argument from the previous section, shifting from the general necessity of toil to the specific social requirement for a large, uneducated laboring class to maintain national wealth and stability.</meta>

<page-num>334</page-num>
<header>An Essay on Charity</header>

As the greatest part of the drudgery is to be done by daylight, so it is by this alone that they actually measure the time of their labor, without any thought of the hours they are employed or the weariness they feel; and the hired laborer in the country must get up in the morning, not because he has rested enough, but because the sun is about to rise. This last point alone would be an intolerable hardship to adults under thirty who, during their youth, had been used to lying in bed as long as they could sleep; but all three factors together make up such a condition of life as a man from a more comfortable background would hardly choose, even if it should deliver him from a jail or a shrew.

If there must be such people—as no great nation can be happy without vast numbers of them—would not a wise legislature cultivate their kind with all imaginable care, and provide against a scarcity of them as he would prevent a scarcity of food itself? No man would be poor and exhaust himself for a livelihood if he could help it. The absolute necessity that all people have for food and drink—and in cold climates for clothes and lodging—makes them submit to anything that can be endured. If no one felt want, no one would work; but the greatest hardships are looked upon as solid pleasures when they keep a man from starving.

From what has been said, it is manifest that in a free nation where slaves are not allowed, the surest wealth consists in a multitude of the hardworking poor; for besides the fact that they are the never-failing source of fleets and armies, without them there could be no enjoyment, and no product of any country could be valuable. To make society happy and people comfortable under the lowliest circumstances, it is necessary that great numbers of them should be ignorant as well as poor. Knowledge both enlarges and multiplies our desires, and the fewer things a man wishes for, the more easily his necessities may be supplied.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the economic necessity of an uneducated laboring class, specifically focusing on why education is detrimental to those destined for manual labor.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
# and Charity-Schools.

The welfare and happiness, therefore, of every state and kingdom require that the knowledge of the working poor should be confined within the limits of their occupations, and never extended (regarding visible things) beyond what relates to their calling. The more a shepherd, a plowman, or any other peasant knows of the world, and the things that are foreign to his labor or employment, the less fit he will be to endure the fatigues and hardships of it with cheerfulness and contentment.

Reading, writing, and arithmetic are very necessary to those whose business requires such qualifications, but where people's livelihood has no dependence on these arts, they are very harmful to the poor, who are forced to earn their daily bread by their daily labor. Few children make any progress at school without also being capable of being employed in some business or another; so that every hour those sorts of poor people spend at their books is so much time lost to society. Going to school, in comparison to working, is idleness; and the longer boys continue in this easy sort of life, the more unfit they will be when grown up for downright labor, both in terms of strength and inclination. As for men who are to remain and end their days in a laborious, tiresome, and painful station of life: the sooner they are put to it at first, the more patiently they will submit to it forever after. Hard labor and the coarsest diet are a proper punishment for several kinds of criminals, but to impose either on those who have not been used to or brought up with both is the greatest cruelty, when there is no crime you can charge them with.

Reading and writing are not attained without some labor of the brain and diligence, and before people are even moderately skilled in either, they value themselves infinitely above those who are completely ignorant of them—often with so little justice and moderation as if they were of another species. As

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that educating the poor is socially and economically disadvantageous. He contends that schooling fosters a distaste for manual labor and that a functioning social hierarchy requires a significant gap in knowledge between the master and the servant.</meta>
<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>An Essay on Charity</header>

As all humans naturally have an aversion to trouble and taking pains, so we are all fond of, and apt to overvalue, those qualifications we have acquired at the expense of our ease and quiet for years together. Those who spent a great part of their youth in learning to read, write, and do arithmetic expect—and not unjustly—to be employed where those qualifications may be of use to them. Most of them will look upon manual labor with the utmost contempt—I mean labor performed in the service of others in the lowest station of life, and for the smallest pay. A man who has had some education may follow farming by choice and be diligent at the dirtiest and most laborious work; but in that case, the business must be his own, and greed, the care of a family, or some other pressing motive must drive him to it. But he will not make a good hired hand and serve a farmer for a pitiful reward; at least, he is not as fit for it as a day laborer who has always been employed with the plow and dung cart, and does not remember ever living otherwise.

When submissiveness and menial services are required, we shall always observe that they are never so cheerfully nor so heartily performed as by inferiors for superiors; I mean inferiors not only in wealth and status, but likewise in knowledge and understanding. A servant can have no genuine respect for his master as soon as he has sense enough to find out that he serves a fool. When we are meant to learn or to obey, we shall experience in ourselves that the higher the opinion we have of the wisdom and ability of those who are either to teach or command us, the greater deference we pay to their laws and instructions. No creatures submit contentedly to their equals, and if a horse knew as much as a man, I would not want to be its rider.

### and Charity-Schools.

Here I am forced again to make a *digression*, though I declare I never felt less like doing so than I have at this minute; but I see a thousand punishments being prepared <note>the original phrase "rods in piss" refers to the practice of soaking birch rods in urine to keep them supple for flogging</note>, and the whole gang of petty pedants against me for attacking the <term>Christ-cross-row</term> <note>the alphabet, so named for the cross that typically preceded the letters in a child's primer</note>, and opposing the very elements of literature.

This is no panic-induced fear, and the reader will not imagine my apprehensions are ill-founded if he considers what an army of petty tyrants I must deal with—all of whom either actually punish with the *birch* or else are seeking such a promotion. For if I had no other adversaries than the starving wretches of both sexes throughout the kingdom of *Great Britain* who, from a natural dislike of work, have a great distaste for their current employment—and feeling within themselves a much stronger inclination to command than they ever felt to obey others, think themselves qualified and wish from their hearts to be masters and mistresses of *Charity-Schools*—the number of my enemies would, by the most modest calculation, amount to at least one hundred thousand.

It seems to me I hear them cry out that a more dangerous doctrine was never introduced, and that *Popery* is nothing compared to it; they ask what *Saracen* brute it is who draws his ugly weapon for the destruction of learning. It is ten to one that they will indict me for attempting, at the instigation of the *Prince of Darkness*, to introduce into these realms greater ignorance and barbarity than any nation was ever plunged into by the *Goths* and *Vandals* since the light of the Gospel first appeared in the world. Whoever suffers under public hatred is always accused of crimes he was never guilty of; and it will be suspected that I have had a hand in erasing the Holy Scriptures, and perhaps it will be claimed that it was at my request that the small Bibles published by patent in the year 1721, and chiefly used in *Charity-Schools*, were, through the badness of

### and Charity-Schools.

<warning>The left edge of the page is cropped, causing loss of text at the beginning of each line.</warning>

<note>Bracketed text indicates reconstruction of cropped words based on context.</note>

The apologetic defense I am going to make is to clear myself from having any design against the **Arts** and **Sciences**, as some Heads of Colleges—those careful preservers of human learning—have apprehended upon seeing ignorance admitted as a necessary ingredient in a mixed civil society.

In the first place, I would have nearly double the number of professors in every University compared to what there are now. Theology among us is generally well-taught, but the two other faculties have very little instruction, at least what is most needed, especially **Physick** <note>medicine</note>. Every branch ought to have two or three professors who take pains to communicate their skill and knowledge to others. In public lectures, a man has great opportunities to show off his talents, but private instructions are more useful; in **Pharmacy** and the knowledge of materials, they are as necessary as Anatomy or the history of diseases. It is a shame that when men have received their degree and are by authority entrusted with the lives of the subjects, they should have to come to **London** to become acquainted with the **Materia Medica** <note>the body of knowledge regarding the natural substances used for medical purposes</note> and the composition of medicines, and take instructions from others who never had a university education themselves. It is certain that in the city I named, there is ten times more opportunity for a **man** to improve himself in **Anatomy**, **Pharmacy**, and the practice of **Physick**, than in both **Universities** together. What has an [apothecary] to do with **silks**, or who would look for **pickles** at a **mercer's** <note>a dealer in textile fabrics, especially silks</note>? Where things are well managed, **hospitals** are made as useful for the advancement of students in the art of **Physick** as they are for the recovery of health in the [sick].

What sense ought to govern men in learning a **trade**? No man ever apprenticed his son to a **goldsmith** to make him a **linen-draper**.

He suggests that hospitals should serve as teaching centers and argues that education should be specialized to one's intended trade.</summary>

Print and Paper rendered illegible: which I protest I am as innocent of as the child unborn. But I am in a thousand fears; the more I consider my case the worse I like it, and the greatest comfort I have is in my sincere belief, that hardly anybody will mind a word of what I say; or else if ever the people suspected that what I write would be of any weight to any considerable part of society, I should not have the courage barely to think on all the trades I should disoblige, and I cannot but smile when I reflect on the variety of uncouth sufferings that would be prepared for me, if the punishment they would differently inflict on me was emblematically to point at my crime. For if I was not suddenly stuck full of useless penknives up to the hilts, the Company of Stationers would certainly take me in hand, and either have me buried alive in their Hall under a great heap of Primers and Spelling-Books they would not be able to sell; or else send me up against the tide to be bruised to death in a paper mill that would be obliged to stand still a week on my account. The ink-makers at the same time would, for the public good, offer to choke me with astringents, or drown me in the black liquor that would be left upon their hands: which, if they joined stock, might easily be performed in less than a month: and if I should escape the cruelty of these united bodies, the resentment of a private monopolist would be as fatal to me, and I should soon find myself pelted and knocked on the head with little squat Bibles—clasped in brass and ready armed for mischief—that, charitable learning ceasing, would be fit for nothing but to be opened to fight with, and exercise truly polemic.

The digression I spoke of just now is not a foolish trifle that ended with the last paragraph, and which the grave critic, to whom all mirth is unreasonable, will think very impertinent <unclear>y</unclear>

<summary>The author satirically describes the "punishments" he might face from the printing and stationery trades if his arguments against charity schools and mass literacy were to actually reduce the demand for books and paper. He imagines being buried in unsold primers or pelted with brass-bound Bibles by those who profit from the business of education.</summary>

<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>
<warning>Heavy ink bleed-through and dark patches throughout the page may partially obscure some characters.</warning>

I am going to make a serious apology immediately, to clear myself of having any design against the arts and sciences, as some heads of colleges and other careful preservers of human learning might have feared after seeing ignorance recommended as a necessary ingredient in the mix of civil society.

In the first place, I would have nearly double the number of professors in every university compared to what there is now. Theology among us is generally well-supplied, but the two other faculties have very little to boast of, especially <term>Physick</term> <note>medicine</note>. Every branch of that art ought to have two or three professors who would take the trouble to communicate their skill and knowledge to others. In public lectures, a vain man has great opportunities to show off his talents, but private instructions are more useful to students. Pharmacy and the knowledge of <term>simples</term> <note>medicinal plants or herbs</note> are as necessary as anatomy or the history of diseases. It is a shame that when men have taken their degree, and are authorized to be entrusted with the lives of subjects, they should be forced to come to *London* to become acquainted with the <term>Materia Medica</term> <note>medical substances</note> and the composition of medicines, and receive instructions from others who never had a university education themselves. It is certain that in the city I named, there is ten times more opportunity for a man to improve himself in anatomy, botany, pharmacy, and the practice of <term>Physick</term>, than at both universities together. What does an oil shop have to do with silks? Or who would look for hams and pickles at a <term>mercer’s</term> <note>a dealer in fine fabrics</note>? Where things are well managed, hospitals are made as useful for the advancement of students in the art of medicine as they are for the recovery of health in the poor.

Good sense ought to govern men in learning as well as in trade: no man ever apprenticed his son to a goldsmith to make him a linen-draper.

<summary>Mandeville clarifies that his recommendation of "ignorance" for the poor does not imply a dislike of higher education; instead, he argues for a significant expansion and practical reform of university medical faculties. He criticizes the lack of practical training in universities, noting that medical graduates often must go to London to learn the actual practice of pharmacy and anatomy.</summary>

This is, in my humble opinion, a great sign that some part of the great wealth they possess is not as well applied as it might be.

Professors should, in addition to the stipends allowed them by the public, receive fees from every student they teach, so that self-interest as well as competition and the love of glory might spur them on to labor and diligence. When a man excels in any one study or branch of learning and is qualified to teach others, he ought to be hired if money can buy his services, without regard for which party—or indeed which country or nation—he belongs to, whether he is black or white. Universities should be public marketplaces for all manner of knowledge, just as the annual fairs held at *Leipzig*, *Frankfurt*, and other places in Germany are for different wares and merchandise, where no distinction is made between natives and foreigners, and which people travel to from all parts of the world with equal freedom and equal privilege.

From paying the fees I mentioned, I would excuse all students intended for the ministry of the Gospel. It is to this religious zeal—or at least the human frailties that pass for and represent it—that we owe the great abundance of poor scholars the nation enjoys. ...for no mortal who had been educated in any reasonable comfort could live upon them, unless he possessed real virtue; it is foolish, and indeed harmful, that we should expect more virtue from the clergy than we generally find in the laity.

The great care I would take to promote that part of learning which is more immediately useful to society should not make me neglect the more specialized and refined arts; rather, all the liberal arts and every branch of literature should be encouraged throughout the kingdom more than they currently are, if my wishing could do it. In every county, there should be one or more large schools erected at the public expense for *Latin* and *Greek*, which should be divided into six or more classes, with specific masters for each of them. The whole should be under the care and inspection of some learned men in authority, who would not only be governors in name, but would actually take the trouble at least twice a year to hear every class thoroughly examined by its master, and not content themselves with judging the progress the scholars had made from themes <note>Latin or Greek school compositions</note> and other exercises that had been written out of their sight.

At the same time, I would abolish and prevent the proliferation of those petty schools that never would have existed had their masters not been extremely poor. It is a common error that no one can spell or write *English* well without a small smattering of *Latin*. This is upheld by pedants for their own interest, and is maintained by none more strenuously than those among them who are "poor scholars" in more than one sense; meanwhile, it is an abominable falsehood. I have known, and am still acquainted with several—including some of the fair sex—who never learned any *Latin*, and yet maintain strict orthography <note>correct spelling</note> and write admirably good sense; whereas, on the other hand, everyone may encounter the scribblings of those who pretend...

...it was to provide a rag for their children except through begging. But now, when a boy or a girl is wanted for any small service, we consider it a duty to employ our charity-school children before any others. Their education looks like a reward for being vicious and inactive, a benefit commonly bestowed on parents who deserve to be punished for shamefully neglecting their families. In one place, you may hear a half-drunk scoundrel, cursing himself, call for another pot of beer, and as a good reason for it add that his boy is provided with clothes and has his schooling for free. In another, you shall see a poor woman in great necessity, whose child is to be taken care of because she herself is a lazy woman and never did anything to remedy her wants in good earnest, except bewail them at a gin-shop.

If the children of everyone who can educate them at our universities through their own industry are well taught, there will be enough men of learning to supply this nation and another like it; and reading, writing, or arithmetic would never be lacking in the business that requires them, even if none were to learn them except those whose parents could afford the cost. It is not with education as it is with the gifts of the Holy Spirit, which cannot be purchased with money; and "bought wit," if we believe the proverb, is not the worst kind.

I thought it necessary to say this much about learning to prevent the outcries of the enemies of truth and fair dealing, who—had I not explained myself so fully on this topic—would have represented me as a deadly foe to all literature and useful knowledge, and a wicked advocate for universal ignorance and stupidity. I shall now fulfill my promise of answering what I know the supporters of charity schools would object against me, by saying that they raised the children under their care for legitimate and hardworking trades, and not for idleness, as I did insinuat<unclear>e</unclear>

<page-num>245</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>
<sig>M 3</sig>

I have already sufficiently shown why going to school was idleness compared to working, and I have rejected this kind of education for the children of the poor because it makes them incapable forever after of hard labor, which is their proper place. In every civil society, this is a lot they should not complain about or resent, if it is demanded of them with discretion and humanity. What remains is for me to speak about apprenticing them to trades, which I shall try to demonstrate is destructive to the harmony of a nation and an inappropriate interference with things that few of these governors know anything about.

To do this, let us examine the nature of societies and what their composition should consist of, if we wish to raise them to as high a degree of strength, beauty, and perfection as the foundation on which we build will allow. The variety of services required to supply the luxurious and extravagant desires, as well as the real necessities of man, along with all their subordinate callings, is immense in a nation like ours. Yet it is certain that, although the number of these various occupations is excessively large, it is far from being infinite; if you add one more than is required, it must be superfluous. If a man had a good inventory and the best shop in *Cheapside* to sell turbans, he would be ruined; and if *Demetrius* or any other silversmith made nothing but *Diana's* shrines, he would not earn his bread now that the worship of that goddess is out of fashion. Just as it is foolish to establish trades that are not needed, the next worst thing is to increase the numbers in any one trade beyond what is required. As things are managed among us, it would be absurd to have as many brewers as there are bakers, or as many woolen drapers as there are shoemakers. This proportion regarding the numbers in every trade finds its own balance, and is never better maintained than when nobody meddles or interferes with

<summary>The author argues that educating the poor for trades disrupts the natural economic balance of a nation. He suggests that the number of workers in any given profession should be determined by market demand rather than the interference of charity school governors.</summary>
<keywords>economic balance, labor, trade proportions, charity schools, social hierarchy, supply and demand, vocational training</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of charity schools, moving from the general concept of education for the poor to the specific problems caused by apprenticing these children into trades that are already overcrowded.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>246</page-num>
<header>An Essay on Charity</header>

People who have children to educate who must earn their own living are always consulting and deliberating about what trade or calling they should bring them up in, until they are settled; and thousands think about this who hardly think at all about anything else. First, they limit themselves to their circumstances, and he who can only provide ten pounds with his son must not look for a trade where they ask a hundred with an apprentice. But the next thing they consider is always which will be the most advantageous; if there is a calling where, at that time, people are more generally employed than they are in any other within the same reach, there are immediately half a score of fathers ready to supply it with their sons. Therefore, the greatest care most companies have is concerning the regulation of the number of apprentices. Now, when all trades complain, and perhaps justly, that they are overstocked, you obviously injure that trade to which you add one member more than would naturally result from the nature of society.

Besides that, the governors of charity schools do not deliberate so much about which trade is best, but rather which tradesmen they can find who will take the boys for a certain sum; and few men of substance and experience will have anything to do with these children. They are afraid of a hundred inconveniences from the needy parents of these children. So they are bound, or at least most commonly, either to drunks and neglectful masters, or else to those who are very needy and do not care what becomes of their apprentices after they have received the money. By this, it seems as if we studied nothing more than how to have a perpetual nursery for charity schools.

When all trades and handicrafts are overstocked, it is a certain sign that there is a fault in the management of the whole; for it is impossible there should be too many people if the country is able to <unclear>maintain</unclear> them. Are provisions expensive? Whose fault is it?

<sig>M 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: have</meta>

...as long as you have ground untilled and hands unemployed? But I shall be answered that to increase plenty must, in the long run, ruin the farmer or lower the rents all over England. To which I reply that what the husbandman complains of most is what I would redress: the greatest grievance of farmers, gardeners, and others, where hard labor is required and dirty work must be done, is that they cannot get servants for the same wages they used to pay them. The day-laborer grumbles at sixteen pence to do no other drudgery than what thirty years ago his grandfather did cheerfully for half the money. As to the rents, it is impossible they should fall while you increase your numbers, but the price of provisions and all labor in general must fall with them, if not before; and a man with a hundred and fifty pounds a year has no reason to complain that his income is reduced to one hundred, if he can buy as much for that one hundred as he previously could have done for two hundred.

There is no intrinsic worth in money except what changes with the times, and whether a guinea is worth twenty pounds or a shilling, it is (as I have already hinted before) the labor of the poor—and not the high or low value set on gold or silver—from which all the comforts of life must arise. It is in our power to have a much greater plenty than we enjoy if agriculture and fisheries were taken care of as they might be; but we are so little capable of increasing our labor that we have hardly enough poor people to do what is necessary for our subsistence. The social balance is spoiled, and the bulk of the nation—which should everywhere consist of the laboring poor who are unacquainted with anything but their work—knows little of the other parts. In all business where direct labor is shunned or overpaid, there is plenty of people. For every one merchant you

# An Essay on Charity

...have ten bookkeepers, or at least pretenders; and everywhere in the country, the farmer lacks workers. Ask for a footman who has been in gentlemen’s families for some time, and you will find a dozen who all claim to be butlers. You may find chambermaids by the dozen, but you cannot find a cook for anything less than extravagant wages.

Nobody will do the dirty, slavish work if they can avoid it. I do not blame them; but all these things show that people of the lowest rank know too much to be of service to us. Servants demand more than masters and mistresses can afford, and what madness it is to encourage them in this by industriously increasing, at our own expense, the knowledge for which they will surely make us pay for over again! And it is not only those who are educated at our own expense who encroach upon us, but also the raw, ignorant country girls and foolish fellows—who can do nothing and are good for nothing—who impose upon us as well. The scarcity of servants caused by the education of the former gives an opportunity for the latter to raise their price and demand what should only be given to servants who understand their business and possess most of the good qualities that can be required of them.

There is no place in the world where there are more handsome fellows to look at or to run an errand than some of our footmen; but what are they good for, overall? The majority of them are rogues and not to be trusted; and even if they are honest, half of them are drunkards and will get drunk three or four times a week. The surly ones are generally quarrelsome and, valuing their manhood above all other considerations, do not care what clothes they spoil or what disappointments they may cause when their prowess is questioned. Those who are good-natured are generally terrible womanizers, always chasing after the girls, and so

249

...the maidservants they encounter. Many of them are guilty of all these vices: womanizing, drinking, and quarreling; yet they shall have all their faults overlooked and tolerated because they are men of good appearance and humble manners who know how to wait on gentlemen. This is an unpardonable folly in masters and generally ends in the ruin of servants.

Some few there are who are not addicted to any of these failings and also understand their duty; but as these are rare, there is not one in fifty who does not overrate himself. His wages must be excessive, and you can never stop giving to him; everything in the house is his perquisite, and he will not stay with you unless his vails <note>tips or gratuities given to servants by guests</note> are sufficient to maintain a middle-class family. And though you had taken him from the dunghill, out of a hospital <note>a charitable institution or school for the poor</note>, or a prison, you shall never keep him longer than he can earn from his position what he, in his high opinion of himself, thinks he deserves. Indeed, even the best and most civilized, who were never rude or disrespectful, will leave the most indulgent master—framing fifty excuses and telling outright lies to get away cleanly—as soon as they can improve their situation. A man who runs a half-crown or twelve-penny ordinary <note>a tavern or public house serving a fixed-price meal</note> does not look for money from his customers more than a footman does from every guest who dines or sups with his master; and I doubt whether the former does not often think a shilling or half a crown (depending on the status of the person) is as much his due as the other does.

A householder <note>original: Housekeeper; here meaning the master or head of the house</note> who cannot afford to host many gatherings and does not often invite people to his table can have no respectable man-servant; he is forced to settle for some country bumpkin or other awkward fellow, who will likewise leave him as soon as he imagines himself fit for any other service and is made wiser by his
