<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the servant class, focusing on how public gathering places and excessive compensation corrupt their character and work ethic.</meta>

rascally companions. All famous eating-houses and places that many gentlemen frequent for diversion or business, more especially the precincts of *Westminster Hall*, are the great schools for servants, where the dullest fellows may have their understandings improved and get rid of their stupidity and their innocence at once. They are the academies for footmen, where public lectures are daily read on all the sciences of low debauchery by their experienced professors; and students are instructed in over seven hundred illiberal arts—how to cheat, impose upon, and find out the blind side of their masters—with so much diligence that in a few years they become graduates in iniquity. Young gentlemen and others who are not thoroughly well-versed in the world, when they hire such knowledgeable tricksters into their service, are commonly indulgent beyond measure. For fear of revealing their lack of experience, they hardly dare to contradict or deny them anything; this is often the reason that, by allowing them unreasonable privileges, they expose their ignorance even when they are most trying to conceal it.

Some perhaps will blame the things I complain about on luxury, regarding which I have said that it could do no harm to a rich nation if imports never exceeded exports; but I do not think this accusation is just, and nothing ought to be feared on account of luxury that is actually the effect of folly. A man may be very extravagant in indulging his ease and his pleasure, making the enjoyment of the world as laborious <note>original: operose</note> and expensive as it can be made, provided he can afford it, while at the same time showing his good sense in everything about him. This he cannot be said to do <unclear>if</unclear> he diligently renders his people incapable of performing the service he expects from them. <unclear>It is</unclear> too much money, excessive wages, and unreasonable <term>vails</term> <note>tips or gratuities given to servants by guests</note> that spoil servants in *England*.

[A man] may have twenty-five horses in his stables without being guilty of folly, if it suits the rest of his circumstances; but if he keeps only one and overfeeds it to show off his wealth, he is a fool for his pains. Is it not madness to allow servants to take three, and others five *percent*, of what they pay to tradesmen on behalf of their masters? This is well known to watchmakers and others who sell toys, superfluous knick-knacks, and other curiosities, especially if they deal with people of high status and fashionable gentlemen who consider themselves above counting their own money. If they should accept a gift when offered, it might be overlooked; but it is an unpardonable impudence that they should claim it as their due and fight for it if refused. Those who have all the necessities of life provided for can have no need for money except for what harms them as servants—unless they were to save it for old age or sickness, which among our skip-kennels <note>A derogatory term for footmen or lackeys.</note> is not very common; and even then, it only makes them insolent and unbearable.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I am credibly informed that a group of footmen has reached such a level of insolence as to have entered into a society together and made laws by which they bind themselves not to serve for less than a certain sum, nor to carry burdens or any bundle or parcel above a certain weight (not exceeding two or three pounds), along with other regulations directly opposed to the interest of those they serve and altogether destructive to the purpose for which they were intended. If any of them are dismissed for strictly adhering to the orders of this honourable corporation, he is taken care of until another job is found for him; and there is no money lacking at any time to start and maintain a lawsuit against any master who shall attempt to strike or offer any other injury to his "gentleman footman," contrary to the statutes of their society. # An Essay on Charity

<warning>Bottom right corner is heavily obscured by shadow or ink bleed.</warning>

If they are allowed to continue consulting and providing for their own ease and convenience any further, we may expect quickly to see the *French* comedy *Le Maitre le Valet* acted in good earnest in most families. If this is not redressed in a short time—and if those footmen increase their numbers as much as they possibly can, as well as assemble whenever they please with impunity—it will be in their power to make a tragedy of it whenever they have a mind to do so.

But even if we suppose those apprehensions are frivolous and groundless, it is undeniable that servants in general are daily encroaching upon masters and mistresses, and endeavoring to be more on a level with them. They not only seem solicitous to abolish the low dignity of their condition, but have already considerably raised it in common estimation from the original meanness in which the public welfare requires it should always remain. I do not say that these things are entirely due to charity schools; there are other evils to which they may be partly ascribed. *London* is too big for the country, and in several respects we are failing ourselves. But even if a thousand faults had to coincide before the inconveniences we labor under could be produced, can any man who considers what I have said doubt that charity schools are accessory? Or at least, that they are more likely to create and increase these complaints than to lessen or redress them?

The only thing of weight, then, that can be said in their behalf is that so many thousand children are educated by them in the Christian faith and the principles of the Church of *England*. To demonstrate that this is not a sufficient plea for them, I must ask the reader—as I hate repetitions—to look back on what I have said before. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of charity schools, arguing that religious instruction is more effectively delivered through the church and the enforcement of Sabbath observance than through formal schooling for the poor.</meta>

and Charity-Schools.

Regarding religion, what children learn at school may just as well be taught at church through either preaching or catechizing; from which, or some other place of worship, I would not have even the humblest person in a parish who is able to walk be absent on Sundays. It is the Sabbath, the most useful day in seven, which is set apart for divine service and religious exercise as well as for resting from physical labor; and it is a duty incumbent on all magistrates to take particular care of that day. The poor especially, and their children, should be made to go to church on it both in the morning and afternoon, because they have no time on any other day. By precept and example, they ought to be encouraged and accustomed to it from their very infancy; the willful neglect of it ought to be considered scandalous, and if absolute compulsion toward what I urge might seem too harsh and perhaps impractical, then all diversions at least ought to be strictly prohibited, and the poor hindered from every amusement in public that might allure or draw them away from it.

Where this care is taken by the magistrates as far as it lies in their power, ministers of the Gospel may instill into even the most limited minds more piety and devotion, and better principles of virtue and religion, than charity schools ever did or ever will produce; and those who complain, when they have such opportunities, that they cannot imbue their parishioners with sufficient knowledge of what they need as Christians without the assistance of reading and writing, are either very lazy or very ignorant and undeserving themselves.

That the most knowledgeable are not the most religious will be evident if we conduct a trial between people of different abilities even at this juncture, where going to church is not made such an obligation on the poor and illiterate as it might be. Let us select a hundred poor men, the first

# An Essay on Charity

...we can find who are over forty and were raised in hard labor from their infancy—those who never went to school at all and always lived far from knowledge and large towns. Let us compare these to an equal number of very good scholars, all of whom have had a university education; and let half of them, if you like, be clergymen, well-versed in **Philology** and **Polemical Learning**. Then let us impartially examine the lives and conduct of both groups, and I dare guarantee that among the first group, who can neither read nor write, we shall find more unity and neighborly love, less wickedness and attachment to worldly things, more contentment of mind, more innocence, sincerity, and other good qualities that lead to public peace and true happiness, than we shall find among the latter. Among the scholars, on the contrary, we can be certain of the height of pride and insolence, eternal quarrels and dissensions, irreconcilable hatreds, strife, envy, slander, and other vices destructive to mutual harmony—vices with which the illiterate, laboring poor are hardly ever tainted to any significant degree.

I am quite convinced that what I have said in the last paragraph will be no news to most of my readers; but if it is the truth, why should it be suppressed? And why must our concern for religion be eternally used as a cloak to hide our real motives and worldly intentions? If both parties would agree to pull off the mask, we would soon discover that, whatever they pretend, they aim at nothing so much in **Charity-Schools** as strengthening their own political party. The great advocates for the Church, by educating children in the principles of religion, mean <unclear>nothing more than</unclear> inspiring them with an extreme veneration for the clergy of the Church of England, and a strong aversion and everlasting hostility toward all those who dissent from it. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the motives behind the establishment of charity schools, specifically addressing how both the clergy and the "defenders of liberty" use these institutions to further their own agendas.</meta>

most admired for their charity sermons and most fond of preaching them; and on the other hand, whether in recent years we have had any riots or party scuffles among the mob in which the youth of a famous hospital in this city were not always the foremost ringleaders.

The great defenders of liberty, who are ever guarding themselves and skirmishing against arbitrary power—often when they are in no danger of it—are, generally speaking, not very superstitious, nor do they seem to place much importance on any modern apostleship: Yet some of these likewise speak out loudly for charity schools, but what they expect from them has no relation to religion or morality. They only look upon them as the proper means to destroy and frustrate the power of the priests over the laity. Reading and writing increase knowledge, and the more men know, the better they can judge for themselves, and they imagine that, if knowledge could be rendered universal, people could not be priest-ridden, which is the thing they fear the most.

I confess it is very probable that the first group will achieve their aim. But surely wise men who are not zealous for a party, or bigots toward the priests, will not think it worthwhile to suffer so many inconveniences as charity schools may cause, merely to promote the ambition and power of the clergy. To the other group, I would answer that if all those who are educated at the expense of their parents or relations will but think for themselves and refuse to have their reason deceived by the priests, we need not be concerned about how the clergy will influence the ignorant who have no education at all. Let them make the most of them. Considering the schools we have for those who can afford to pay for learning, it is ridiculous to imagine that the abolishing of charity schools would be a step toward any ignorance that could be harmful to the nation.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that educating the poor through charity schools may be detrimental to society's need for manual labor, following his discussion on how different factions use these schools for their own political or religious ends.</meta>

# An Essay on Charity

I would not want to be thought cruel, and I am well assured—if I know anything of myself—that I abhor inhumanity. However, to be compassionate to excess where reason forbids it, and where the general interest of society requires steadiness of thought and resolution, is an unpardonable weakness. I know it will always be argued against me that it is barbarous that the children of the poor should have no opportunity to exert themselves, as long as God has not denied them natural abilities and genius any more than the rich. But I cannot think this is any harder than the fact that they should not have money, even though they have the same inclination to spend as others do. I do not deny that great and useful men have come from hospitals <note>In this context, "hospitals" refers to charitable institutions for the education of poor or orphaned children.</note>; but it is also very probable that when they were first employed, many people just as capable as they were—who were not raised in hospitals—were neglected. Given the same good fortune, those others would have done just as well as they did, if they had been utilized instead.

There are many examples of women who have excelled in learning, and even in war; but this is no reason we should raise them all in Latin and Greek or in military discipline, instead of needlework and housekeeping. There is no scarcity of spirit or natural abilities among us, and no soil or climate has human creatures to boast of who are better formed—either inside or out—than this island generally produces. It is not wit, genius, or teachability we lack, but diligence, application, and persistence.

A great deal of hard and dirty labor must be done, and a coarse way of living must be accepted. Where shall we find a better nursery for those necessities than the children of the poor? Certainly, none are closer to it or better suited for it. Furthermore, the things I called hardships neither seem to be nor actually are such to those who have been raised with them and know nothing better. There is not a more contented people

These are truths that are undeniable; yet I know few people will be pleased to have them divulged. What makes them odious is an unreasonable vein of petty reverence for the poor that runs through most multitudes—and more particularly in this nation—and arises from a mixture of pity, folly, and superstition. It is from a lively sense of this compound that men cannot endure to hear or see anything said or acted against the poor, without considering how just the one, or insolent the other. So a beggar must not be beaten though he strikes you first. <term>Journeymen</term> tailors go to law with their masters and are obstinate in a wrong cause, yet they must be pitied; and murmuring weavers must be relieved, and have fifty silly things done to humor them, though in the midst of their poverty they insult their betters, and on all occasions appear to be more prone to making holidays and riots than they are to working or sobriety.

This puts me in mind of our wool, which considering the posture of our affairs and the behavior of the poor, I sincerely believe ought not upon any account to be carried abroad. But if we look into the reason why allowing it to be fetched away is so pernicious, our heavy complaint and lamentations that it is exported can be no great credit to us. Considering the mighty and manifold hazards that must be run before it can be got off the coast and safely landed beyond the sea, it is manifest that the foreigners, before they can work our wool, must pay considerably more for it than what we can have it for at home. Yet notwithstanding this great difference in the <term>prime cost</term>, they can afford to sell the manufactures made of it cheaper at foreign markets than ourselves. There is no people who have yet reached a higher perfection in woolen manufacture, either regarding speed or the quality of work—at least in the most significant branches—than we have. Therefore, what we complain about can only depend on the difference in the management of the poor between other nations and ours. If the laboring people in one country work twelve hours a day, six days a week, and in another they are employed only eight hours a day and no more than four days a week, the latter is obliged to have nine workers for what the former does with four. But if, moreover, the living expenses—the food, clothing, and whatever is consumed by the workers of the industrious nation—cost only half as much as what is spent by an equal number of the <unclear>other</unclear>, the consequence must be that the first will have the work of eighteen men for the same price that the other pays for the work of four. I would not suggest, nor do I think, that the difference in either diligence or the necessities of life between us and any neighboring nation is nearly as great as what I describe; yet I would have it considered that half of that difference, or even much less, is sufficient to outweigh the disadvantage they suffer regarding the price of wool.

Nothing is more evident to me than that no nation, in any manufacturing whatsoever, can undersell their neighbors with whom they are at best only equals in skill, speed, and the convenience of working—especially when the <term>prime cost</term> of the material to be manufactured is not in their favor—unless they have provisions and everything relating to their sustenance cheaper, or that <unclear>the</unclear> work-

--- Page 277 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding international trade competition, focusing on how the diligence and standard of living of the laboring class determine a nation's ability to export goods cheaply. It specifically addresses why some nations can undersell others by employing more industrious workers who are content with less.</meta>

men who are either more diligent, and will stay longer at their work, or be content with a meaner and coarser way of living than those of their neighbors. This is certain: that where numbers are equal, the more industrious people are, and the fewer hands the same quantity of work is performed by, the greater plenty there is in a country of the necessities for life, and the more significant and cheaper that country may make its exports.

It being granted then, that an abundance of work is to be done, the next thing which I think to be likewise undeniable is, that the more cheerfully it is done the better, as well for those who perform it as for the rest of society. To be happy is to be pleased, and the less notion a man has of a better way of living, the more content he will be with his own; and on the other hand, the greater a man's knowledge and experience is in the world, the more exquisite the delicacy of his taste, and the more consummate a judge he is of things in general, certainly the more difficult it will be to please him. I would not propose anything that is barbarous or inhuman: but when a man enjoys himself, laughs and sings, and in his gesture and behavior shows me all the tokens of contentment and satisfaction, I pronounce him happy, and have nothing to do with his wit or capacity. I never enter into the reasonableness of his mirth; at least I ought not to judge of it by my own standard, and argue from the effect which the thing that makes him merry would have upon me. At that rate, a man who hates cheese must call me a fool for loving blue mold. *De gustibus non est disputandum* is as true in a metaphorical as it is in the literal sense, and the greater the distance is between people as to their condition, their circumstances, and manner of living, the less capable they are of judging one another's troubles or pleasures.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the relative happiness of different social classes. While the previous page discussed the necessity of an industrious laboring class that is content with its station, this page illustrates the point by comparing the psychological and social burdens of a king with the simple, though physically harsh, life of a peasant.</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>An Essay on Charity</header>

If the lowliest and most uncivilized peasant were allowed, *incognito*, to observe the greatest king for a fortnight, though he might pick out several things he would like for himself, he would still find many more things which—if he and the monarch were to switch places—he would wish to have immediately changed or corrected, and which he is amazed to see the king endure. And again, if the sovereign were to examine the peasant in the same way, the peasant's labor would be unbearable; his dirt and squalor, his diet and love affairs, and his pastimes and recreations would all be disgusting. But then, what charms would the king find in the other's peace of mind, and the calmness and tranquility of his soul? There would be no need for deception with any of his family, or pretended affection for his mortal enemies; no wife serving a foreign interest, and no danger to fear from his children; no plots to unravel, and no poison to fear; no popular statesman at home or cunning foreign courts to manage; no supposed patriots to bribe; no insatiable favorite to satisfy; no selfish ministry to obey; and no divided nation to please or fickle mob to humor, who would dictate and interfere with his pleasures.

If impartial reason were to be the judge between real good and real evil, and a list made accordingly of the various delights and vexations encountered in both stations, I doubt whether the condition of kings would be at all preferable to that of peasants—even as ignorant and hardworking as I seem to require the latter to be. The reason why the majority of people would rather be kings than peasants is, first, due to pride and ambition, which are deeply rooted in human nature, and to satisfy which we daily see men undergo and disregard the greatest hazards and difficulties. Secondly, it is due to the difference in the force with which our feelings are affected as the

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding social classes, specifically focusing on how the perception of happiness is influenced by one’s station and how national prosperity depends on a managed laboring class.</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>

Objects are either material or spiritual. Things that immediately strike our physical senses affect our passions more violently than those that are the result of thought and the dictates of the most logical reason; there is a much stronger bias toward liking or disliking the former than there is in the latter.

Having thus demonstrated that what I suggest would cause no injury or even the slightest decrease in happiness for the poor, I leave it to the wise reader to decide: is it not more likely that we should increase our exports through the methods I suggest, rather than by sitting still and cursing our neighbors for beating us at our own game? Some of them outsell us in manufactured goods made from our own raw materials—which they bought at a high price—while others grow rich, despite the distance and effort, from the very fish that we neglect, even though they are ready to jump into our mouths.

Just as you may compel the poor to work without force by discouraging idleness with skill and consistency, you may also condition them to endure real hardships—without them ever realizing they are hardships—by raising them in ignorance. By "raising them in ignorance," I mean nothing more (as I suggested long ago) than that their knowledge of worldly affairs should be confined within the boundaries of their own occupations; at least, we should not take any trouble to expand it beyond those limits. When we have used these two tools to make provisions, and consequently labor, cheap, we must inevitably outsell our neighbors and, at the same time, increase our population. This is the noble and courageous way of confronting our trade rivals and outperforming them in foreign markets through pure merit.

it is their boast that they will not live as the poor of other nations? If we cannot change their resolution, why should we applaud the fairness of their sentiments when they go against the common interest? I have often wondered in the past how an *Englishman*, who claimed to have the honor and glory as well as the welfare of his country at heart, could take delight in the evening hearing an idle tenant who owed him more than a year's rent ridicule the *French* for wearing wooden shoes, when in the morning he had suffered the mortification of hearing the great King *William*—that ambitious monarch as well as able statesman—openly admit to the world, with grief and anger in his expression, and complain of the exorbitant power of *France*. Yet I do not recommend wooden shoes, nor do the principles I would introduce require arbitrary power in one person. Liberty and property, I hope, may remain secure, and yet the poor be better employed than they are; though their children should wear out their clothes through useful labor and blacken them with country dirt for a purpose, instead of tearing them off their backs at play or staining them with ink for no reason.

There is more than three or four hundred years' worth of work for a hundred thousand more poor people than we currently have on this island. To make every part of it useful and the whole country thoroughly inhabited, many rivers need to be made navigable and canals cut in hundreds of places. Some lands must be drained and secured from future floods; much barren soil must be made fertile, and thousands of acres rendered more beneficial by being made more accessible. <insert>v. *The gods sell all things for labor.*</insert> There is no difficulty of this nature that labor and patience cannot overcome. --- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the need for large-scale public infrastructure projects, contrasting the efficiency and durability of ancient Roman works with the poor state of contemporary English roads.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
<header>and Charity-Schools.</header>

Let us look back on the stupendous works of the Romans, more especially their highways and aqueducts. Let us consider, on one hand, the vast extent of several of their roads—how substantial they made them and how long they have lasted—and on the other, a poor traveler who, at every ten-mile interval, is stopped by a turnpike and pestered for a penny to mend the roads in the summer with what everyone knows will be dirt before the succeeding winter has ended.

The convenience of the public ought always to be the public's concern, and no private interest of a town or a whole county should ever hinder the execution of a project or plan that would clearly tend to the improvement of the whole. Every member of the legislature who knows his duty and would choose to act like a wise man rather than curry favor with his neighbors will prefer even the smallest benefit accruing to the entire kingdom over the most obvious advantage to the place he represents.

We have materials of our own and lack neither stone nor timber to do anything. If the money that people give voluntarily to beggars who do not deserve it, along with what every householder is obliged to pay to the poor of his parish (which is otherwise misused or poorly applied), were put together every year, it would create a sufficient fund to keep many thousands of people at work. I do not say this because I think it is practical, but only to show that we have enough money to spare to employ vast multitudes of laborers. Nor would we need as much money for it as we might perhaps imagine. Since it is taken for granted that a soldier, whose strength and vigor must be maintained at least as much as anyone else's, can live on sixpence a day, I cannot understand the necessity of giving a laborer sixteen or eighteen pence a day for the greater part of the year.

The fearful and cautious people who are always jealous of their liberty, I know will cry out that if the multitudes I speak of were kept in constant pay, property and privileges would be precarious. But they might be answered that sure means could be found, and such regulations made regarding whose hands to trust with the management and direction of these laborers, that it would be impossible for the Prince or anyone else to make an ill use of their numbers.

What I have said in the last four or five paragraphs, I foresee will be laughed at with much scorn by many of my readers, and at best be called *building castles in the air*; but whether that is my fault or theirs is a question. When public spirit has left a nation, the people not only lose their patience with it and all thoughts of perseverance, but also become so narrow-minded that it is painful for them even to think of things that are of uncommon extent or require a great length of time; and whatever is noble or sublime in such circumstances is considered *chimerical*. Where deep ignorance is entirely defeated and expelled, and a little learning is scattered indiscriminately among all the people, *self-love* turns *knowledge* into *cunning*; and the more this latter quality prevails in any country, the more the people will focus all their care, concern, and effort on the present time, without regard for what is to come after them, or hardly ever thinking beyond the next generation.

But as *cunning*, according to my <term>Lord Verulam</term>, is but *left-handed wisdom*, so a prudent legislature ought to provide against this disorder of society as soon as its symptoms appear, among which the following are the most obvious. *Imaginary rewards* are generally despised; everyone is for *turning a penny* and *short bargains*; he who is distrustful of everything and believes nothing but what he sees with his own eyes is <unclear>count-</unclear>

...considered the most prudent, and in all their dealings, men seem to act from no other principle than that of *the devil take the hindmost*. Instead of planting oaks, which will require a hundred and fifty years before they are fit to be cut down, they build houses with a design that they shall not stand more than twelve or fourteen years. All minds focus on the uncertainty of things and the vicissitudes of human affairs. Mathematics becomes the only valuable study and is used in everything, even where it is ridiculous; and men seem to repose no greater trust in Providence than they would in a bankrupt merchant.

It is the business of the public to supply the defects of society, and take that in hand first which is most neglected by private persons. Opposites are best cured by opposites, and therefore, as example is more effective than precept in correcting national failings, the legislature ought to resolve upon some great undertakings that must be the work of ages as well as vast labor, and convince the world that they did nothing without an anxious regard for their latest posterity. This will fix, or at least help to settle, the volatile genius and fickle spirit of the kingdom, remind us that we are not born for ourselves only, and be a means of rendering men less distrustful and inspiring them with a true love for their country and a tender affection for the land itself—than which nothing is more necessary to aggrandize a nation. Forms of government may alter, religions and even languages may change, but *Great Britain*—or at least (if that too should lose its name) the island itself—will remain, and in all human probability last as long as any part of the globe. All ages have always paid their kind acknowledgments to their ancestors for the benefits derived from them; and a Christian who enjoys the multitude of fountains and vast plenty

Here I should have concluded this rhapsody of thoughts, but something comes to mind concerning the main scope and design of this essay—which is to prove the necessity for a certain portion of ignorance in a well-ordered society. I must not omit this, because by mentioning it I shall make an argument on my side regarding something that, if I had not spoken of it, might easily have appeared as a strong objection against me. It is the opinion of most people, and mine among the rest, that the most commendable quality of the current *Czar* of *Muscovy* <note>Peter the Great</note> is his unwearied application in raising his subjects from their native stupidity and civilizing his nation; but then we must consider that this is what they stood in need of, and that not long ago the majority of them were little better than *brute beasts*. In proportion to the extent of his dominions and the multitudes he commands, he did not have that number or variety of tradesmen and artisans which the true improvement of the country required, and therefore he was right to leave no stone unturned to procure them. ## A

## *Nature of Society.*

Most moralists and philosophers have until now agreed that there could be no virtue without self-denial; but a recent author, who is now widely read by people of sense, is of a contrary opinion and imagines that men, without any trouble or violence upon themselves, may be naturally virtuous. He seems to require and expect goodness in his species just as we do a sweet taste in grapes and China oranges <note>sweet oranges, which were a luxury import</note>, of which, if any of them are sour, we boldly pronounce that they have not reached that perfection of which their nature is capable. <page-num>268</page-num>
<header>A Search into the</header>

propensity to seek its welfare. In following this supposition, he calls every action performed with regard to the public good "virtuous," and all selfishness that completely excludes such a regard "vice." Regarding our species, he looks upon virtue and vice as permanent realities that must always be the same in all countries and all ages. He imagines that a man of sound understanding, by following the rules of good sense, may not only discover that *Pulchrum & Honestum* <note>The beautiful and the honorable</note> both in morality and in the works of art and nature, but also govern himself by his reason with as much ease and readiness as a good rider manages a well-trained horse by the bridle.

The attentive reader who has read the previous part of this book will soon perceive that two systems cannot be more opposite than his lordship's and mine. I confess his notions are generous and refined; they are a high compliment to humankind and are capable—with the help of a little enthusiasm—of inspiring us with the most noble sentiments concerning the dignity of our exalted nature. What a pity it is that they are not true. I would not say this much if I had not already demonstrated in almost every page of this treatise that their validity is inconsistent with our daily experience. But to leave not the slightest shadow of an objection unanswered, I intend to expand on some things that I have so far only touched upon briefly. This is in order to convince the reader not only that the good and amiable qualities of man are not what make him a social creature more than other animals, but also that it would be utterly impossible either to raise any great number of people into a populous, rich, and flourishing nation, or—once so raised—to keep and maintain them in that condition without the assistance of what we call evil, both natural and moral.

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into whether there is an objective, "intrinsic" worth in things—specifically examining the concepts of the beautiful and the honorable (*pulchrum & honestum*)—by using the subjective valuation of art as a primary example.</meta>

To better perform what I have undertaken, I shall first examine the reality of the *pulchrum & honestum*, the *tò kalon* <note>The beautiful and the honorable</note> that the ancients talked about so much. The meaning of this is to discuss whether there is a real worth and excellence in things—a superiority of one over another—which everyone who understands them well will always agree upon; or whether there are few things, if any, that receive the same esteem and are judged the same way in all countries and all ages. When we first set out in search of this intrinsic worth and find one thing better than another, and a third better than that, and so on, we begin to entertain great hopes of success; but when we encounter several things that are all very good or all very bad, we are puzzled and do not always agree with ourselves, much less with others. There are different faults as well as beauties that will be differently admired or disapproved of as styles and fashions change and men vary in their tastes and moods.

Judges of painting will never disagree in opinion when a fine picture is compared to the daubing of a novice; but how strangely they have differed regarding the works of eminent masters! There are factions among connoisseurs, and few of them agree in their esteem regarding different ages and countries, and the best pictures do not always command the best prices. A well-known original will always be worth more than any copy made of it by an unknown hand, even if the copy were better. The value set on paintings depends not only on the name of the master and the period of his life in which he painted them, but also, to a great extent, on the scarcity of his works. But what is even more unreasonable is that value depends on the status of the people who own them, as well as the length of time they have been in great families; and if the *Cartoons* <note>The Raphael Cartoons, a series of large designs for tapestries</note> now at *Hampton Court* were done by a less famous

...famous hand than that of *Raphael*, and had a private person for their owner who would be forced to sell them, they would never yield a tenth part of the money which, with all their gross faults, they are now esteemed to be worth.

Notwithstanding all this, I will readily admit that the judgment to be made of painting might achieve universal certainty, or at least be less alterable and precarious than almost anything else. The reason is plain: there is a standard to follow that always remains the same. Painting is an *imitation* of nature, a *copying* of things which men have everywhere before them. I hope my good-humored reader will forgive me if, thinking on this glorious invention, I make a *reflection* a little out of season, though very much conducive to my main design. That is: however valuable the *art* I speak of may be, we are beholden to an *imperfection* in the chief of our senses for all the pleasures and ravishing delight we receive from this happy *deceit*. I shall explain myself. Air and space are not objects of sight, but as soon as we can see with even the least attention, we observe that the bulk of the things we see is lessened by degrees as they are further remote from us; and nothing but experience gained from these observations can teach us to make any tolerable guesses at the distance of things. If someone born blind should remain so until twenty and then be suddenly blessed with sight, he would be strangely puzzled regarding the difference in distances, and would hardly be able immediately, by his eyes alone, to determine which was nearest to him: a post almost within the reach of his stick, or a steeple that might be half a mile away. Let us look as closely as we can upon a hole in a wall that has nothing but the open air behind it, and we shall not be able to see it any other way than that the sky fills up the vacancy and is as near to us as the back part of the stones that surround the space

This circumstance, not to call it a defect, in our sense of sight, makes us liable to be deceived; and everything, except motion, may by art be represented to us on a flat surface in the same manner as we see them in life and nature. If a man had never seen this art put into practice, a looking glass might soon convince him that such a thing was possible; and I cannot help thinking that the reflections from very smooth and well-polished bodies made upon our eyes must have given the first occasion for the inventions of drawing and painting.

In the works of nature, worth and excellence are just as uncertain; and even in human beings, what is beautiful in one country is not so in another. How whimsical is the florist in his choice! Sometimes the tulip, sometimes the auricula, and at other times the carnation shall monopolize his esteem, and every year a new flower in his judgment surpasses all the old ones, though it is much inferior to them both in color and shape. Three hundred years ago, men were shaved as closely as they are now; since then, they have worn beards and cut them in a vast variety of forms that were all as becoming when fashionable as they would now be ridiculous. How shabby and comical a man looks, who is otherwise well-dressed, in a narrow-brimmed hat when everybody else wears broad ones; and again, how monstrous is a very great hat when the other extreme has been in fashion for a considerable time? Experience has taught us that these modes seldom last more than ten or twelve years, and a man of sixty must have observed five or six revolutions of them at least; yet the beginnings of these changes, though we have seen several, always seem strange and are offensive anew whenever they return. The ways of laying out a garden judiciously are almost innumerable, and what is called beautiful in them varies according to the different tastes of nations and ages. In grass plots, knots, and parterres, a great diversity of forms is generally agreeable; but a circle may be as pleasing to the eye as a square. An oval cannot be more suitable to one place than it is possible for a triangle to be to another; and the superiority an octagon has over a hexagon is no greater among geometric figures than the advantage an eight has over a six among the chances in a game of Hazard.

Churches, ever since Christians have been able to build them, resemble the form of a cross, with the upper end pointing toward the east; and an architect who should neglect this, where there is room and it can be conveniently done, would be thought to have committed an unpardonable fault. But it would be foolish to expect this of a Turkish mosque or a pagan temple. Among the many beneficial laws that have been made these hundred years, it is not easy to name one of greater utility, and at the same time more exempt from all inconveniences, than that which has regulated the clothing of the dead. Those who were old enough to notice things when that Act was made, and are still alive, must remember the general outcry that was raised against it. At first, nothing could be more shocking to thousands of people than the idea that they were to be buried in wool; and the only thing that made that law bearable was that there was room left for people of some fashion to indulge their weakness without extravagance, considering the other expenses of funerals where mourning clothes are given to several people and rings to a great many. The benefit that accrues to the nation from it is so visible that nothing could ever be said in reason to condemn it, which in a few years made the horror conceived against it lessen every day. By this time, burying in linen being almost forgotten, it is the general opinion that nothing could be more decent than woollen, and the present manner of dressing a corpse; which shows that our liking or disliking of things chiefly depends on fashion and custom, and the precept and example of our betters and those whom, in one way or another, we think to be superior to us.

In morals there is no greater certainty. A plurality of wives is odious among Christians, and all the wit and learning of a great genius in defense of it has been rejected with contempt; but polygamy is not shocking to a Muslim <note>Mahometan</note>. What men have learned from their infancy enslaves them, and the force of custom warps nature, and at the same time imitates her in such a manner that it is often difficult to know which of the two influences us. In the East formerly, sisters married brothers, and it was meritorious for a man to marry his mother. Such alliances are abominable; but it is certain that, whatever horror we conceive at the thoughts of them, there is nothing in nature repugnant against them except what is built upon fashion and custom. A religious Muslim <note>Mahometan</note> who has never tasted any spirituous liquor, and has often seen people drunk, may develop as great an aversion to wine as another person among us, with even the least morality and education, may have against lying with his sister, and both imagine that their antipathy proceeds from nature. It is manifest then that hunting after this *Pulchrum & Honestum* <note>The Beautiful and the Honorable</note> is not much better than a wild-goose chase that is little to be depended upon. But this is not the greatest fault I find with it. The imaginary notions that men may be virtuous without self-denial are a vast inlet to hypocrisy, which, once being made habitual, causes us not only to deceive others but likewise to become altogether unknown to ourselves. In an instance I am going to give, it will appear how, for want of duly examining himself, this might happen to a person of quality, of talent <note>parts</note> and erudition—one in every way resembling the author of the *Characteristics* <note>Lord Shaftesbury</note> himself.

A man who has been brought up in ease and affluence, if he is of a quiet, indolent nature, learns to shun everything that is troublesome, and chooses to curb his passions more because of the inconveniences that arise from the eager pursuit after pleasure and the yielding to all the demands of our inclinations, than from any dislike he has for sensual enjoyments. It is possible that a person educated under a great philosopher, who was a mild and good-natured as well as able tutor, may in such happy circumstances have a better opinion of his inward state than it really deserves, and believe himself virtuous because his passions lie dormant. A man who deals in metaphysics may easily throw himself into a state of enthusiasm, and really believe that he does not fear death while it remains out of sight. But should he be asked why—having this fearlessness either from nature or acquired by philosophy—he did not take up arms when his country was involved in war; or when he saw the nation daily robbed by those at the helm, and the affairs of the Exchequer troubled, why he did not go to court and make use of all his friends and influence to become a Lord Treasurer, so that by his integrity and wise management he might restore the public credit; it is probable he would answer that he loved retirement, had no other ambition than to be a good man, and never aspired to have any share in the government, or that he hated all flattery and slavish attendance, the insincerity of courts, and the bustle of the world. I am willing to believe him: but may not a man of an indolent temper and inactive spirit say all this, and be sincere in it, while at the same time indulging his appetites without being able to subdue them, even though his duty summons him to do so? Virtue consists in action, and whoever is possessed of this Social Love and kind affection for his species, and by his birth or rank can claim any post in public management, ought not to sit still when he can be serviceable, but should exert himself to the utmost for the good of his fellow subjects. Had this noble person been of a warlike disposition or a boisterous temper, he would have chosen another part in the drama of life, and preached a completely contrary doctrine: for we are always pushing our reason whichever way we feel passion drawing it, and self-love pleads to all human creatures for their different views, still furnishing every individual with arguments to justify their inclinations.

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Lord Shaftesbury's philosophy, arguing that "calm virtues" and an innate sense of goodness are insufficient to drive human progress or overcome natural laziness.</meta>

<page-num>276</page-num>
<header>A Search into the</header>

That boasted middle way, and the calm virtues recommended in the *Characteristics*, are good for nothing but to breed drones, and might qualify a man for the stupid enjoyments of a monastic life, or at best a country justice of the peace, but they would never fit him for labor and assiduity, or stir him up to great achievements and perilous undertakings. Man's natural love of ease and idleness, and proneness to indulge his sensual pleasures, are not to be cured by precept: his strong habits and inclinations can only be subdued by passions of greater violence. Preach and demonstrate to a coward the unreasonableness of his fears, and you will not make him valiant, any more than you can make him taller by bidding him to be ten feet high, whereas the secret to raise courage, as I have made it public in *Remark R*, is almost infallible.

The fear of death is strongest when we are in our greatest vigor, and our appetite is keen; when we are sharp-sighted, quick of hearing, and every part performs its office. The reason is plain, because then life is most delicious and ourselves most capable of enjoying it. How comes it then that a man of honor should so easily accept a challenge, though at thirty and in perfect health? It is his pride that conquers his fear: for when his pride is not concerned this fear will appear most glaringly. If he is not used to the sea, let him but be in a storm, or, if he never was ill before, have but a sore throat or a slight fever, and he will show a thousand anxieties, and in them the inestimable value he sets on life. Had man been naturally humble and proof against flattery, the politician could never have had his ends, or known what to have made of him. Without vices the excellency of the species would have ever remained undiscovered, and every worthy who has made himself famous in the world is a strong evidence against this amiable system.

<summary>The author argues that human progress and great achievements are driven by strong passions like pride rather than "calm virtues," which only encourage idleness. <meta>This page continues the argument that human achievements are driven by passions like pride and vanity rather than "calm virtues." <page-num>277</page-num>

If the courage of the great *Macedonian* reached the level of madness when he fought alone against a whole garrison, his delusion was no less when he imagined himself to be a god, or at least doubted whether he was or not. As soon as we reflect on this, we discover both the passion and the extravagance of it that buoyed up his spirits in the most imminent dangers and carried him through all the difficulties and fatigues he underwent.

There never was in the world a brighter example of an able and complete magistrate than *Cicero*! When I think of his care and vigilance, the real hazards he ignored, and the pains he took for the safety of *Rome*; his wisdom and sagacity in detecting and disappointing the strategies of the boldest and most subtle conspirators; and at the same time his love for literature, arts, and sciences, his capacity in metaphysics, the accuracy of his reasonings, the force of his eloquence, the refinement of his style, and the noble spirit that runs through his writings—when I think, I say, on all these things together, I am struck with amazement, and the least I can say of him is that he was an extraordinary man. But when I have set the many good qualities he had in the best light, it is as evident to me on the other side that, had his vanity been inferior to his greatest excellence, the good sense and knowledge of the world he was so eminently possessed of could never have let him be such a disgusting as well as noisy proclaimer of his own praises as he was. Nor would it have allowed him, rather than not proclaim his own merit, to write a verse that a schoolboy would have been laughed at for: *O! Had this violent motive not overruled his consummate prudence, he might not only have saved himself but likewise most of his friends who were ruined by his loss; and he would, in all probability, have been the second man in *Rome* if he could have stooped to it. But he knew the boundless mind and unlimited generosity of the victor; it was his clemency he feared, and therefore he chose death because it was less terrible to his pride than the thought of giving his mortal foe such a tempting opportunity to show the magnanimity of his soul. *Caesar* would have found such an opportunity in forgiving an inveterate enemy like *Cato* and offering him his friendship; it is thought by the judicious that this penetrating as well as ambitious conqueror would not have let that opportunity slip if the other had dared to live.

Another argument to prove the kind disposition and real affection we naturally have for our species is our love of company, and the aversion that men in their right senses generally have toward solitude, beyond other creatures. This is given a fine gloss in the *Characteristics*, and is presented in very good language to the best advantage. I would not want any wise critic to pronounce me a misanthrope from this short story; whoever does so is very much mistaken. I am a great lover of company, and if the reader is not quite tired of mine before I show the weakness and absurdity of that piece of flattery made toward our species, which I was just now speaking of, I will give him a description of the man I would choose for conversation. I promise that before he has finished what at first he might only take for a digression irrelevant to my purpose, he shall find the use of it.

By early and skillful instruction, he should be thoroughly imbued with the notions of honor and shame, and have developed a habitual aversion to everything that has the least tendency toward impudence, rudeness, or inhumanity. He should be well-versed in the *Latin* language and not ignorant of *Greek*, and moreover understand one or two modern languages besides his own. He should be acquainted with the fashions and customs of the ancients, but thoroughly skilled in the history of his own country and the manners of the age he lives in. In addition to literature, he should have studied some useful science or other, visited some foreign courts and universities, and made the true use of traveling. <meta>This page continues the author's advice on the upbringing of a gentleman, moving from the management of youthful mistakes to the definition of "good company" and the social virtues of conversation.</meta>

...after. The very disasters of youth have sometimes frightened gentlemen into a more steady prudence than, in all probability, they would ever have mastered without them. To keep him from turpitude and things that are openly scandalous, there is nothing better than to procure him free access to one or two noble families where his frequent attendance is counted a duty; and while by that means you preserve his pride, he is kept in a continual dread of shame.

A man of a tolerable fortune, nearly as accomplished as I have required him to be, who still improves himself and sees the world until he is thirty, cannot be disagreeable to converse with—at least while he continues in health and prosperity and has nothing to spoil his temper. When such a person, either by chance or appointment, meets with three or four of his equals and all agree to pass away a few hours together, the whole is what I call good company. There is nothing said in it that is not either instructive or diverting to a man of sense. It is possible they may not always be of the same opinion, but there can be no contest between any except over who shall yield first to the person from whom he differs. Only one speaks at a time, and no louder than to be plainly understood by the person sitting farthest away. The greatest pleasure aimed at by every one of them is to have the satisfaction of pleasing others, which they all practically know may be as effectively done by listening with attention and an approving countenance as if we said very good things ourselves.

Most people of any taste would like such a conversation and justly prefer it to being alone when they did not know how to spend their time; but if they could employ themselves in something from which they expected either a more solid or a more lasting satisfaction, they would deny themselves this pleasure and follow what was of greater conse-

But would not a man, though he had seen no human being in a fortnight, remain alone just as much longer, rather than join the company of **noisy fellows** who take delight in contradiction and find glory in picking a quarrel? Would not one who has books read forever, or set himself to write upon some subject or other, rather than spend every night with **party-men** who consider the island to be good for nothing while their adversaries are permitted to live upon it? Would not a man be by himself for a month, and go to bed before seven o'clock, rather than mix with **fox-hunters** who, having tried in vain all day long to break their necks, join together at night in a second attempt upon their lives by drinking, and to express their mirth, are louder with senseless noises indoors than their barking and less troublesome companions are outside? I have no great respect for a man who would not rather tire himself with walking—or if he were shut up, scatter pins about the room in order to pick them up again—than keep company for six hours with ten common **sailors** on the day their ship was paid off.

I will grant, nevertheless, that the greatest part of **mankind**, rather than be alone for any considerable time, would submit to the things I have named. But I cannot see why this love of company, this strong desire for society, should be interpreted so much in our favor and alleged as a mark of some **intrinsic worth** in man not to be found in other animals. For to prove from it the goodness of our nature and a generous love in man—extending beyond himself to the rest of his species, by virtue of which he is a **sociable creature**—this eagerness for company and aversion to being alone ought to have been most conspicuous and most intense in the best of their kind: the men of the greatest **genius**, talent, and accomplishments, and those who are the least subject to vices; the contrary of which

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that man's desire for company is rooted in self-interest and psychological needs—such as the desire for dominance or entertainment—rather than an innate, selfless love for his species.</meta>

<warning>Heavy ink saturation causes some characters to appear blotchy.</warning>

...is true. The weakest minds, who are least able to govern their passions; guilty consciences that abhor reflection; and the worthless, who are incapable of producing anything useful of their own, are the greatest enemies of solitude. They will settle for any company rather than be without it; whereas men of sense and knowledge, who can think and contemplate things and are but little disturbed by their passions, can bear to be by themselves the longest without reluctance. To avoid noise, folly, and impertinence, they will flee from twenty groups; rather than encounter anything disagreeable to their good taste, they will prefer their study or a garden—indeed, even a common field or a desert—to the society of certain men.

But let us suppose the love of company were so inseparable from our species that no man could endure to be alone for one moment; what conclusions could be drawn from this? Does man not love company, just as he loves everything else, for his own sake? No friendships or civilities are lasting if they are not reciprocal. In all your weekly and daily meetings for recreation, as well as annual feasts and the most solemn carousals, every member who attends them has his own goals. Some frequent a club they would never go to unless they were the leader of it. I have known a man who was the "oracle" of his company, who was very regular and felt uneasy at anything that hindered him from arriving on time, yet left his circle altogether as soon as another member was added who could match him and disputed his superiority. There are people who are incapable of holding an argument themselves, yet are malicious enough to take delight in hearing others quarrel. Although they never involve themselves in the controversy, they would think a company dull if they could not have that entertainment.

Even the most polite people in the world, and those of whom I spoke at first, give no pleasure to others that is not repaid to their self-love, and does not at last center in themselves, no matter how they twist and turn it. But the clearest demonstration that in all clubs and societies of sociable people everyone has the greatest consideration for himself is that the disinterested person, who would rather overpay than wrangle; the good-humored person, who is never irritable nor easily offended; and the easygoing and indolent person, who hates disputes and never talks for the sake of triumph, is everywhere the darling of the company. In contrast, the man of sense and knowledge who will not be deceived or talked out of his reason; the man of genius and spirit who can say sharp and witty things, though he never lashes out at anything but what deserves it; and the man of honor who neither gives nor takes an insult, may be esteemed, but is seldom as well-loved as a weaker, less accomplished man.

Just as in these instances friendly qualities arise from our perpetually contriving our own satisfaction, so on other occasions they proceed from the natural timidity of man and the anxious care he takes of himself. Two Londoners, whose business does not require them to have any dealings together, may know, see, and pass by one another every day upon the Exchange with no more civility than bulls would show. Yet let them meet at *Bristol*, and they will tip their hats; at the slightest opportunity, they will enter into conversation and be glad for one another's company. <unclear>As</unclear> all Europeans, they look upon one another as countrymen, and if no passion interferes, will feel a natural inclination to love one another. In fact, two men who are at odds, if they are forced to travel together, will often set aside their animosities, be affable, and converse in a friendly manner—especially if the road is unsafe and they are both strangers in the place to which they are going. Superficial judges attribute these things to man’s sociability, his natural inclination toward friendship, and his love of company; but whoever examines things properly and observes man more closely will find that on all these occasions we only endeavor to strengthen our own interest and are moved by the causes already alleged.

What I have attempted so far has been to prove that the *pulchrum & honestum* <note>Latin: the beautiful and the honorable</note>, the excellence and real worth of things, are most commonly precarious and change as fashions and customs vary; consequently, the inferences drawn from their certainty are insignificant, and the noble notions concerning the natural goodness of man are harmful, as they tend to mislead and are merely imaginary. I have illustrated the truth of this latter point with the most obvious examples in history. I have spoken of our love of company and aversion to solitude, examined their various motives thoroughly, and made it appear that they all center in self-love. I intend now to investigate the nature of society and, diving into its very origins, make it evident that it is not the good and amiable qualities of man, but his bad and hateful qualities—his imperfections and his lack of the excellencies with which other creatures are endowed—that are the primary causes that made man sociable beyond other animals the moment after he lost Paradise. If he had remained in his primitive innocence and continued to enjoy the blessings that attended it, there is no shadow of probability that

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that human sociability is not an innate virtue, but rather a product of our needs and the difficulties of survival. The author begins to explore how the hostile environment forces humans together for self-preservation.</meta>

How necessary our appetites and passions are for the welfare of all trades and handicrafts has been sufficiently proven throughout the book; and no one denies that they are our bad qualities, or at least produce them. It remains, then, for me to set forth the variety of obstacles that hinder and perplex man in the labor he is constantly employed in—the procuring of what he wants, which in other words is called the business of self-preservation—while at the same time I demonstrate that the sociability of man arises only from these two things: namely, the multiplicity of his desires, and the continual opposition he meets with in his endeavors to gratify them.

The *obstacles* I speak of relate either to our own *frame* or the *globe* we inhabit; I mean the *condition* of it since it has been cursed. I have often endeavored to contemplate the two *things* I named last separately, but could never keep them asunder; they always interfere and mix with one another, and at last make up together a frightful *chaos* of *evil*. All the *elements* are our *enemies*: *water* drowns and *fire* consumes those who unskillfully approach them. The *earth* in a *thousand places* produces *plants* and other *vegetables* that are hurtful to *man*, while she feeds and cherishes a variety of *creatures* that are noxious to him, and suffers a *legion* of *poisons* to dwell within her. But the most unkind of all the *elements* is that which we cannot live one moment without: it is impossible to repeat all the *injuries* we receive from the *wind* and *weather*; and though the greatest part of *mankind* has ever been employed in defending their *species* from the *inclemency* of the *air*, yet no *art* or *labor* has hitherto been able to find a *security* against the *wild rage* of some *meteors* <note>In the 18th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, such as storms, lightning, or heavy winds.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's argument that civilization is a product of human necessity and flaw rather than inherent social virtue, contrasting current society with a hypothetical "Golden Age."</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>
<header>A Search into the</header>

...injuries of the air as well as all other harms, contented with the necessaries of life, which the globe he inhabited furnished him with without assistance. When he was not yet conscious of guilt, he found himself in every place to be the well-obeyed, unrivaled lord of all; and, unaffected by his greatness, he was wholly wrapped up in sublime meditations on the infinity of his Creator, who daily graciously deigned to speak to him intelligibly and visit him without harm.

In such a Golden Age, no reason or probability can be alleged why mankind should ever have raised themselves into such large societies as have been in the world for as long as we can give a tolerable account of it. Where a man has everything he desires and nothing to vex or disturb him, there is nothing that can be added to his happiness; and it is impossible to name a trade, art, science, dignity, or employment that would not be superfluous in such a blessed state. If we pursue this thought, we shall easily perceive that no society could have sprung from the amiable virtues or loving qualities of man; but on the contrary, all of them must have had their origin from his wants, his imperfections, and the variety of his appetites. We shall find likewise that the more their pride and vanity are displayed and all their desires enlarged, the more capable they must be of being raised into large and vastly numerous societies.

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument from page 289, exploring how human nature—specifically fear, self-interest, and the need for peace—necessitates the structures of government and the use of social hypocrisy.</meta>

<page-num>290</page-num>
<header>A Search into the</header>
<meta>Heavy ink staining and bleed-through on the right side of the page, particularly in the lower quadrant.</meta>

as long as he lived and had his senses, to maintain a superior sway over all his own offspring and descendants, however numerous they might grow. But once the old stock was dead, the sons would quarrel, and there could be no peace for long before there had been war. Seniority among brothers carries no great weight, and the pre-eminence that is given to it was only invented as an expedient to live in peace. Man, being a fearful animal and not naturally rapacious, loves peace and quiet, and he would never fight if nobody offended him and if he could have what he fights for without it. To this fearful disposition and the aversion he has to being disturbed, we owe all the various projects and forms of government. Monarchy, without doubt, was the first. Aristocracy and democracy were two different methods of correcting the inconveniences of the first, and a mixture of these three was an improvement on all the rest.

But whether we are savages or politicians, it is impossible that man—mere fallen man—should act with any other goal than to please himself while he has the use of his faculties, and the greatest excesses of either love or despair can have no other center. In one sense, there is no difference between will and pleasure, and every motion made in spite of them must be unnatural and convulsive. Since action is so confined, then, and we are always forced to do what pleases us while our thoughts are simultaneously free and uncontrolled, it is impossible we could be sociable creatures without hypocrisy. The proof of this is plain: since we cannot prevent the ideas that are continually arising within us, all social interaction would be lost if we had not learned, through art and prudent dissimulation, to hide and stifle them. If all we think were laid open to others in the same manner as it is <unclear>to</unclear> ourselves, it is impossible that, endowed with <unclear>speech</unclear>, we could be tolerable to one another. <meta>catchword: I</meta>

[per]suaded that every reader feels the truth of what I say; and I tell my antagonist that his conscience flies in his face, while his tongue is preparing to refute me. In all civil societies, men are taught imperceptibly to be hypocrites from their cradle; nobody dares to admit that he profits from public calamities, or even from the loss of private persons. The sexton would be stoned should he wish openly for the death of the parishioners, though everybody knows that he has nothing else to live on.

To me, it is a great pleasure, when I look at the affairs of human life, to behold the various and often strangely opposite forms into which the hope of gain and thoughts of profit shape men, according to the different employments they hold and stations they are in. How gay and merry every face appears at a well-ordered ball, and what a solemn sadness is observed at the masquerade of a funeral! But the undertaker is as much pleased with his gains as the dancing master: both are equally tired in their occupations, and the mirth of the one is as much forced as the gravity of the other is affected. Those who have never paid attention to the conversation of a spruce mercer <note>A dealer in expensive fabrics, especially silks.</note> and a young lady who is his customer when she comes to his shop, have neglected a scene of life that is very entertaining. I beg of my serious reader that he would, for a while, set aside a little of his gravity and allow me to examine these people separately, as to their inner thoughts and the different motives they act from.

His business is to sell as much silk as he can at a price by which he shall get what he considers reasonable, according to the customary profits of the trade. As for the lady, what she wants is to please her fancy and buy the items she wants a groat <note>An old English coin worth four pence.</note> or sixpence cheaper *per* yard than they are commonly sold for. When two persons are so well met, the conversation must be very agreeable, as well as extremely mannerly, though they talk about trifles. While she remains irresolute about what to take, he seems to be the same in advising her; and is very cautious how to direct her choice; but when once she has made it and is fixed, he immediately becomes positive that it is the best of the sort, extols her taste, and the more he looks upon it, the more he wonders why he should not before have discovered the pre-eminence of it over anything he has in his shop. By precept, example, and great application, he has learned unobserved to slide into the inmost recesses of the soul, found the capacity of his customers, and found out their blind side unknown to them: in all which he is instructed in fifty other stratagems to make her overvalue her own judgment as well as the commodity she would purchase.
The greatest advantage he has over her lies in the most material part of the commerce between them, the debate about the price, which he knows to a farthing, and she is wholly ignorant of: Therefore he nowhere more egregiously imposes on her understanding; and though here he has the liberty of telling what lies he pleases, as to the prime cost <note>The original purchase price paid by the merchant.</note> and the money he has refused, yet he trusts not to them only; but attacking her vanity makes her believe the most incredible things in the world, concerning his own weakness and her superior abilities; He had taken a resolution, he says, never to part with that piece under such a price, but she has the power of talking him out of his goods beyond anybody he ever sold to: He protests that he loses by his silk, but seeing that she has a fancy for it, and is resolved to give no more, rather than disoblige a lady he has such an uncommon value for, he'll let her have it, and only begs that another time she will not bargain so hard with him. In the meantime, the buyer, who knows she is no fool and has a ready tongue, is easily persuaded that she has a very charming way of talking. Thinking it sufficient for the sake of good manners to downplay her own merit and retort the compliment with a witty remark, she contentedly swallows the substance of everything he tells her. The outcome is that, with the satisfaction of having saved ninepence *per* yard, she has bought her silk at exactly the same price as anyone else might have, and often pays sixpence more than he would have accepted rather than not sell it at all.

It is possible that this lady—due to a lack of sufficient flattery, a fault she chooses to find in his behavior, perhaps the way his necktie is tied, or some other equally "substantial" dislike—may be lost as a customer, and her business bestowed on some other member of the trade. But where many of them are grouped together, it is not always easy to decide which shop to visit, and the reasons some women have for their choice are often very whimsical and kept as a great secret. We never follow our inclinations more freely than when they cannot be traced, and it is unreasonable for others to suspect them. One virtuous woman preferred one shop over all the rest because she had seen a handsome fellow in it; another woman of good character chose a shop because she had received greater civility in front of it than had been paid her anywhere else, back when she had no intention of buying anything and was simply walking to *St. Paul’s* Church. Among fashionable mercers, the honest dealer must stay in front of his own door and, to draw in random customers, use no other forwardness or persistence than an obedient air, a submissive posture, and perhaps a bow to every well-dressed woman who approaches his shop.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of human interaction and the "nature of society," moving from the sophisticated flattery of the urban mercer to the aggressive, physical solicitation practiced by river watermen toward country peasants.</meta>

<header>Nature of Society.</header>

What I said last makes me think of another way of attracting customers, one that is as different as possible from what I have just been discussing. I mean the method used by the watermen, especially toward those they recognize as peasants by their appearance and clothing. It is quite a sight to see half a dozen people surround a man they have never seen in their lives before. Two of them, who manage to get the closest, each throw an arm over his neck and hug him as lovingly and familiarly as if he were their brother just returned home from an *East India voyage*. A third person grabs his hand, another his sleeve, his coat, its buttons, or anything else he can reach. Meanwhile, a fifth or sixth person, who has already run around him twice without being able to reach him, plants himself directly in front of the man they are holding. Within three inches of the man's nose, he shouts over his rivals with an open-mouthed cry, revealing a terrifying set of large teeth and the small remains of chewed bread and cheese, which the countryman's arrival had prevented him from swallowing.

No offense is taken at any of this, and the peasant rightly thinks they are making a great fuss over him. Therefore, far from resisting them, he patiently allows himself to be pushed or pulled in whichever direction the surrounding crowd's strength directs him. He is not refined enough to find fault with the breath of a man who has just finished his pipe, or a greasy head of hair rubbing against his face. He has been used to dirt and sweat since his cradle, and it does not bother him to hear ten people—some right at his ear and the furthest no more than five feet away—shouting at the top of their lungs as if he were a hundred yards off. He is aware that he makes just as much noise when he is happy himself, and he is secretly pleased by their rowdy behavior. He interprets the hauling and pulling as it is intended: it is a form of courtship he can feel and under-
