I have seen a man think all this, or something like it, as plainly as I could see the nose on his face; at the same time, he moves along very contentedly under a load of watermen and, with a smiling face, carries seven or eight <term>stone</term> <note>A unit of weight equal to 14 pounds.</note> more than his own weight to the water’s edge.

If the little humor I have shown in drawing these two images from low life is unbecoming of me, I am sorry for it; but I promise not to be guilty of that fault anymore. I will now, without loss of time, proceed with my argument in simple, unadorned prose to demonstrate the gross error of those who imagine that the social virtues and the amiable qualities that are praiseworthy in us are as beneficial to the public as they are to the individuals who possess them, and that the means of thriving—and whatever contributes to the welfare and real happiness of private families—must have the same effect upon society as a whole. I confess I have worked toward this goal all along, and I flatter myself that I have not been unsuccessful. But I hope nobody will dislike a problem any more for seeing its truth proved in more ways than one.

It is certain that the fewer desires a man has and the less he covets, the easier his life is; the more active he is in supplying his own wants

It is the sensual courtier who sets no limits to his luxury; the fickle strumpet who invents new fashions every week; the haughty duchess who, in her equipage, entertainments, and all her behavior, would imitate a princess; the profuse rake and lavish heir, who scatter about their money without wit or judgment, buy everything they see, and either destroy or give it away the next day; the covetous and perjured villain who squeezed an immense treasure from the tears of widows and orphans, and left the prodigals the money to spend: it is these that are the prey and proper food of a full-grown <term>Leviathan</term>; or in other words, such is the calamitous condition of human affairs that we stand in need of the plagues and monsters I named to have all the variety of labor performed, which the skill of men is capable of inventing in order to procure an honest livelihood for the vast multitudes of working poor that are required to make a large society. And it is folly to imagine that great and wealthy nations can subsist, and be at once powerful and polite, without them.

I protest against Popery as much as ever *Luther* or *Calvin* did, or *Queen Elizabeth* herself, but I believe from my heart that the Reformation has scarcely been more instrumental in rendering the kingdoms and states that have embraced it flourishing beyond other nations than the silly and capricious invention of hooped and quilted petti-

But if this should be denied me by the enemies of priestly power, at least I am sure that—setting aside the great men who have fought for and against that layman’s blessing—it has from its first beginning to this day not employed as many hands (honest, industrious, laboring hands) as the abominable improvement in female luxury I mentioned has done in a few years. Religion is one thing, and trade is another. He who gives the most trouble to thousands of his neighbors and invents the most laborious manufactures is, right or wrong, the greatest friend to society.

What a bustle must be made in several parts of the world before a fine scarlet or crimson cloth can be produced, and what a multiplicity of trades and craftsmen must be employed! Not only those who are obvious, such as wool-combers, spinners, the weaver, the cloth-worker, the scourer, the dyer, the setter, the drawer, and the packer; but others who are more remote and might seem foreign to it, such as the millwright, the pewterer, and the chemist. These are all necessary, as are a great number of other handicrafts, to provide the tools, utensils, and other implements belonging to the trades already named. But all these things are done at home and may be performed without extraordinary fatigue or danger; the most daunting prospect remains when we reflect on the toil and hazard that must be undergone abroad: the vast seas we must cross, the different climates we must endure, and the several nations we must be indebted to for their assistance. Spain alone, it is true, might furnish us with wool to make the finest cloth; but what skill and effort, what experience and ingenuity are required to dye all those beautiful colors! How widely dispersed throughout the universe are the drugs and other ingredients that must meet in one kettle. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the immense scale and risks of international trade, specifically shifting the focus to the physical and human costs associated with global shipping.</meta>

<page-num>303</page-num>

->Nature of Society.<-

Should people not talk of their ships and navigation as a special blessing to them, and find an uncommon happiness in having an infinite number of vessels dispersed throughout the wide world, with some always going to and others coming from every part of the universe?

But for once, in our consideration of these things, let us confine ourselves only to what the ships suffer—the vessels themselves with their rigging and accessories—without thinking of the cargo they carry or the hands that work them. We shall find that the damage sustained in that way alone is very considerable and must, one year with another, amount to vast sums. There are the ships that are foundered at sea, split against rocks, and swallowed up by sands—some entirely by the fierceness of tempests, others by that combined with a pilot’s lack of experience and knowledge of the coasts. There are the masts that are blown down or forced to be cut and thrown overboard; the yards, sails, and cordage of different sizes that are destroyed by storms; and the anchors that are lost. Add to these the necessary repairs for leaks that have sprung and other damage received from the rage of the winds and the violence of the waves. Many ships are set on fire by carelessness and the effects of strong liquors, which no one is more addicted to than sailors. Sometimes unhealthy climates, and at other times bad provisions, breed fatal diseases that sweep away the greatest part of the crew; not a few ships are lost for lack of hands.

These are all calamities inseparable from navigation, and they seem to be great impediments that clog the wheels of foreign commerce. How happy a merchant would think himself if his ships always had fine weather and the wind he wished for, and if every mariner he employed—from the highest to the lowest—were a knowledgeable, experienced sailor and a careful, sober, good man! If such

<meta>This page continues the thought experiment regarding the consequences of perfect maritime safety and success, exploring the economic impact if ships and cargo were never lost to the elements.</meta>

<page-num>304</page-num>
<header>A Search into the</header>

<margin>If</margin> happiness were to be had for prayers, what shipowner or dealer in *Europe*—nay, the whole world—would not be teasing Heaven all day long to obtain such a blessing for himself, without regard for what harm it would do to others? Such a petition would certainly be a very unreasonable one, yet where is the man who does not imagine that he has a right to make it? And therefore, as everyone claims an equal right to those favors, let us—without reflecting on the impossibility of its being true—suppose all their prayers were effective and their wishes answered, and afterwards examine the result of such a happiness.

Ships would last fully as long as timber houses, because they are built just as strongly, and the latter are liable to suffer from high winds and other storms, which the former (by our supposition) are not. So, before there would be any real occasion for new ships, the master builders now living, and everyone under them who is set to work on them, would all die a natural death, if they did not starve or come to some untimely end first. For in the first place, if all ships had favorable winds and never waited for the wind, they would make very quick voyages both outgoing and returning. Secondly, no merchandise would be damaged by the sea or thrown overboard by the stress of weather, but the entire cargo would always come safely ashore. From this it would follow that three parts out of four of the merchant ships already built would be superfluous for the present, and the stock of ships now in the world would serve for a great many years. Masts and yards would last as long as the vessels themselves, and we would not need to bother *Norway* on that account for a long while yet. The sails and rigging of the few ships used would wear out, of course, but not a quarter as fast as they do now, for they often suffer more in a one-hour storm than in ten days of fair weather.

He suggests that the destruction and delays caused by nature are actually necessary for maintaining the global economy and demand for labor.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the hypothetical exploration of a world without maritime disasters, focusing on how such safety would cause economic stagnation by eliminating the need for constant replacement of ships and cargo.</meta>

<page-num>305</page-num>

### Nature of Society.

There would seldom be any need for anchors and cables, and one of each would last a ship indefinitely. This item alone would force many a long, unwanted holiday upon the anchor-smiths and the rope-yards. This general lack of consumption would so affect the timber merchants and all who import iron, sailcloth, hemp, pitch, tar, etc., that four-fifths of what I stated—at the beginning of this reflection on sea affairs—made up a considerable branch of the trade of *Europe* would be entirely lost.

I have only touched so far on the consequences of this "blessing" in relation to shipping, but it would be detrimental to all other branches of trade as well, and destructive to the poor of every country that exports anything of its own produce or manufacture. The goods and merchandise that go to the bottom of the sea every year—which are spoiled at sea by salt water, by heat, or by vermin; destroyed by fire; or lost to the merchant by other accidents, all due to storms or long voyages, or else the neglect or greed of sailors—such goods and merchandise, I say, make up a considerable portion of what is sent abroad throughout the world every year, and must have employed great multitudes of the poor before they were ever loaded onto the ships. A hundred bales of cloth that are burnt or sunk in the *Mediterranean* are as beneficial to the poor in *England* as if they had safely arrived at *Smyrna* or *Aleppo* and every yard of them had been sold at retail in the Grand Signior’s <note>The Grand Signior was a common historical term for the Sultan of the Ottoman Empire.</note> dominions.

The merchant may go bankrupt, and with him the clothier, the dyer, the packer, and other tradesmen; the middle class may suffer, but the poor who were set to work on those goods can never lose what they have earned. Day laborers commonly receive their earnings once a week, and all the working people who were employed either in any of the various branches of the manufacturing itself, or in the several land and

<summary>The author argues that maritime losses and the "consumption" of goods are essential for the economy, as the labor required to replace lost cargo and ships provides steady employment for the poor. Without these losses, major industries like shipbuilding and textile manufacturing would shrink, harming the working class regardless of the merchant's success.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Mandeville's argument that economic prosperity relies on the destruction and replacement of goods, using the paradox of maritime disasters and the necessity of rain to illustrate his point.</meta>

<header>A Search into the</header>

The costs of water transportation required to bring goods to perfection—from the sheep's back to the vessel they were loaded into—were paid (at least for the most part) before the parcel ever came on board. Should any of my readers draw infinite conclusions from my assertions—that goods sunk or burnt are as beneficial to the poor as if they had been successfully sold and put to their proper uses—I would consider him a nitpicker and not worth answering. If it were to always rain and the sun never shine, the fruits of the earth would soon rot and be destroyed; yet it is no paradox to affirm that, to grow grass or corn, rain is as necessary as sunshine.

How this "blessing" of fair winds and fine weather would affect the mariners themselves and the supply of sailors can be easily guessed from what has already been said. Since hardly one ship in four would be used, and the vessels themselves would always be exempt from storms, fewer hands would be required to work them. Consequently, five out of six of the seamen we have could be spared; in this nation, where most jobs for the poor are already overstocked, this would be an unfortunate development. As soon as those surplus seamen were gone, it would be impossible to man such large fleets as we currently can. However, I do not see this as a detriment or even the slightest inconvenience; because the reduction in the number of mariners would be global, the only consequence would be that, in the event of war, the maritime powers would be forced to fight with fewer ships—which would be a blessing rather than an evil. And if you would carry this happiness to the highest peak of perfection, one need only add one more desirable blessing, and no nation would ever fight at all. The blessing I hint at is what all good Christians are bound to pray for: namely, that all princes and states <unclear>might</unclear> be...

<summary>Mandeville argues that maritime disasters stimulate the economy by ensuring labor is paid for even when goods are lost, comparing destruction to the necessity of rain for crops. He further claims that a world with perfect weather would lead to mass unemployment for sailors and a reduction in naval warfare.</summary>

<page-num>307</page-num>
<header>and Charity Schools.</header>

...true to their oaths and promises, and just to one another, as well as to their own subjects; that they might have a greater regard for the dictates of conscience and religion than for those of state politics and worldly wisdom, and prefer the spiritual welfare of others to their own carnal desires, and the honesty, the safety, the peace, and tranquility of the nations they govern to their own love of glory, spirit of revenge, avarice, and ambition.

The last paragraph will to many seem a digression that contributes little to my purpose; but what I mean by it is to demonstrate that goodness, integrity, and a peaceful disposition in rulers and governors of nations are not the proper qualifications to aggrandize them and increase their numbers; any more than the uninterrupted series of success that every private person would be blessed with if he could, and which I have shown would be injurious and destructive to a large society that should place happiness in worldly greatness and being envied by its neighbors, and value itself upon its honor and its strength.

No man needs to guard himself against blessings, but calamities require hands to avert them. The amiable qualities of man do not stir the species to action: his honesty, his love of company, his goodness, contentment, and frugality are so many comforts to an indolent society, and the more real and unaffected they are, the more they keep everything at rest and peace; and the more they will everywhere prevent trouble and movement itself. The same may almost be said of the gifts and munificence of heaven, and all the bounties and benefits of nature: this is certain, that the more extensive they are, and the greater plenty we have of them, the more labor we save. But the necessities, the vices, and imperfections of man, together with the various inclemencies of the weather...

<summary>Mandeville argues that the virtues of rulers, such as integrity and peacefulness, do not increase a nation's power or population, just as personal success can be destructive to a society focused on worldly greatness. He concludes that while virtues lead to a quiet, indolent peace, it is human vices and natural calamities that actually drive societal activity and labor.</summary>
<keywords>rulers, ambition, societal growth, virtue vs vice, economic labor, national greatness, indolence, Bernard Mandeville</keywords>

# *A Search into the*

and other elements contain in them the seeds of all arts, industry, and labor. It is the extremes of heat and cold, the instability and severity of seasons, the violence and uncertainty of winds, the vast power and treachery of water, the rage and untractableness of fire, and the stubbornness and sterility of the earth that strain our ingenuity on how we shall either avoid the mischiefs they may produce or correct their harmfulness and turn their various forces to our advantage a thousand different ways, while we are employed in supplying the infinite variety of our wants, which will ever be multiplied as our knowledge is enlarged and our desires increase. Hunger, thirst, and nakedness are the first tyrants that force us to stir; afterwards, our pride, sloth, sensuality, and fickleness are the great patrons that promote all arts and sciences, trades, handicrafts, and vocations, while the great taskmasters—necessity, avarice, envy, and ambition—each in the class that belongs to him, keep the members of society to their labor and make them all submit, most of them cheerfully, to the drudgery of their station, kings and princes not excepted.

The greater the variety of trades and manufactures, the more <term>operose</term> <note>laborious or involving great effort</note> they are, and the more they are divided into many branches, the greater numbers may be contained in a society without being in one another’s way, and the more easily they may be rendered a rich, powerful, and flourishing people. Few virtues employ any hands, and therefore they may render a small nation good, but they can never make a great one. To be strong and laborious, patient in difficulties, and assiduous in all business are commendable qualities; but as they do their own work, so they are their own reward, and neither art nor industry have ever paid their tribute to them; whereas the excellence of human thought and contrivance has

### Nature of Society.

has been and is yet nowhere more conspicuous than in the variety of tools and instruments of workmen and artificers, and the multiplicity of engines that were all invented either to assist the weakness of man, to correct his many imperfections, to gratify his laziness, or to address his impatience.
    In morality, as in nature, there is nothing so perfectly good in creatures that it cannot be hurtful to someone in society, nor anything so entirely evil but that it may prove beneficial to some part of the creation or another. So, things are only good and evil in reference to something else, and according to the light and position in which they are placed. What pleases us is good in that regard, and by this rule, every man wishes well for himself to the best of his capacity, with little respect for his neighbor. There never was any rain yet—even in a very dry season when public prayers had been made for it—but someone or other who wanted to go out wished it might be fair weather just for that day. When the corn stands thick in the spring, and the majority of the country rejoices at the pleasing sight, the rich farmer who kept his last year’s crop for a better market pines at the sight and inwardly grieves at the prospect of a plentiful harvest. Indeed, we often hear idle people openly wish for the possessions of others, and—to avoid appearing harmful, supposedly—add this wise proviso: that it should be without detriment to the owners. But I am afraid they often do so without any such restriction in their hearts.
    It is a stroke of luck that the prayers as well as the wishes of most people are insignificant and amount to nothing; otherwise, the only thing that could keep mankind fit for society, and the world from falling into confusion, would be the impossibility of all the petitions made to heaven being granted. A dutiful, handsome young gentleman, newly returned from his travels, stays at the *Briel* <note>The Brielle (Den Briel), a major Dutch seaport used by travelers to and from England</note> waiting with im-

# A Search into the

patience for an easterly wind to carry him over to *England*, where a dying father—who wants to embrace him and give him his blessing before he draws his last breath—lies longing after him, melted with grief and tenderness. In the meantime, a *British* minister, who is to take care of the Protestant interest in Germany, is riding post-haste to *Harwich*, in a violent hurry to arrive at *Ratisbon* before the <term>Diet</term> <note>The Imperial Diet, or assembly, of the Holy Roman Empire</note> breaks up. At the same time, a rich fleet lies ready for the *Mediterranean*, and a fine squadron is bound for the *Baltic*. All these things may probably happen at once; at least, there is no difficulty in supposing they should. If these people are not atheists, or very great reprobates, they will all have some good thoughts before they go to sleep, and consequently, around bedtime, they must all differently pray for a fair wind and a prosperous voyage. I do not say but it is their duty, and it is possible they may all be heard, but I am sure they cannot all be served at the same time.

After this, I believe I have demonstrated that neither the friendly qualities and kind affections that are natural to man, nor the real virtues he is capable of acquiring through reason and self-denial, are the foundation of society. Instead, what we call evil in this world, moral as well as natural, is the grand principle that makes us sociable creatures; it is the solid basis, the life and support of all trades and employments without exception. In that, we must look for the true origin of all arts and sciences, and the moment evil ceases, society must be ruined, if not totally dissolved.

I could add a thousand things to reinforce and further illustrate this truth with a great deal of pleasure; but for fear of being tiresome, I shall make an end. Though I confess that I have not been half as concerned with gaining the approval of others as I have studied to please myself in this amusement; yet if I ever hear that by following this diversion

# A VINDICATION of the BOOK from the Aspersions contained in a Presentment of the Grand Jury of *Middlesex*, and an abusive Letter to Lord *C.*

So that the reader may be fully instructed in the merits of the cause between my adversaries and myself, it is necessary that, before he sees my defense, he should know the whole charge and have before him all the accusations against me in full.

->*The Presentment of the Grand Jury is worded thus:*<-

WE, the Grand Jury for the County of *Middlesex*, have with the greatest sorrow and concern observed the many books and pamphlets that are published almost every week against the sacred articles of our *holy religion* and all discipline and order in the *church*; and the manner in which this is carried on seems to us to have a direct tendency to propagate *infidelity* and, consequently, the corruption of all morals.

We are justly mindful of the goodness of the Almighty, who has preserved us from the *plague* <note>Likely referring to the Great Plague of Marseille (1720–1722), which caused significant alarm in England.</note> which has visited our neighboring nation, and for which great mercy His Majesty was graciously pleased to command by his proclamation that thanks should be returned to heaven; but how provoking must it be to the Almighty that his mercies and deliverances extended to this nation,

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>The author introduces his defense against a legal presentment by the Grand Jury of Middlesex, which accuses his work of promoting infidelity and undermining religious order. The Jury highlights their concern that such writings are ungrateful to God, especially following Britain's preservation from a nearby plague.</summary>
<keywords>Vindication, Grand Jury of Middlesex, Presentment, religion, infidelity, morality, plague, legal defense</keywords>

--- Page 337 ---
( 325 )

<meta>This page continues the Presentment of the Grand Jury of Middlesex, detailing specific charges against works they believe subvert religion and morality.</meta>

...and our thanksgiving that was publicly commanded for it should be accompanied by such flagrant impieties.

We know of nothing that can be of greater service to his *Majesty* and the *Protestant Succession* (which is happily established among us for the *defense of the Christian religion*) than the suppression of *blasphemy* and *profaneness*, which has a direct tendency to subvert the very foundation on which his *Majesty’s government* is fixed.

So *restless* have these *zealots for infidelity* been in their diabolical attempts against *religion*, that they have:

*First*, openly blasphemed and denied the doctrine of the *Ever Blessed Trinity*, endeavoring by specious pretenses to revive the *Arian heresy*, which was never introduced into any nation but that the vengeance of Heaven pursued it.

*Secondly*, they affirm an *absolute fate* and deny the *providence* and *government* of the *Almighty* in the world.

*Thirdly*, they have endeavored to subvert all order and discipline of the church, and by vile and unjust reflections on the *clergy*, they strive to bring contempt on all religion; so that by the *libertinism* of their opinions, they may encourage and draw others into the immoralities of their practice.

*Fourthly*, so that a general *libertinism* may be more effectively established, the *universities* are decried, and all *instructions* of *youth* in the principles of the *Christian religion* are denounced with the greatest malice and falsehood.

*Fifthly*, the more effectively to carry on these works of darkness, studied artifices and invented pretenses have been used to run down religion and virtue as prejudicial to society and detrimental to the state; and to recommend *luxury*, *avarice*, *pride*, and all kinds of vices as being ne-

These principles, having a direct tendency toward the subversion of all religion and civil government, our duty to the *Almighty*, our love for our *country*, and regard for our *oaths* oblige us to *present*,

### *The letter I complain of is this:*

**My Lord**,
**I**t is welcome news to all the King’s loyal subjects and true friends to the established government and succession in the *illustrious House of* **HANOVER**, that your Lordship is said to be contriving some *effectual means* of securing us from the dangers with which his Majesty’s happy government seems to be threatened by *Catiline*, under the name of *Cato*; by the writer of a book entitled *The Fable of the Bees*, etc.; and by others of their *fraternity*, who are undoubtedly useful friends to the *Pretender*, and diligent for his sake in laboring to subvert and ruin our constitution under a specious pretense of defending it. Your Lordship’s wise resolution totally to suppress such impious writings, and the direction already given for having them presented immediately by some of the Grand Juries, will effectively convince the nation

A letter included in the text praises efforts to suppress these writings, characterizing their authors as secret allies of the Pretender.</summary>
<keywords>Grand Jury of Middlesex, Bernard Mandeville, The Fable of the Bees, British Journal, House of Hanover, Catiline, Cato's Letters, The Pretender, sedition, censorship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the address to a Lord regarding the Presentment of the Grand Jury of Middlesex, arguing that suppressing "irreligious" books is necessary to maintain public trust in the government and protect the Protestant succession.</meta>

that no attempts against *Christianity* will be suffered or endured here. And this conviction will at once rid people’s minds of the uneasiness which this flagitious <note>wicked or villainous</note> race of writers has endeavored to raise in them; it will therefore be a firm bulwark to the *Protestant Religion*; will effectively defeat the projects and hopes of the *Pretender* <note>James Francis Edward Stuart, the Jacobite claimant to the throne</note>; and best secure us against any change in the *Ministry*. And no faithful *Briton* could be unconcerned if the people should imagine even the slightest neglect in any single person bearing a part in the *Ministry*, or begin to grow *jealous* <note>suspicious or distrustful</note> that anything could be done which is not being done in defending their *religion* from every appearance of approaching danger.

And, my Lord, this *jealousy* might have been apt to rise if no measures had been taken to discourage and crush the open advocates of *irreligion*. It is no easy matter to get *jealousy* out of one’s mind once it has entered. *Jealousy*, my Lord! It is as furious a fiend as any of them all. I have seen a small, thin, weak woman so invigorated by a fit of *jealousy* that five grenadiers could not hold her. My Lord, go on with your just methods of keeping the people clear of this cursed *jealousy*; for among the various kinds and occasions of it, that which concerns their *religion* is the most violent, flagrant, and frantic sort of all; and accordingly has, in former reigns, produced those various mischiefs which your Lordship has faithfully determined to prevent, dutifully regarding the Royal Authority and conforming to the example of His Majesty, who has graciously given DIRECTIONS (which are well known to your Lordship) for the preserving of unity in the Church and the purity of the Christian faith. It is in vain to think that the people of *England* will ever give up their *religion*, or be very fond of any *Ministry* that will not support it, as the wisdom of this *Ministry* has done, against such audacious attacks as are made

upon it by the *Scribblers*; for *Scribbler*, your Lordship

who, under whatever plausible appearance of good

Tyranny is the ruin of human society; and there

justly conceived an utter abhorrence and dread of

in effect protesters against *all religion*.

And really, the people cannot be blamed much
for being a little unwilling to part with their religion:
For they tell you that there is a *God*; and
that *God* governs the world; and that he is accustomed
to bless or ruin a kingdom in proportion to the
degrees of *religion* or *irreligion* prevailing in it. Your
Lordship has a fine collection of books; and, which
is a finer thing still, you certainly understand
them and can find an account of any important
affair in an instant. I would therefore gladly know
whether your Lordship can show, from any *writer*—let
him be as profane as the *Scribblers* would have him—that
any one empire, kingdom, country, or province,
great or small, did not dwindle and sink,
and was confounded, once it failed to provide
studiously for the support of religion.

--- Page 341 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument to a Lord, following a discussion on the dangers posed by "Scribblers" who undermine religion under the guise of intellectual debate.</meta>

( 329 )

The *Scribblers* talk much of the *Roman Government*, and *Liberty*, and the *Spirit* of the *Ancient Romans*. But it is undeniable that their most plausible talk of these things is all *Pretense*, and *Grimace*, and an *Artifice* to serve the purposes of *Irreligion*; and consequently to make the people *uneasy* and ruin the kingdom. For if they did in *Reality* esteem, and would faithfully recommend to their countrymen, the *Sentiments* and *Principles*, the main purposes and *Practices* of the wise and prosperous *Romans*, they would, in the first place, remind us that *Ancient Rome* was as remarkable for *observing* and promoting *Natural Religion*, as *Modern Rome* has been for corrupting that which is *Revealed*. And as the *Ancient Romans* did notably recommend themselves to the favor of *Heaven* by their faithful care of *Religion*, so were they abundantly convinced, and did accordingly acknowledge with *universal consent*, that their care of *Religion* was the *great means* <note>Cicero, in his oration *De Haruspicum Responsis*, asks: "Who is so senseless as not to understand that this great empire was born, increased, and retained by the divine power of the gods?"</note> of *God’s* preserving the empire and crowning it with *Conquest* and *Success*, *Prosperity* and *Glory*. Hence it was that when their *Orators* were intent upon doing their utmost to move and persuade the people upon any *occasion*, they always reminded them of their *Religion*, if *that* could be in any way affected by the point in debate; not doubting that the people would decide in their *favor* if they could but demonstrate that the *safety* of *Religion* depended upon the *success* of their cause. And indeed, neither the *Romans* nor any other *nation* on *earth* ever allowed their *established religion* to be openly ridiculed, discredited, or opposed; and I am sure, your *Lordship*, that you would not for all the world want this thing to be done with *impunity* among *us*, which was never endured in the world.

<summary>The author argues that modern critics hypocritically use Roman history to attack religion, whereas the ancient Romans actually attributed their national success to their strict devotion and religious observance. He maintains that no successful nation has ever permitted its established religion to be publicly mocked or undermined.</summary>
<keywords>Roman Government, Ancient Rome, Natural Religion, established religion, Cicero, national prosperity, religious devotion, Scribblers</keywords>

--- Page 342 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of those who undermine religion, shifting from the historical example of the Romans to a direct condemnation of contemporary "infidelity" and the specific opposition to Charity Schools.</meta>

( 330 )

World Before. Has any man ever, since the blessed revelation of the *Gospel*, run riot against *Christianity* as some men—nay, and some few women too—have recently done? Must the *Devil* grow so rampant at this rate, and not be called *Coram Nobis*? <note>Coram Nobis: A Latin legal term meaning "before us," typically referring to appearing before a court or the King.</note> Why should he not content himself with carrying people off in the common way—the way of cursing and swearing, breaking the Sabbath, cheating, bribery, hypocrisy, drunkenness, whoring, and such things as he used to do? He should never be allowed to dominate men's mouths and writings as he does now, with loud, tremendous infidelity, blasphemy, and profaneness, enough to frighten the King’s subjects out of their wits. We have now come to a short question: *God* or the *Devil*? That is the choice; and time will show who stands with whom. This much may be said at present: those have abundantly shown their spirit of opposition to sacred things who have not only attacked the *National* profession and exercise of religion—endeavoring, with bitterness and skill, to make it odious and contemptible—but are also anxious to hinder *multitudes* of the natives of this island from having the very seeds of religion sown among them to their benefit.

Arguments are urged with the utmost vehemence against the education of poor children in the *Charity Schools*, although not one just reason has been offered against the provision made for that education. The things that have been objected against it are <unclear>not</unclear>, in fact, true; and nothing ought to be regarded by serious and wise men as a weighty or just argument if it is not a *true* one. How does <unclear>Catiline</unclear> have the confidence left to look any man in the face, after he has spent more confidence than most men’s whole stock amounts to, by saying that *this pretended charity has, in effect, destroyed all other charities that were previously given to the elderly, the sick, and the weak*?

<summary>The author condemns the unprecedented rise of public blasphemy and infidelity, arguing that critics of the national religion are actively trying to prevent the poor from receiving religious instruction. He specifically defends Charity Schools against the "false" argument that they drain resources from other forms of charitable relief.</summary>

<keywords>Christianity, infidelity, blasphemy, Charity Schools, religious education, social welfare, Catiline, public morality</keywords>

--- Page 343 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Charity Schools, directly refuting the claim that they reduce general charitable giving or lead to the corruption of the servant class.</meta>

( 331 )

It seems quite clear that if those who do not contribute to any *Charity School* have become more uncharitable toward any other cause than they were before, their lack of charity to the one is not due to their contribution to the other. And as for those who do contribute to these schools, they are so far from being more stingy in their relief of other causes than they were before, that poor widows, the aged, and the disabled clearly receive more relief from *them*—in proportion to their numbers and abilities—than from any equivalent number of men in the same financial circumstances who do not involve themselves with *Charity Schools* in any way, except to condemn and disparage them. I will meet *Catiline* at the *Grecian Coffee House* any day of the week and, by listing specific individuals in as great a number as he pleases, demonstrate the truth of what I say. 

But I do not expect him to meet me, because it is *his business* not to encourage the demonstration of truth, but to disguise it. Otherwise, he never could have allowed himself, after describing Charity Schools as *intended to raise children with reading, writing, and sober behavior so that they may be qualified to be servants*, to immediately add these words: *“A sort of idle and rioting vermin, by which the kingdom is already almost devoured, and who have become everywhere a public nuisance, etc.”* 

What? Is it because of the Charity Schools that servants have become so idle, such rioting vermin, and such a public nuisance; that female servants become prostitutes and male servants become robbers, burglars, and swindlers? (as he says they commonly do.) Is this due to the Charity Schools? Or, if it is not, how can he allow himself the liberty of representing these schools as a means of increasing this burden of mischief, which has indeed too clearly fallen upon the public? Imbibing the principles of virtue has not, usually, been thought the chief cause—

<summary>The author defends Charity Schools against claims that they foster social vice, arguing that supporters of these schools are more generally charitable than their critics. He specifically challenges an opponent, "Catiline," for blaming the schools for the perceived rise in dishonest and idle servants.</summary>
<keywords>Charity Schools, philanthropy, social class, servants, Catiline, Grecian Coffee House, poverty, education, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 344 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Charity Schools, completing the thought from the previous page regarding the alleged "mis-occasion" or "misfortune" of these institutions being blamed for social vices.</meta>

occasion for running into vice. If the early knowledge of *Truth* and of our *obligations* to it were the surest means of departing from it, nobody would doubt that the knowledge of *Truth* was instilled into *Catiline* very early and with the utmost care. It is a fine thing for him to spread a report, and to lay as much stress upon it as he does, that *there is more collected at the church doors in a day to make these poor boys and girls appear in caps and livery coats than for all the poor in a year.* Oh, rare *Catiline*! This point you will carry most successfully; for you have no witnesses against you, nor any living soul to contradict you—except for the collectors and overseers of the poor, and all other principal inhabitants of most of the parishes in *England* where any charity schools are located.

The irony of it is, my Lord, that these *scribblers* would still like to be thought of as *good moral men*. But when men make it their business to mislead and deceive their neighbors—and that in matters of *importance*—by distorting and disguising the truth through *misrepresentations* and false insinuations, if such men are not guilty of *usurpation* while they take upon themselves the character of *good moral men*, then it is not immoral for any man to be false and deceitful in cases where the *law* cannot touch him for being so, and *morality* bears no relation to *truth* and *fair dealing*. However, I shall not be very willing to meet one of these *moral men* upon *Hounslow Heath* <note>A place near London then famous for highwaymen and robberies.</note> if I should happen to ride that way without pistols. For I have a notion that those who have *no* conscience in one point do not much abound with it in another. Your Lordship, who judges accurately of *men* as well as *books*, will easily imagine (even if you had no other knowledge of the charity schools) that there must be something very *excellent* in them, because such kinds of *men* as *these* are so fervent in *opposing* them.

<summary>The author sarcastically refutes "Catiline's" claim that charity school funding exceeds general poor relief and argues that the dishonest critics of these schools lack true moral integrity.</summary>
<keywords>Charity Schools, Catiline, poor relief, morality, Hounslow Heath, religious education, social criticism</keywords>

--- Page 345 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of charity schools, transitioning from a rebuttal of moral accusations to addressing economic objections regarding the labor of children in husbandry and manufacturing.</meta>

They tell you that these schools are hindrances to *husbandry* and to *manufacturing*. As for *husbandry*, the children are not kept in the schools any longer than until they are of an age and strength to perform its main tasks, or to endure constant labor in it; and even while they are undergoing this course of education, your Lordship may depend upon it that they shall never be hindered from working in the fields or being employed in such labor as they are capable of at any time of the year when they can find such employment to support their parents and themselves. In this case, the parents in their respective regions are the proper judges of their own situations and circumstances; at the same time, they are not so fond of their children gaining a little *knowledge* rather than a little *money* that they would not find *other* employment for them instead of going to school whenever they can earn a penny by doing so. And the case is the same regarding *manufacturing*; the trustees of the charity schools and the parents of the children raised in them would be thankful to those gentlemen who make the objection if they would assist in removing it by subscribing to a fund for joining the employment of *manufacturing* to the business of learning to *read* and *write* in the charity schools. *This* would be a noble work; it is already accomplished by the supporters of some charity schools, and is aimed at and earnestly desired by all the rest—but *Rome* was not built in a day. Until this great thing can be brought about, let the masters and managers of the manufacturing businesses in various places throughout the kingdom be so charitable as to employ the poor children for a certain number of hours every day in their respective industries, while the trustees take care to fill their other hours of the day with the usual duties of the charity schools. It is an easy matter for *partisans*, and for scheming and perverted minds, to invent plausi-

Your Lordship and other persons of integrity, whose words are the faithful representatives of their meaning, would now think—if I had not given you a key to Catiline’s talk—that he had been fully convinced that the children in the charity schools are bred to be traitors.

My Lord, if any one master is allowed by the trustees to continue in any charity school against whom proof can be brought that he is disaffected to the government, or that he does not as faithfully teach the children obedience and loyalty to the King as any other duty in the catechism, then I will gratify Catiline with a license to pull down the schools and hang up the masters, according to his heart’s desire.

These and such things as these are urged with the same bitterness and as little truth in the book mentioned above, namely *The Fable of the Bees; or, Private Vices, Public Benefits, etc.* Catiline rejects the fundamental articles of *faith*, impiously comparing the doctrine of the Blessed Trinity to *Fee-fa-fum*: this profligate author of the *Fable* is not only an auxiliary to *Catiline* in opposition to *faith*, but has taken it upon himself to tear up the very foundations of *moral virtue* and establish *vice* in its place. The best physician in the world never labored more to purge the *natural* body of bad qualities than this "Bumble-Bee" has done to purge the body *politic* of good ones. If any trivial inconvenience did arise from so excellent a work, as some little inconvenience attends all human institutions and affairs, the excellence of the work would still be a matter of *joy*, and find encouragement with all the *wise* and the *good*, who despise such insignificant objections against it as other men are not ashamed to raise and defend.

I shall not trouble your Lordship with a particular vindication of the clergy, nor is there any reason that I should, for they are already secure of your *Lordship’s* good affection toward them, and they are able to vindicate themselves wherever such a vindication is needed, being as *faithful*, *virtuous*, and *learned* a body of men as any in *Europe*; and yet they suspend the *publication* of arguments in a solemn defense of themselves, because they neither *expect* nor *desire* the approval and esteem of impious and abandoned men. At the same time, they cannot doubt that all persons—not only those of great insight but those of *common sense*—now clearly see that the arrows shot against the clergy are intended to wound and destroy the *Divine Institution* of the ministerial offices, and to extirpate the religion which those sacred offices were appointed to preserve and promote.

This was always *supposed* and *suspected* by every honest and impartial man; but it is now *demonstrated* by those who before had given cause for such suspicions, for they have now openly declared that *faith* in its principal articles is not only needless but ridiculous; that the welfare of human society must sink and perish under the encouragement of *virtue*; and that immorality is the only *firm* foundation upon which the happiness of mankind can be built and survive. <meta>This page continues the author's transition from recounting the accusations against his book to presenting his formal defense, which was originally published in the London Journal in response to a grand jury presentment and an abusive letter.</meta>

it. The answer was published in the *London Journal* of *August* 10, 1723, in these words:

WHEREAS in the *Evening-Post* of *Thursday, July* 11, a presentment was inserted from the Grand Jury of *Middlesex* against the publisher of a book entitled *The Fable of the Bees; or, Private Vices, Public Benefits*; and since then, a passionate and abusive letter has been published against the same book and its author in the *London Journal* of *Saturday, July* 27; I think myself indispensably obliged to vindicate the aforementioned book against the black aspersions that have been undeservedly cast upon it, being conscious that I did not have the least ill design in composing it. Since the accusations against it have been made openly in the public papers, it is not equitable that its defense should appear in a more private manner. What I have to say in my behalf, I shall address to all people of sense and sincerity, asking no other favor of them than their patience and attention. Setting aside what in that letter relates to others, and everything that is foreign and immaterial, I shall begin with the passage that is quoted from the book, *namely: After this, I flatter myself to have demonstrated that neither the friendly qualities and kind affections that are natural to man, nor the real virtues he is capable of acquiring by reason and self-denial, are the foundation of society; but that what we call evil in this world, moral as well as natural, is the grand principle that makes us sociable creatures; the solid basis, the life and support of all trades and employments without exception: that there we must look for the true origin of all arts and sciences; and that the moment evil ceases, society must be spoiled, if not totally dissolved.* These words, I admit, are in the book, and, being both innocent and true, are likely to remain there in all future printings. But I will likewise admit very freely that, if I had written with a design to be understood by the simplest minds, I would

...not have chosen the subject treated there; or if I had, I would have amplified and explained every sentence, spoken and made distinctions with authority, and never appeared without the pointer <note>Originally "fescue," a small stick or wire used to point out letters to children learning to read.</note> in my hand. For example, to make the passage referred to intelligible, I would have devoted a page or two to the meaning of the word *Evil*; after that, I would have taught them that every defect, every lack, was an evil; that all those mutual services which the individual members of a society pay to each other depended on the variety of those needs; and that consequently, the greater the variety of needs there was, the larger the number of individuals who might find their private interest in working for the good of others and, united together, compose one body. Is there any trade or handicraft that does not supply us with something we lacked? This lack, certainly, before it was supplied, was an evil which that trade or handicraft was meant to remedy, and without which it could never have been thought of. Is there any art or science that was not invented to fix some defect? Had the latter not existed, there could have been no occasion for the former to remove it. I say, on page 308:

> *The excellence of human thought and ingenuity has been, and is still, nowhere more conspicuous than in the variety of tools and instruments of workmen and craftsmen, and the multitude of machines that were all invented either to assist the weakness of man, to correct his many imperfections, to gratify his laziness, or to satisfy his impatience.*

Several of the preceding pages continue in the same vein. But what relation does all this have to religion or infidelity, any more than it has to navigation or the peace in the North?

The many workers who are employed to supply our natural needs—those that are truly such, like hunger, thirst, and nakedness—are few in number compared to the vast numbers who are all innocently gratifying the depravity of our corrupt nature; I mean the industrious people who earn a livelihood by their

The short-sighted common people, in the chain of causes, can seldom see further than one link; but those who can enlarge their view, and will give themselves the leisure of gazing on the prospect of connected events, may in a hundred places see good spring up and sprout from evil, as naturally as chickens do from eggs.

The words are to be found on page 59, in the remark made on the seeming paradox that in the *Grumbling Hive*:

->*The worst of all the multitude*<-

Where in many instances it may be amply discovered how unsearchable Providence daily orders the comforts of the hardworking, and even the deliverances of the oppressed, to come forth secretly not only from the vices of the luxurious, but likewise the crimes of the wicked and most abandoned.

Men of candor and capacity perceive at first sight that in the passage censured, there is no meaning hidden or expressed that is not altogether contained in the following words: *Man is a needy creature on innumerable accounts, and yet from those very necessities, and nothing else, arise all trades and employments.* But it is ridiculous for men to meddle with books above their sphere.

*The Fable of the Bees* was designed for the entertainment of people of knowledge and education when they have an idle hour which they do not know how to spend better. It is a book of severe and exalted morality that contains a strict test of virtue, an infallible touchstone to distinguish the real from the counterfeit, and shows many actions to be faulty that are passed off on the world as good ones. It describes the nature and symptoms of human passions and detects their force and disguises;

and traces self-love in its darkest recesses; I might safely add, beyond any other system of ethics: The whole is a rhapsody void of order or method, but no part of it has anything in it that is sour or pedantic; the style, I confess, is very unequal, sometimes very high and rhetorical, and sometimes very low and even very trivial; such as it is, I am satisfied that it has entertained persons of great probity and virtue, and unquestionable good sense; and I am in no fear that it will ever cease to do so while it is read by such people. Whoever has seen the violent charge against this book will pardon me for saying more in praise of it than a man not suffering under the same necessity would say of his own work on any other occasion.

The praises of stews <note>brothels</note> complained of in the presentment <note>legal indictment</note> are nowhere in the book. What might have given cause for this charge must be a political dissertation concerning the best method to guard and preserve women of honor and virtue from the insults of dissolute men, whose passions are often ungovernable: As in this there is a dilemma between two evils, both of which it is impossible to avoid, I have treated it with the utmost caution and begin thus: *I am far from encouraging vice, and would think it an unspeakable happiness for a state if the sin of impurity could be utterly banished from it; but I am afraid it is impossible.* I give my reasons why I think it so; and speaking occasionally of the music-houses in Amsterdam, I give a short account of them, than which nothing can be more harmless; and I appeal to all impartial judges whether what I have said of them is not ten times more likely to give men (even the most voluptuous) a disgust and aversion toward them than it is to raise any criminal desire. I am sorry the grand jury should believe that I published this with a design to corrupt the nation, without considering that, in the first place, there is <unclear>not a</unclear>

<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s defense against the legal presentment of his book, specifically arguing that the work is not intended for a general audience and therefore cannot be responsible for corrupting public morals.</meta>

sentence nor a syllable that can either offend the chastest ear, or sully the imagination of the most vicious; or in the second place, that the matter complained of is clearly addressed to magistrates and politicians, or at least the more serious and thinking part of mankind; whereas a general corruption of manners as to lewdness, expected to be produced by reading, can only be feared from obscenities easily purchased, and every way adapted to the tastes and capacities of the heedless multitude and inexperienced youth of both sexes: but that the work, so outrageously cried out against, was never intended for either of these classes of people, is self-evident from every circumstance. The beginning of the prose is altogether philosophical, and hardly intelligible to anyone who has not been used to matters of speculation; and its running title is so far from being attractive or inviting that, without having read the book itself, nobody knows what to make of it, while at the same time the price is five shillings. From all this it is plain that if the book contains any dangerous tenets, I have not been very anxious to scatter them among the people. I have not said a word to please or engage them, and the greatest compliment I have paid them has been, *Apage vulgus* <note>Away with the common people</note>. But *as nothing (I say, p. 182) would more clearly demonstrate the falsity of my notions than that the generality of the people should agree with them, so I do not expect the approval of the multitude. I write not for many, nor seek for any well-wishers, except among the few who can think abstractly and have their minds elevated above the vulgar.* Of this I have made no ill use, and have always preserved such a tender regard for the public that when I have advanced any uncommon sentiments, I have used all the precautions imaginable so that they might not be harmful to weak minds that might casually dip into the book. --- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s defense of his work, clarifying that while he explores how vice contributes to national wealth, he still maintains that individual virtue is a duty and that investigating causes does not imply a desire to promote harm.</meta>

<page-num>344</page-num>

...could be raised into a rich and mighty kingdom, or, once raised, sustain themselves in their wealth and power for any considerable time, without the vices of man. I had premised—which was true—that I had never said or imagined that man could not be virtuous just as well in a rich and mighty kingdom as in the most wretched commonwealth. This caution, a man less scrupulous than myself might have thought unnecessary, since he had already explained himself on that point in the very same paragraph, which begins thus: *I lay down as a first principle that in all societies, great or small, it is the duty of every member of it to be good; that virtue ought to be encouraged, vice discouraged, the laws obeyed, and the transgressors punished.* There is not a line in the book that contradicts this doctrine, and I defy my enemies to disprove what I have advanced on page 182: that *if I have shown the way to worldly greatness, I have always, without hesitation, preferred the road that leads to virtue.* No man ever took more pains not to be misunderstood than myself. Note page 181: when I say that *societies cannot be raised to wealth and power, and the height of earthly glory, without vices,* I do not think that by saying so I command men to be vicious, any more than I command them to be quarrelsome or greedy when I affirm that the legal profession would not be maintained in such numbers and splendor if there were not an abundance of overly selfish and litigious people.

A caution of the same nature I had already given toward the end of the preface, regarding an obvious evil inseparable from the prosperity of London. To investigate the real causes of things implies no evil intent, nor has it any tendency to do harm. A man may write on poisons and be an excellent physician. Page 307, I say: *No man needs to guard himself against blessings, but calamities require hands to avert them.* And page 308: *It is the extremes of heat and cold, the inconsistency and badness of seasons, the violence and uncertainty of winds, the vast power and treachery of water, the rage and intractable nature of fire, and the stubbornness and sterility of the earth, that strain and* invention.

<summary>Mandeville defends his work by arguing that describing the role of vice in national prosperity is not an endorsement of immorality, citing his stated principle that individual virtue and the rule of law are essential duties. He compares his analysis to a physician studying poisons or a lawyer acknowledging that his profession relies on the existence of litigious people.</summary>

--- Page 357 ---
<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s defense of his work, specifically addressing the role of human ingenuity in managing the "mischiefs" of society and clarifying his original intentions in writing *The Fable of the Bees*.</meta>

<page-num>345</page-num>

**Invention**, how we shall either avoid the mischiefs they produce, or correct their harmfulness, and turn their various forces to our own advantage in a thousand different ways. While a man is inquiring into the occupations of vast multitudes, I cannot see why he may not say all this and much more without being accused of depreciating or speaking slightingly of the gifts and generosity of Heaven; when at the same time he demonstrates that without rain and sunshine, this globe would not be habitable for creatures like ourselves. It is an unusual subject, and I would never quarrel with the man who might tell me that it might as well have been left alone; yet I always thought it would please people of any reasonable taste and not be easily forgotten.

I could never conquer my vanity as well as I could wish, and I am too proud to commit crimes. As for the main scope and intent of the book—the purpose for which it was written—I swear with the utmost sincerity that it was exactly what I declared in the preface. At the bottom of the fourth page, you will find these words: *If you ask me why I have done all this, <term>cui bono</term>? <note>to what end or for whose benefit?</note> and what good these ideas will produce? Truly, besides the reader’s entertainment, I believe none at all. But if I were asked what naturally ought to be expected from them, I would answer that, in the first place, people who continually find fault with others would, by reading them, be taught to look at home; and by examining their own consciences, they would be made ashamed of always complaining about things of which they are more or less guilty themselves. Secondly, those who are so fond of the ease and comforts of a great and flourishing nation would learn to submit more patiently to those inconveniences which no government on earth can remedy, once they see the impossibility of enjoying a great share of the former without also partaking in the latter.*

The first edition of the *Fable of the Bees*, which came out in 1714, was never criticized or publicly noticed; and the only reason I can think of 

<summary>Mandeville defends his analytical approach to society as compatible with religious devotion and explains that his book's goal was to highlight human hypocrisy and the inevitable trade-offs of national prosperity. He notes that the original 1714 edition of his work initially attracted no controversy.</summary>

<keywords>Invention, Bernard Mandeville, The Fable of the Bees, 1714 edition, cui bono, social utility, hypocrisy, national prosperity, government</keywords>

--- Page 358 ---
<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s defense against critics, specifically addressing the controversy surrounding the second edition and the addition of his essay on charity schools.</meta>

<page-num>346</page-num>

Why the second edition should be so unmercifully treated, though it contains many precautions which the first edition lacked, is due to "An Essay on Charity and Charity-Schools," which was added to what was printed before. I confess that it is my opinion that all hard and dirty work in a well-governed nation ought to be the lot and portion of the poor, and that to divert their children from useful labor until they are fourteen or fifteen years old is the wrong method to qualify them for it when they are grown up. I have given several reasons for my opinion in that essay, to which I refer all impartial and understanding men, assuring them that they will not find such monstrous impiety in it as is reported. The extent to which I have been an advocate for libertinism and immorality, or an enemy to the instruction of youth in the Christian faith, can be gathered from the effort I have put into the topic of education for over seven pages together; and afterwards again, on page 153, where I speak of the instruction that the children of the poor might receive at church. Regarding this—or some other place of worship—I say that I would not have even the lowliest person in a parish who is able to walk there be absent on Sundays. I wrote these words:

> It is the Sabbath, the most useful day in seven, that is set apart for divine service and religious exercise, as well as for rest from physical labor; and it is a duty incumbent upon all magistrates to take particular care of that day. The poor especially, and their children, should be made to go to church on it, both in the morning and the afternoon, because they have no time on any other day. By instruction and example, they ought to be encouraged in this from their very infancy. The willful neglect of it ought to be considered scandalous; and if direct compulsion to do what I urge might seem too harsh and perhaps impractical, then all diversions at least ought to be strictly prohibited, and the poor prevented from every amusement out in public that might allure or draw them away from it.

If the arguments I have used are not convincing, I desire that they be refuted, and I will acknowledge it as a favor.

<summary>Mandeville defends his "Essay on Charity and Charity-Schools," arguing that manual labor is the proper role for the poor and that long-term schooling makes their children unfit for it. He counters accusations of impiety by highlighting his support for mandatory religious attendance and the sanctification of the Sabbath for the lower classes.</summary>
<keywords>Charity-Schools, the poor, manual labor, education, Christian faith, Sabbath, divine service, magistrates, social order, Bernard Mandeville</keywords>

--- Page 359 ---
<meta>This page continues Mandeville’s defense of his work, specifically addressing the backlash from his "Essay on Charity and Charity-Schools" and the misinterpretation of his central paradox.</meta>

<page-num>( 347 )</page-num>

...anyone who convinces me of my errors, without using abusive language, by showing me where I have been mistaken. But slander, it seems, is the shortest way to refute an adversary when men are touched in a sensitive spot. Vast sums are gathered for these charity schools, and I understand human nature too well to imagine that those who share in that money would hear them spoken against with any patience. Therefore, I foresaw the treatment I would receive; and having repeated the common slogans used in favor of charity schools, I told my readers, on *page 285, that it is the general opinion that he who speaks even the slightest word against them is an uncharitable, hard-hearted, and inhuman—if not a wicked, profane, and atheistical—wretch.* For this reason, it cannot be thought a great surprise to me when, in that extraordinary letter to Lord *C.*, I saw myself called a *"profligate author,"* and the publication of my principles described as *"an open and avowed proposal to extirpate the Christian faith and all virtue."* What I had done was called *"so stunning, shocking, so frightful, so flagrant an enormity"* that it cried out for the vengeance of heaven. This is nothing more than what I have always expected from the enemies of truth and fair dealing, and I shall say nothing in retaliation against the angry author of that letter, who tries to expose me to public fury. I pity him, and have enough charity to believe that he has been deceived himself by trusting to reputation and the hearsay of others; for no man in his right mind can imagine that he could have read even a quarter of my book and still write as he does.

I am sorry if the words *Private Vices, Public Benefits* have ever given any offense to a well-meaning man. Their mystery is soon revealed once they are rightly understood; but no sincere man who has read the last paragraph will question their innocence. In that paragraph, I take my leave of the reader and conclude by repeating the seeming paradox, the substance of which is ad-

<summary>Mandeville argues that his critics use slander rather than reason because his critique of charity schools threatens their financial interests. He defends his famous paradox, "Private Vices, Public Benefits," claiming it is misunderstood and innocent when read in context.</summary>

<keywords>Bernard Mandeville, Charity-Schools, Calumny, Private Vices, Public Benefits, Paradox, Lord C., Vengeance of Heaven, Slander</keywords>

--- Page 360 ---
<meta>This page concludes Mandeville’s "Vindication," where he clarifies his central thesis and offers a final challenge to his critics regarding the charges of immorality brought against *The Fable of the Bees*.</meta>

<page-num>(348)</page-num>

advanced on the Title Page: that **Private Vices**, through the dexterous management of a skillful politician, may be turned into **Public Benefits**. These are the last words of the book, printed in the same large typeface as the rest. But I will set aside everything I have said in my **Vindication**; and if in the entire book called *The Fable of the Bees*—which was presented by the Grand Jury of *Middlesex* to the judges of the *King’s Bench*—there can be found the slightest trace of blasphemy or profaneness, or anything tending toward immorality or the corruption of manners, I request that it be made public. And if this is done without insults, personal attacks, or inciting the mob against me—things I never intend to answer—I will not only recant, but also beg pardon from the offended public in the most solemn manner; and (if the hangman is considered too good for the task) I will burn the book myself at any reasonable time and place my adversaries might choose to appoint.

->The Author of the *Fable of the Bees*<-
